    Chapter 1: hot shot from vegas

    Carol bangs her head softly against the head-rest of her car. Her hands are gripped tightly around the steering wheel going nowhere. The day has barely started but the unrelenting heat from the Albuquerque sun still manages to scorch through the windshield, dotting her neck and forehead in a thin sheen of sweat.

    Her car, tucked away in the corner of the office parking lot, idles quietly as the cool air from the air conditioning struggles to do it’s job. The call for a Jane Doe found on the Rio Grande River Trail had just come through her radio. Carol, in her pessimistic nature, is already preparing for the worst. Not just about what might have happened with Jane Doe, but with how this will affect the department.

    The department has been inundated with case upon case, crime upon crime, for months now. At every corner there is paperwork to be filed, evidence to be processed, and simply put: it’s a fucking shit show. And it doesn’t help that they are short on staff because Val just had to get pregnant and go on maternity leave, leaving Carol and many others, but especially Carol, working overtime, every waking moment devoted to the job.

    Her detective badge, pinned to the inside of her blazer thrown haphazardly onto the passenger seat glints almost too bright in the sunlight that it might actually start a fire from the reflection it casts on the roof liner.

    Carol sits up, flips the sun visor down, and takes a moment to ready herself in the mirror careful not to wrinkle the photo of Helen. She dabs away the perspiration that dots her forehead with a crumpled up Los Pollos Hermanos napkin that she pulls out of her glove compartment and applies a thin layer of chapstick that tingles her lips with mint. Then, a quick zhuzh of her hair, and a silent but all important “you got this,” whispered to herself and she’s out the door, blazer and book bag in one hand, coffee in the other. The rubber soles of her shoes practically squelch against the sizzling pavement.

    At least the office building is cool, the frigid blast of the AC most welcomed on her skin. Kenny, the only security guard that Carol actually likes, old and frail as he may be, waves her through the metal detector and Manousos, as always, is on the other side waiting for her.

    “So did you hear?” Manousos asks as they walk towards the elevator. The lobby has life this morning, loud chatter, unfamiliar faces. Typical activity when big cases come through, especially when a dead body is involved.

    “About Jane Doe?”

    “Well yes, but no. They are bringing in a replacement for Val. Big guns, apparently.”

    Carol huffs, shakes her head. She knew this day would come but she can’t say that it still doesn’t upset her. There is no one bigger than Val. Her grip tightens around the handle of her travel mug, ironically enough, the one Val had gifted her for her birthday just before she left. Bright yellow and covered in a floral pattern—so not Carol, but that was just the nature of Val, to buy someone something that she would want for herself.

    Ultimately, Carol knows they need the support but how could anyone ever replace Val? The glue that held this entire department together; one of the very few women in her position and she was damn fucking good at it too.

    Carol jams her thumb into the “up” button and groans, letting her head fall to Manousos’ shoulder for just a moment. It must be a man, she thinks, some questionably-qualified, glorified ex-cop. It makes Carol sick.

    “Do you know who?”

    “Some hot shot from Vegas. Detective of the Year three times in a row. Solved that big murder and drug ring case that happened at the MGM Grand. Guess you’ll have some competition.”

    Carol rolls her eyes. She’s been Detective of the Year four times, albeit not consecutively, since they started handing out this silly award six years ago. She remembers the case Manousos is talking about, however. It had been all over the news and the gossip between all the jurisdictions had been more than Carol could handle. There had been too much testosterone involved; a plethora of male egos just combating one another.

    But it begs the question to Carol, why would someone from one of the busiest metropolitan crime areas in the Southwestern United States want to come to Albuquerque, New Mexico of all places? Punishment? Demotion? All the team needs is a man with a chip on his shoulder and a point to prove.

    “As if we need any more male bravado consuming this place.”

    Manousos simply shrugs. “It’s a woman.”

    Oh. A relief, sort of.

    It’s just that Carol Sturka does not like change.

    She remembers how difficult it had been adjusting when the Chief appointed Manousos as her partner after her first partner, Steven, was forced to retire due to a severe back injury sustained in the field.

    They had argued and bickered all the time. Manousos would purposely only speak to her in Spanish when she was getting on his nerves or being stubborn; and Carol would fire back the assorted profanities she knew—chinga tu madré, cabrón being her go-to, but all it would do was ease the tension, eliciting reluctant giggles from two very grown adults with very serious jobs. But in Carol’s defence, she had been rife with grief after Helen had passed and Manousos was getting the brunt of it. Now, some seven odd years later, Manousos is one of the only people who makes this job bearable. And Carol would never admit it, but Manousos is one of her best friends.

    After what feels like an eternity, the elevator doors slide open. A few fleeting moments of calm before the doors reopen on the tenth floor to what Carol can only assume will be chaos.

    “It’s only temporary…right?” The elevator dings with each floor it ascends. Val is one of the only other few people who kept her sane at this job. “I mean, she is coming back. She’s gotta come back,” she says, concern edging into her voice.

    All Manousos can do is offer her a gentle squeeze on her shoulder.

    “No sé. We will find out soon. Chief is making an announcement later. I heard from Rick she’s being flown in this afternoon.”

    Carol sighs and the elevator doors open on the tenth floor to Chief Schrader, who is standing there, hands out, palms up, and with a gimmicky smile on his face.

    “Ah, there they are, my two favourite detectives.”

    It’s hardly 9:30am and this breath is already riddled with the nauseating scent of the cheap cigars he smokes. There are crumbs on his tie and his belly threatens to protrude through the buttons on his way-too-thin and tight white shirt.

    It smells even worse in his office. A mixture of fresh and day old coffee, and onions from whatever breakfast sandwich he had been eating. He briefs them on Jane Doe—Koumba and Laxmi are already on the scene processing the evidence—and reminds them of the meeting after lunch where they will be introduced to their newest team member.

    Suddenly Carol wishes her coffee mug were filled with vodka instead.

     

    +

     

    There are more unfamiliar faces in the lobby when Carol returns from lunch. She flashes her badge quickly to Kenny who waves her through. A man, about three times her sizes huffs by her in a blind fury, nearly knocking the coffee out of her hand.

    ‘Watch it, dickhead,’ she thinks to herself.

    Journalists and news crews gather in small groups. A cacophony of bodies and voices sailing throughout the room. Everyone is always eager to be first on the scene when big news breaks. However, through the flock of bodies and cameras, one woman in particular stands out to Carol. Tall and slender with legs that go on for days. Dark brown wavy hair that falls just to her shoulders. Her grey pinstripe pant suit perfectly fits her and her heels have her towering over everyone. She’s got these magnificently high cheekbones and Carol can’t remember the last time she’s seen someone so attractive here.

    She has half a mind to entertain the idea that that is Val’s replacement but she doesn’t exactly scream “hot shot from Vegas” to Carol. An attorney perhaps. She’s looking at her phone but when she looks up, her brows are furrowed as if she’s lost.

    On Carol’s best day, she has game. Lots of it; this natural ability hidden away behind all the sarcasm and dry wit to charm most anybody, especially beautiful women. That is after all how she met Helen. And consequently all the other woman she had hooked up with when she felt she might be ready to put herself back out there. She wasn’t though and sometimes she feels like she might never be.

    She has nothing to lose, Carol figures, so she approaches the woman coolly, one hand in her pocket, the other holding her coffee. If nothing more, she can tell herself she made a beautiful woman smile today.

    Sidled next to her, Carol has to crane her head all the way up just to even look at her. She’s still for a moment, allowing the woman to feel her presence first, then with just the right amount of self-assuredness that can’t be taught, she says,

    “Can I help you find where you might be going? It can be a bit of a maze in here.”

    Carol awaits a response but the woman hardly bats at eye at her, just a quick sideways glance with one of the most forced smiles that she’s ever seen.

    “No thanks, I know where I’m going,” the woman says flatly, her eyes cast back down at her phone, and then walks off.

    It leaves Carol in shock, mouth halfway agape. She can deal with the rejection but the attitude was seemingly uncalled for. Maybe she does give off hot shot energy and something tells her in that moment that she just had her first encounter with Val’s replacement.

    Fuck.

    When she turns around, Manousos is laughing at her.

    “Not a word,” she grumbles and Manousos pulls a fake zipper across his mouth.

     

    +

     

    It comes as absolutely no surprise to Carol when twenty minutes later, standing front and centre of the briefing room next to the Chief is that same woman. Tall and beautiful as she may be, now suddenly soured by their encounter downstairs. Only this time she has some disingenuous smile plastered across her face.

    With every team member in attendance today, Carol and Manousos slink to the back of the room, half shielded by the water cooler. Carol tugs uncomfortably at her collar and exchanges a knowing glance with Manousos. The woman is laughing boisterously with the Chief, one hand on his arm, like she’s putting on a show.

    Okay, so she definitely does give off hot shot vibes.

    “Manny, is this a sick joke?” Carol whispers to Manousos.

    “Let’s give her the benefit of the doubt. Maybe you caught her at a bad time.” His words are hollow though and Carol half appreciates his optimism.

    “Yeah,” Carol scoffs. “We’ll see.”

    Some minutes pass before the Chief finally introduces her to the room.

    Lieutenant Zosia Nowak.

    And she has indeed been Las Vegas’ Detective of the Year for the third year running. The Chief blabbers on for what feels like forever, boasting about every single asset she has as if Carol Sturka does not exist. And if Carol is not careful, she might end up rolling her eyes so hard that they get stuck in the back of her head.

    Suddenly, the water cooler lets out a loud gurgle and Carol has nowhere to hide. Half the heads in the room swivel around and for not more than one second, she might make direct eye contact with Lieutenant Nowak. Quickly as it happens, the Chief starts talking again and Carol wonders if she even recognizes her from earlier.

    Then after a few words from the new Lieutenant herself about how she’s so excited to be joining the team and doing everything she can to make everyone’s lives easier, to which again Carol has to fight the urge to not roll her eyes, the meeting ends with a flourish. Applause for their newest team member erupts and just like that, the room begins to clear out.

    “Carol. Manny.” The Chief beckons them over with a quick motion of his hand.

    Carol can only imagine what he’s going to say. Lieutenant Nowak is still standing next to him, her hands clasped together as if she were waiting for them.

    “I want to personally introduce you to Lieutenant Zosia Nowak—“

    “Zosia is just fine,” she interrupts with that same disingenuous smile.

    “Right. Zosia,” he says. “This is Carol Sturka and Manousos Oviedo. Two of the very finest and most experienced.”

    Manousos extends his hand first, sensing Carol’s hesitation.

    “You can call me Manny,” he offers.

    “Nice to meet you, Manny,” Zosia says, but her eyes never really meet his.

    He then flashes Carol a quick look, as if to say be nice. So Carol extends her hand to Zosia and her handshake is firmer than she would have expected. Warmer too. Their gazes settle on each other, this time just a beat longer than the last and Carol is still trying to figure out if the Lieutenant remembers copping an attitude with her downstairs.

    “Welcome to the force,” Carol smiles, but only Manousos would know it’s a wince. “I’m sure you will fit right in.”

    “Carol Sturka,” Zosia says with a tone of familiarity that takes Carol aback. Their hands are still firmly connected. “We finally meet.”

    Carol quirks one eyebrow up and the warmth of Zosia’s hand lingers on her own as their hands separate. She cannot possibly fathom what Zosia means by that.

    “Finally?”

    “I’ve heard about you. Your reputation crosses state lines.”

    Oh.

    “Is that so…”

    “Your work on the Santa Fe Serial Killer case was quite impressive.” It starts out genuine, like a compliment, but there’s an empty look behind Zosia’s eyes that makes Carol wonder if she’s being mocked.

    “See? I told ya,” the Chief interjects. “Two of the finest.”

    But maybe Manousos is right. After all, Carol of all people knows what it’s like to be caught at the wrong moment and say the wrong thing or do the wrong thing. She still does it to this day. However, there is an insincerity to the Lieutenant’s whole demeanour that she can’t quite shake.

    Before another word can be said, the Chief’s phone chimes loudly in his pocket. Zosia has quickly looked away, her eyes trained on something in the distance, and Carol takes that brief moment to look at her. She really is attractive—very much Carol’s type even though she bears no resemblance to Helen—and something about that perturbs her. Then she wonders what might Helen think about her. Would she be able to see through whatever this phony egotistical act may be? Who the fuck is Carol kidding—of course Helen would see through it; she saw through all of Carol’s bullshit for better or for worse—and still loved her.

    Carol swallows back the feeling.

    “Listen,” the Chief says after looking at his phone, reeling everyone back in. “I’m sure you all will get acquainted with each other soon. You’ll be spending quite a bit of time together as you know. But right now, Zosia, we’re needed in HR to finalize your transfer.”

    Manousos tries to offer a parting word, a polite formality as if to say, ‘we can’t wait to start working with you!’ but Zosia simply turns on her heels and strides away with the Chief without so much as a goodbye.

    Carol, still stuck on the fact that Zosia already knew who she was, simply looks to Manousos who very clearly shares the same sentiment.

    “Pendeja,” they whisper quietly in unison.

     

    +

     

    The bullpen is always the rowdiest just before the day shift ends with everyone congregating and deciding on which bar to go to. But not only is it a shift change, with the night crew slowing peppering in, but it is usually when there is a spike in calls and reports.

    Carol hangs around for a bit. Manousos is with her even though she knows he is dying to get home to his husband but they need the Jane Doe report from Laxmi and they are waiting to see if Chief Schrader wants to debrief with them for anything else.

    Carol perches herself up against a desk and is deep in conversation with Koumba. It’s the first time all day she’s been able to take a breath. They are conversing about nothing in particular when Koumba suddenly stands upright and nods past Carol’s shoulder, like a nervous school boy who was just caught doing something he shouldn’t be. Carol and Manousos turn around in unison. Zosia seems to have appeared out of nowhere and is hovering right behind Carol. She’s holding a stack of papers that she recognizes, so Carol, in the good mood she’s finally in, tries to be the bigger person and offer Zosia some guidance.

    “If you’re looking for the copy room, it’s the—“

    “—the room that says copy room?” Zosia doesn’t even look at Carol. “Yup, got it, thanks.”

    This time Carol scoffs loudly. She doesn’t even care if Zosia may or may not have heard.

    “Is she fucking for real?” she says to Koumba and Manousos.

    “She won’t survive here with that attitude,” Koumba says.

    “She won’t survive you,” Manousos adds.

    Carol balls her fists tightly in her pockets. She can’t understand where all this hostility is coming from, but she also doesn’t have the mental capacity in this moment to dwell on it. That will be a problem for tomorrow.

    “See if I ever try and help her again,” is all she says.

     

    +

     

    Carol drives home in silence. The moon is bright and round tonight, glinting off the street signs and passing cars. Normally the radio would be blaring or a podcast playing, but tonight Carol welcomes the quiet. A moment to collect her thoughts and decompress.

    She thinks about Jane Doe, who has now been identified as a twenty-three year old woman named Zoe Diaz. She thinks about what her final moments might have been like; was she alone? Did she suffer? There had been no visible injuries or foul play. How must her family must be feeling? Does she have siblings? A best friend? Her thoughts are a vicious cycle.

    She thinks about how she could not have gotten through the day without Manousos.

    She thinks about Helen; and how on a night like tonight, she would have been waiting out on the back patio with dinner and wine for two.

    And then she thinks about Lieutenant Zosia fucking Nowak. Rude as the day is long, but fuckable nonetheless. It makes her feel insane.

    The cap is quick to come off the vodka bottle once Carol is in her living room. No occasion for the lowball glass, a mug will do. She doesn’t bother to make herself dinner, she’ll eat later if she’s hungry. Right now she’d just like to turn her brain off and think about nothing so she pours herself a drink. The couch cushions engulf her as she sinks into the softness, feet up on the coffee table, and flicks on the TV. An episode of The Golden Girls plays, and two drinks and a handful of Doritos later, Carol is fast asleep.

     

    +++++

     

    The fluorescent lights in the break room flicker and buzz incessantly as Carol impatiently listens to the coffee percolating through the machine. It drips so monotonously that it almost puts her back to sleep. She had forgotten to take her travel mug home last night so is forced to settle for the extremely mediocre and usually burnt office coffee. She leans against the counter, ankles crossed, and repeatedly clicks the pen in her hand while the staticky radio plays hits from the 80s.

    Through the windows, the skies are uncharacteristically overcast and the clouds look heavy with rain, although it hasn’t rained in months, and it probably won’t anytime soon.

    Carol is early today for no other reason than she woke up before her alarm and felt too anxious to sit at home. She’s due at the Medical Examiners office soon anyways to review the autopsy results of Zoe Diaz. She taps her fingers against the counter willing the coffee to come out faster.

    Her blazer feels extra starchy this morning, all the threads scratching her in all the wrong places. She stretches awkwardly, throwing her arms in different directions hoping the fabric will settle but it doesn’t.

    Finally, the coffee is ready. She sits, slowly grimacing her way through each sip as she goes through the notes Koumba and Laxmi had given her.

    Then, before she can even really start, she hears a very precise click-clack of high heels against the linoleum floor and she would bet, without a shadow of a doubt, that it is Zosia.

    She’s right.

    Zosia enters the room with some grand bravado, as if she doesn’t even see Carol sitting right there. Her hair is pinned back into a low bun today and her blouse is cut just low enough that it might draw some attention, but not enough to be deemed unprofessional. Carol waits to see if Zosia will acknowledge her first.

    “Sturka,” she finally says and Carol becomes annoyed. She hates being called by her last name.

    “Morning,” Carol manages. She pauses; thinks. “Are you settling in okay?”

    “It’s fine.”

    ‘It’s fine,’ Carol mocks silently to herself while making a face.

    Zosia says nothing more. Instead she rummages through the cabinets for what Carol assumes might be a coffee mug.

    “Where the fuck are the…” Zosia’s voice trails off, lost to the sound of the radio.

    “Mugs?”

    Oh, now she needs help. In any other situation, Carol would have retorted back with something rude and snarky but she thinks twice because this is someone she’ll have to work with for the foreseeable future so she bites her tongue. If her coffee wasn’t already bitter, it is now.

    Zosia glances back expectantly and Carol simply points to the corner cabinet without looking up from her notes. Then, as if she can read Zosia’s mind, she points to the cabinet on the opposite side and then to the fridge.

    “Sugar. Milk.” Carol says.

    Carol watches Zosia open the fridge. She’s too tall, has to bend down ever so slightly to see inside and Carol can’t help but stare at her ass.

    “There’s like three kinds of oat milk in here.”

    “What’s wrong with oat milk?”

    “It’s not real milk.”

    “Then don’t have milk.”

    Zosia sighs and shuts the fridge. She rips open four sugar packets at once, spilling most of it on the counter and then empties them into her coffee. Carol is almost impressed by it.

    Then an awkward silence fills the break room. Just the sound of the radio and Zosia’s metal spoon clinking around the ceramic mug. Carol looks back to her notes, making scribbles in the margin. Maybe if it looks like she’s working, Zosia will leave her alone.

    Zosia breaks the silence first.

    “I’ll need you and Manny to get me up to speed on the Jane Doe case when he gets in. And I want the autopsy results on my desk as soon as you have them.”

    “She has a name now. Zoe Diaz.”

    “Right, well, come find me later so we can discuss Ms. Diaz.”

    Carol nods, giving her a two finger salute.

    “Yes, Lieutenant.” It comes out more sarcastic than she intends and she halfway regrets it. If Zosia notices, she doesn’t let on, already distracted by some message on her phone.

    “See you later, Sturka,” Zosia says, all the while her eyes never leave her phone, even as she walks out the room.

    Carol groans and leans back in her chair. She tosses her pen to the table with so much force that it bounces twice before rolling onto the floor. She puts her hands around the back of her neck, squeezing tightly. She wonders what fucked up karmic punishment this is.

    “Thanks a lot, Val,” she says to the ceiling.

    The ME reports that Zoe Diaz had died by strangulation. There had been excessive amounts of methamphetamine in her system, and upon a thorough background check run by Carol and Manousos, her boyfriend, Jason Chan, had been heavily involved in illegal drug activity with a man named Ignacio Varga.

    “Nice work,” the Lieutenant offers, and it almost sounds genuine. “But there’s still a lot more work to do.”

    Carol and Manousos count it as a win.

    +

     

    Her office is not her home, although Carol spends more time here than her actual bedroom or living room. She absently stares at the screen of her laptop. There’s nothing on it, just a blank excel worksheet with cell A1 blinking at her. She can’t even remember why she had opened it.

    It’s humid in here this afternoon with her arms sticking to the leather armrests of her chair. She makes a mental note to tell Rick to get someone in here to fix the air conditioning.

    Just as she remembers why she had opened excel, there are two firm knocks on the door and it swings open before she even has any time to acknowledge it.

    “Sturka.” Zosia invites herself in and Carol is caught somewhere in between being irritated and trying to remain professional.

    Zosia’s eyes do a quick scan of the office and then settle onto Carol. It’s only the second time they have held prolonged eye contact and Carol feels all kinds of conflicted when she can’t look away.

    “Lieutenant.” Carol forces herself to smile.

    “Please, just Zosia.”

    Carol nods. Nevermind, she is irritated. If only she could get the same respect from Zosia.

    Zosia enters further and stands directly in front of Carol’s desk. She’s holding a thick file folder in her hand.

    “So to what do I owe the pleasure?” Carol’s tone is heavy with sarcasm and she isn’t hiding it this time. She watches Zosia’s eyes widen ever so slightly, like she’s been challenged. Has she overstepped?

    “Chief told me to give these to you.” Zosia plops the file folder to the desk and it’s so heavy it makes a thud, the wind it kicks up blowing a tuft of Carol’s hair up. “It’s the rest of the paperwork for Zoe Diaz. He needs it processed by the end of the day.”

    This time it’s Carol’s eyes that widen. She looks at the clock—it’s just after 4pm—and then back to Zosia who has the look and body language of someone who has zero intentions of helping her.

    “This is like a hundred pages. I’ll be here all night.”

    “Better get started then.”

    Carol’s knee begins to bounce erratically under her desk. Val would have never done this to her. Val would have stayed with her and they would have done it together. They would have ordered pizza or Chinese food and stayed up until 3am if they had to.

    Carol lets out a long breath through her nose. At least she has no one waiting for her at home; but does Zosia? A human, or a pet? Right now Carol can’t imagine someone like her having the emotional capacity to care for a completely dependant animal. She’s then expecting Zosia to leave without a word as she usually does, but this time she lingers. She takes a seat in the chair across from Carol and regards her for a moment. Carol’s knee stops bouncing.

    Do not look at her tits.

    “Was there anything else you wanted to discuss?” Carol asks when Zosia’s gaze becomes too heavy.

    Zosia shakes her head and picks up the yellow travel mug on Carol’s desk and reads the words inscribed on it in some Hallmark-y cursive font.

    “Don’t cry because it’s over, smile because it happened.” The corner of Zosia’s mouth quirks up.

    It’s the first earnest expression Carol sees from her, as arrogant as it may be, and Carol still finds herself irritated. She takes the mug out of Zosia’s hand, hoping that Zosia won’t ask for a backstory.

    “It was a gift.”

    Zosia’s eyes continue to wander and she becomes seemingly fixated on a framed photo on Carol’s desk. She reaches forward with minimal effort to pick it up and Carol lunges forward to stop her but Zosia is too quick.

    “Do you mind?”

    “This is cute,” Zosia says in a tone similar to the way the girls in high school used to tell Carol her outfit was cute even though she knew they were just teasing her. “Who is this?”

    Carol grows more annoyed by the second. Her ears feel hot and red and she wishes the ground would open up and swallow her whole. This is exactly what she did not want to get into. Unfortunately, she knows from experience that not answering the question will only prompt more questions and Zosia is already looking at her with that same expectant look from this morning.

    “My…wife.”

    There is the slightest, most discernible change in Zosia’s expression that Carol might have missed if she hadn’t already been looking at her.

    “I didn’t know you were married.”

    “Why would you? And I’m not anymore.”

    “She couldn’t handle the woman you were and left you?”

    Fucking bold, Carol thinks. She’ll need a stiff drink later.

    “That’s one way to put it.” Carol folds her arms and leans back in her chair. She’s about to play her favourite game of guessing what reaction she might get. “She died.”

    It’s the first time since they’ve met that Carol sees a flash of humanity behind Zosia’s eyes. An all too familiar pang of sadness as if her own grief were being mirrored back at her and Carol idly wonders who Zosia might have lost. Maybe it’s all starting to make sense.

    “Oh, fuck. I’m sorry.”

    “It was a while ago.”

    “Doesn’t make it any less shitty, Sturka.”

    “You do know that my first name is Carol, right? Like that is an option you can call me. Preferred, actually.”

    Zosia remains quiet. She sets the picture frame back on the desk and stands. Carol thinks she’s finally going to leave but instead she leans up against the door frame coolly, arms crossed. She eyes Carol up and down, almost as if she were sizing her up. (Carol tells herself she’s checking her out), then she blatantly stares at the four Detective of the Year awards that hang on the wall.

    “So what does one have to do around here to get one of those?” It’s a joke, surely, Carol thinks.

    She scoffs. “You should know, shouldn’t you?”

    “Aside from Ms. Diaz, what crimes exactly are we solving here? Abuela’s jewellery store being robbed? Teenagers stealing booze?”

    Carol actually wants to laugh at her and now she’s really wondering how someone of her calibre ended up here.

    “Someone as smart as you should know that Albuquerque is one of the most populous states in the US. Crime is rampant here. Have you heard of something called the Cartel?”

    “Ha. Is this department even equipped to be dealing with the likes of the Cartel?”

    Finally, a smirk threatens at Carol’s lips. An organic moment presents itself for her to boast about herself in front of her Lieutenant. All Carol does is point up to the four awards on the wall, smug as ever, and Zosia actually offers her an approving nod.

    Actually, that’s a lie. She does love Sprouts, but just not when she’s forced to be here because the only items in her fridge are various condiments, half a dozen eggs, and her leftover Chinese takeout from two nights ago. There’s not even any oat milk.

    Her stomach grumbles at the scent of the freshly baked bread wafting from the bakery section and her ability to focus on the task at hand becomes overwhelmed by her immediate desire to inhale an entire sourdough loaf. She should have stopped for breakfast.

    No less, Carol pushes on, her cart laboredly squeaking through each section. Every damn time she gets the one with the broken wheel. She stops at the potatoes, surveying her options as her hunger turns into hanger. Why are there so many different types? What the fuck is the difference between a yam and a sweet potato? Is a yam even a potato? She grabs the closest ones—fingerling potatoes, whatever those are—and tosses them into the cart.

    She continues to weave through the aisles until an old lady, someone’s great-great-grandmother perhaps, unceremoniously boxes her into the fruit aisle with her cart. Carol groans under her breath and envisions herself ramming her cart forwards, bumper cars style, no mercy. Instead, she takes the more rational approach and decides, hey, maybe I do need kiwis.

    Then, as she’s arguing with herself about whether she should get bananas instead (as if she can’t just get both), she happens to glance across the way and notices a familiar figure.

    A distinct tall head and shoulders hovering around the stand of tomatoes and avocados. Zosia? Has Carol masturbated to her so many times that she’s begun to hallucinate her? It’s not until the woman turns and Carol can see her profile that she can in fact confirm it is her.

    Shit.

    What the fuck is she doing here? Of all the grocery stores in Albuquerque, why this one? Does she live nearby? Have they unknowingly been living in the same neighbourhood? Carol quickly ducks behind a tall display of potted basil and other fresh herbs and she suddenly feels warm.

    She doesn’t want to be seen like this; and not by Zosia in particular. Something she’ll have to unpack later. Her hair is still damp from her shower, her sweatpants are unflattering, and her t-shirt is old and stretched out. It used to belong to Helen and it may or may not have a faded years old brownish-red stain on it, which is a moment that Carol remembers vividly; when a ketchup packet had exploded onto Helen (and the roof of the car) in the middle of their seven hour road trip to Denver to see Radiohead at the Red Rocks Amphitheatre.

    The smell of the basil is too strong and Carol wrinkles her nose at it. She can’t even imagine what she’d say to Zosia, or if Zosia would even acknowledge her. She watches through the herbs in a way that Carol convinces herself isn’t creepy while she’s still trapped.

    It’s weird almost, seeing Zosia like this out in the wild; not within the confines of their workplace. It dawns on Carol that for all the times she’s pictured her naked, she’d never once thought about what she would look like when she wasn’t at work. No make up, unkempt (but somehow seductive) hair, completely dressed down very much similar to the way Carol is.

    Zosia looks peaceful and unguarded and it’s hard to imagine that this is the same woman who gives Carol a headache almost everyday at work.

    It occurs to Carol then too, while she watches Zosia pick up an avocado and inspect it for ripeness, that she doesn’t actually know anything about this woman other than her name. But why would she? Zosia has been nothing but a dismissive enigma on a power trip since they’ve started working together.

    It hadn’t been until Friday, when they had their, for lack of better term, strange encounter in the evidence room and then elevator (which for the record, Carol has not been able to stop thinking about—had she imagined it? Was Zosia actually flirting with her or was it just in her head?), that Carol could have ever entertained the notion that Zosia would or could be anything more than that.

    After what feels like an eternity, the old lady moves out of the way—“oh, sorry love,” she finally croaks out—and Carol quickly dashes out of the produce section, wheels squeaking away, while Zosia is facing the other direction.

    In her car, Carol realizes she’s missed about half the items on her grocery list. She’s annoyed and disappointed that for everything she’s been through in the last decade, she has allowed Zosia, of all people, to fluster her in a way that makes her feel like she has no control. And for the life of her, Carol can’t remember the last time she had ever felt this way around another woman since Helen.

    And Carol believes that Helen would be disappointed too. Well, more so embarrassed or unimpressed because if she could somehow communicate with the spirit of her dead wife right now, she’d be rolling in her grave and saying, “I didn’t die for this shit.”

    Zosia hadn’t even seen her for fucks sake.

    Carol groans, softly banging her head into her steering wheel.

    +

    The bullpen crackles with a nervous tension this morning; an erratic hum of whispers and stares. Chief Schrader had called Zosia and Laxmi into his office nearly thirty minutes ago. Every so often, he can be heard raising his voice, though muffled through all the sounds of phones ringings and keyboards clicking and clacking. Through the frosted glass window, his silhouette paces back and forth while Laxmi sits small and unmoving, and Zosia stands tall leaned up against the window frame.

    Carol anxiously hugs the warmth of her travel mug against her chest, content that she had finally woken up early enough so as to avoid the office sludge. Slowly, she sips through the lid and wishes she could be a fly on the wall.

    “Sounds like someone’s getting the boot,” she says to Koumba, whose desk she’s perched on. She’s known the Chief long enough to know exactly what’s going on behind that door. Across from Koumba, Manousos pretends he’s not invested, flipping through his notepad.

    Koumba fidgets restlessly in his chair, clicking his pen nonstop. “Well she’s definitely not getting promoted. This is the third time she’s misplaced and mislabeled evidence.”

    “On the same case,” Manousos adds, matter-of-factly, then looks back to his notepad.

    Carol scoffs and shakes her head. “I knew it would be too big for her,” she says.

    She remembers advising Laxmi that perhaps she was not ready enough to tackle the likes of Cartel and respectfully, and most definitely not condescendingly, that she should stick to data entry and analyzation instead. The look of anger on Laxmi, pure unbridled fury that was usually only reserved for Carol, had been nothing short of comical. It’s a complicated love-hate relationship they have. And now it may soon be no longer.

    Finally, the door to the Chief’s office opens and a stark silence falls over the bullpen. Eyes perk up, keyboards stop clacking and it’s as if the phones know not to ring.

    Only Laxmi exits. Her head is downcast as she makes a beeline down the hall towards the bathroom.

    “Mhm,” Carol hums and Koumba can be heard letting out a long knowing sigh.

    A second later the Chief emerges, his stocky figure filling the doorframe. His eyes, cold and steady as the tide, scans through the bullpen.

    “Show’s over folks. Get back to work,” he says low and deep as he re-closes the door. And suddenly the bullpen comes back to life as if nothing had happened.

    “Guess you’ll be getting a new partner.” Carol reaches over, giving Koumba a gentle squeeze on the shoulder.

    “You know she’s been on the Lieutenant’s shit-list since she’s been here.” Koumba folds his arms and sits back, as if he’s accepting his fate. “She was on borrowed time.”

    Carol rolls her eyes. She’s no stranger to having the Lieutenant’s eyes on her. “She’s not even the boss.”

    “No,” Manousos says, “but she reports to the boss.”

    “As long as I don’t get stuck with Lazy Lou,” Koumba says.

    “So what do you think they are talking about in there?” Manousos asks.

    On cue, the Chief’s office door opens again, except this time it’s less of a spectacle. His head pokes out around the door and he immediately looks directly at Carol and she feels the tips of her ears flush with heat.

    He doesn’t say anything, just motions her into his office with the curl of his finger.

    Carol looks to both Manousos and Koumba, who both whisper “oooh” at the same time, to which she tuts back at.

    “Maybe you’re about to be partnerless too,” she says to Manousos.

    -

    As usual, the Chief’s office is suffused with the scent of cheap cigars and day old coffee. The one long fluorescent light on the ceiling buzzes like it’s about to flicker out. The only thing thicker than the smell in here is the tension.

    Carol closes the door behind her. She doesn’t mean to, but the first place her gaze settles is onto Zosia, who has one hip leaned up against a filing cabinet, arms crossed.

    Unfortunately for Carol, Zosia is an absolute smoke show in her all black outfit and leather jacket, a look that begs no permission. And though Zosia might share the same exasperation as the Chief, she still finds a moment to smile at Carol, the subtlest of sparks flickering between their gazes. Carol finds it impossible to look away.

    So maybe she hadn’t imagined what happened on Friday.‘I just know what I want’ and the way Zosia had looked at her had played on repeat in her mind all weekend. It’s the same way she’s looking at her now and it’s barely 10AM.

    Carol quickly redirects her gaze to Chief Schrader. She doesn’t bother to sit and the Chief never asks her to. Instead, he lets out a shaky breath and looks up at her from behind his desk. There’s no time for pleasantries and formalities. He gets straight to the point.

    “I’m sure you’ve already figured it out but Detective Sharma has been relieved of her duties effective immediately. The timing wasn’t ideal but it was necessary. That said, we have a big lead on the Diaz case so the department will need to do some reshuffling.”

    Reshuffling? The last time Carol had heard that word, one detective was demoted and two were transferred out and replaced by two from Santa Fe. She swallows, her stomach twisting in knots. What does this have to do with her?

    “So what exactly does that mean?” Carol asks.

    “It means we now know who murdered Miss Diaz. His name is Mateo Peña. Thirty one years old. He was given the order to take the hit on Miss Diaz as collateral when her boyfriend, Jason Chan, failed to repay a hefty debt to the Cartel.”

    “Who gave the order?”

    The Chief’s eyes light up sickly, as if he’d been waiting to give Carol the news.

    “Our good friend, Nacho Varga.”

    Finally from the corner, Zosia pipes up. “He’s a high ranking member of the Salamanca Drug Cartel, but you already knew that.”

    Carol almost rolls her eyes because of course she already knew that. It’s as if Zosia had forgotten that she had been the one to do the background check on Zoe Diaz and it was her and Manousos that had found Ignacio Varga’s name.

    Furthermore, Carol has had the displeasure of knowing of the Salamancas and all their constituents very well. Her and Manousos had had a very unpleasant encounter with Lalo Salamanca in a remote gas station off I-25 a few years ago which had resulted in shots fired and windshields shattered. If Carol’s reputation had truly crossed state lines as Zosia had told her, then Zosia would have definitely known that too.

    She steals a sideways glance at Zosia, who is already (or still) looking at her, and then looks back to the Chief.

    “Okay, so what does this have to do with reshuffling then?” Carol asks, the knots getting tighter.

    “We got a tip from a trusted informant that Peña is meeting Varga at Motel 6 right off of Old Highway 60 tomorrow night and—“ the Chief grins widely, eyes now bright, and points at both Carol and Zosia, “I want you two to lead the stakeout. Surveillance only. You are not to spook them. Peña is a flight risk.”

    Carol’s eyes widen, her jaw tightens. “You want…me and…” she points at Zosia as if there were another person standing there.

    “Is there a problem, Detective Sturka?” Zosia does not miss a beat. She leans off of the filing cabinet and comes around the Chief’s desk all the while never breaking eye contact with Carol.

    “What about Manny?” Carol looks to the Chief. “My partner?”

    “I’m re-assigning Manny with Koumba,” Chief says, “they’ll be your back up if things go south. They won’t be far behind.”

    “Re-assigning?” Carol can’t actually believe the words she’s hearing. Nearly a decade with Manousos and now it might be taken away from her. “Like…permanently?”

    “Carol,” the Chief leans forward, elbows on his desk. His gaze softens, but just barely. “This is a huge case with a huge lead. I need the very best on it. If you’re not up for it then—“

    “No, no.” Carol stands taller, instantly emboldened by the challenge. Fuck if she would ever turn away from something like this. Even more fuck if she would voluntarily give Zosia the satisfaction and ego boost. “If you want the best then you’re getting the best.”

    “Good.” The Chief exhales, his cigar breath sailing past Carol’s nose. “It’s simply eyes only tomorrow. No action, no sudden movements. Understood?”

    Carol nods, but it’s slower than she means to. She’s still trying to process what’s happening and truthfully, the aroma in the office is making her feel woozy.

    Zosia’s been quieter than usual this morning, but from the corner of her eye, Carol can see the slow tilt of Zosia’s head, like she’s being studied.

    “You look worried, Detective.” Zosia’s tone is smug, but teasing.

    Chief Schrader, for his own sanity, is completely oblivious.

    Carol meets Zosia’s gaze and holds it for maybe a beat too long. The knots have disappeared and that hot and bright feeling returns to her stomach. If Zosia wants a challenge, she’ll give her one.

    “Oh, far from it, Lieutenant.” She turns her whole body to face Zosia full on. “This is the first time we’re actually working a case together so I just hope you can keep up.”

    Zosia’s lips curve upwards into something between a grin and a dare. “Don’t worry about me, Detective. I have great stamina.”

    For just a moment, Carol forgets that she’s at work and that the Chief is right there until he leans back loudly in his chair.

    “Great,” he says satisfied. “See, you two will be fine. Think of it as bonding time. A coming together.” (A regrettable choice of words, Carol thinks). “I’ll email you both the details soon. Carol, send Manny and Koumba my way, will ya?”

    And with that he politely gestures both Carol and Zosia out of his office.

    They both turn to leave at the same time, their arms and shoulders brushing together in the door way. Carol feels the heat from Zosia’s body and is expecting her to force her way through but she doesn’t. Carol thinks she may be imagining it, but she swears she feels Zosia’s hand on the small of her back, gently ushering her out first.

    “See you later, partner,” Zosia whispers a little too closely to Carol’s ear for it to be accidental.

    Tiny goosebumps prick at the back of Carol’s neck but by the time she turns around, Zosia is already headed the other way. She balls her hands into tight fists, swallowing hard.

    This is not exactly the coming together she had envisioned.

    +

    “It’s bullshit,” Carol mutters, jamming her thumb into the start button of the copy machine. It thwips and whirrs with great effort before sounding like it’s choking on marbles.

    “It’s not the end of the world,” Manousos offers, shooing her aside so he can un-jam the machine. “We are still going to be on cases together. We are literally still going to be in the same office.”

    Carol huffs, pacing back and forth a little too closely behind Manousos. Quickly, he turns around and stills her by the shoulders, willing her to stop moving.

    “You’re only saying that because you still get to do fun things with Koumba.”

    “If your definition of fun is looking at dead bodies and crime scenes, then sure.”

    “And I…” she shakes her head and her voice trails off. “My first case with her is a stakeout, Manny. You know how long and boring those are? That car won’t be big enough for the both of us.”

    “Hey, what are you saying? We’ve been on hundreds of stakeouts together.”

    “That’s different.”

    “How so?”

    ‘Because I was never debilitatingly attracted to you,’ is what Carol wants to say. Instead, “have you met her?”

    Finally, Manousos un-jams the machine. He pulls out the torn shreds of paper, hands covered in black ink.

    “Maybe you should give her a chance. Get to know her. You’d be surprised what a couple of hours in a small space can do. You think you were all rainbows y mariposas when we first started working together?”

    “My wife had just died.”

    “You can’t play the dead wife card anymore.”

    Carol rolls her eyes, annoyed that Manousos is right, but he usually is.

    “I don’t like when you get all righteous on me.”

    “Then it’s a good thing we won’t be working together anymore.”

    “Pendejo.” 

    +

    The sky is dusted with stars, like white paint specks on dark canvas when Carol leaves the office, a welcomed break from the melancholy the clouds have threatened for the past week. The asphalt still holds the heat of the day as she crosses into the mostly empty parking lot; the lively hum of the building now dissipated, most everyone gone home except for those working the night shift.

    Carol had stayed late to catch up on paperwork knowing that with tomorrow’s stakeout, there’d be no time and she would fall behind. Just as she’s approaching her car, a noise draws her attention from around the corner of the building—the rusty squeak of the back stairwell door being opened, footsteps on the pavement, and then the distinct flick of a lighter.

    Curiosity gets the better of Carol. She creeps around the corner and that’s when she sees Zosia leaning up against the brick wall, one foot outstretched onto the lower bar of the railing across from her, the tip of her cigarette glowing bright and red in the night like a beacon. Carol swears the lights had been off in her office when she left. The smoke curls around her face like a ribbon, silver against the gathering blue.

    Zosia must feel her presence because slowly she turns her head and catches Carol watching her. No words are exchanged. She looks at Carol in a way that makes time feel frozen and Carol’s stomach flip-flops.

    She’s on the nick of backing away when Zosia holds the pack out to her like an invitation.

    Carol shouldn’t. It’s been years since her last cigarette—the taste and smell reminds her too much of Helen—but against her better judgement, and because of Manousos’ stupid (but wise) words ringing in her head, she decides fuck it.

    And there’s something about the way the moonlight hangs between them, quiet like a breath, that erases any dissidence from the last couple weeks and draws her forward.

    Carol slides a cigarette out of the pack and places it between her lips. It fits like muscle memory. She’s expecting Zosia to hand her the lighter, but instead, Zosia pushes off the wall and takes a small step forward, sparking the lighter for her. The heat whooshes against Carol’s face and flutters in the passing breeze so she cups her hands around the flame, the edges of her palms resting softly on Zosia’s hand.

    She inhales slowly, the sudden rush of heat tickling at her throat, then relishes the way it blooms warmly throughout her chest. Maybe she does miss smoking.

    It’s then, Zosia’s left hand still suspended between them, that Carol notices the faintest tan line on her ring finger, but as quickly as she notices, Zosia pulls away, leaning back against the wall.

    “I didn’t take you for a smoker,” Carol says, letting the smoke billow out of her mouth as she mirrors Zosia stance.

    “I’m not.” Zosia pauses. “Usually.”

    “So what’s the occasion?”

    Zosia takes a long, heavy drag and then looks to Carol as if she’s deciding what to say. She blows the smoke up into the sky and then flicks away the tip of ash.

    “It’s my son’s eighteenth birthday,” she says after a brief moment, her tone marked with wist.

    For a second, Carol stupidly short-circuits. She wonders if she’s actually heard that correctly because on the list of things that she had envisioned Zosia would or could be, mother was not one of them. But then again, she still knows nothing about this woman. She’s about to open her mouth and say something, but Zosia cuts her off as if she regrets telling her and doesn’t want any follow up.

    “So what are you doing here so late?” Zosia asks.

    Carol takes the hint. “Have you seen the case load upstairs?” she says in-between drags. “The paperwork isn’t going to do itself.”

    Zosia lets a soft breath out of her nose, something between a laugh and a scoff. “Do you always work this hard?”

    “If I don’t, who will? I take my job seriously. Is there something wrong with that?”

    “No.” Zosia shakes her head, her eyes fluttering all over Carol’s face. “It’s admirable, actually.” And that’s the first earnest thing she’s said to Carol, no bite, no ulterior meaning.

    “Oh…”

    “Don’t act so surprised, Sturka. You know you’re the hardest working person in this department. Schrader would be fucked without you.”

    A compliment from Zosia? It’s not exactly something that Carol would have expected to check off her list given the way the day had started. This morning it would have been a trap, a heated exchange of words as a way for Zosia to rile her up or get under her skin; but now, alone and under the honesty of the late night sky, it means something.

    “I needed a distraction after…“ Carol rolls the tip of her cigarette between her fingers and watches as the ash floats to the pavement. Her chest begins to tighten and her throat runs dry.

    “Your wife died.” Zosia finishes the sentence for her. It’s not even a question. Even in her moment of earnestness, her boldness never leaves her and for once Carol is thankful for that.

    Carol nods, gaze steady on a lamppost at the edge of the parking lot.

    “Winner, winner,” she says, shooting Zosia one finger gun and a click of her tongue. It earns her a quiet laugh. “I threw myself into my work and it’s all I’ve known since then.”

    “How long ago was that?”

    “Almost ten years.”

    Carol waits for the ‘so what happened?’ because there is always one, but it never comes.

    Zosia turns to face Carol, leaning her shoulder against the wall. She crosses her arms with her cigarette still neatly tucked between her fingers.

    “Grief is a bitch,” she says after another long drag.

    Carol meets her gaze. Those words hang heavy in the air as she tries to read Zosia’s expression in the dim light, but Zosia turns again and leans her elbows onto the railing in front of them and looks to the sky.

    “You say that like you know,” Carol suggests.

    Zosia shrugs. “I know a lot of things.” Her tone is unreadable. She drops the now-butt to the ground and stomps it out with the toe of her shoe. She’s quick to change the subject. “So don’t you want to know why I’m here so late?”

    Carol leans off the wall but doesn’t mimic Zosia’s stance this time; just stands a little behind her and lets her eyes slowly sweep down and then back up the length of her body. She wants to know what Zosia means by that, but something tells her not to push.

    “So why are you here so late?”

    “I thought you’d never ask. Schrader asked me to tie up Laxmi’s loose ends. That woman was a ticking time bomb of mistakes.”

    Right. That happened.

    “Yeah well try working with her for four years.”

    It’s muted, but Zosia laughs again. Once more she turns around, now leaning back against the railing with her elbows perched on it, ankles crossed.

    “We can’t all be Detective Carol Sturka, can we?” She smirks. It allows some breathing room in the heaviness that had settled.

    “I’m not sure you’d want to be.”

    “Hmm. And she’s modest too.” Zosia stares right into Carol’s eyes as she says it.

    Carol holds steady, doesn’t break the gaze. She simply stares back, seemingly lost in the pools of golden honey that are Zosia’s eyes, waiting to see who will break first. Much to her surprise, it’s Zosia who does and maybe it’s too dark to tell, but Carol is almost certain she’s blushing.

    “It’s late,” Carol says, recomposing herself. She flicks the butt of her cigarette away and it lands in a small patch of grass before the embers fizzle out. This is the longest and realest conversation she's had with Zosia and she oddly isn't ready for it to end, but she's suddenly aware of the time. “I need to get home. And you should too if you want to be able to keep up tomorrow night.”

    Zosia raises an eyebrow. “You do know we’re going on a stakeout right? Not running a marathon.”

    Carol raises an eyebrow back. Higher, arguably. “Have you been on a stakeout before?” It’s redundant. She takes a step forward and rests an arm on the railing, fingers coolly wrapped around the metal bar. This time she’s the one sizing Zosia up. “Or are you just used to solving murders in bright casinos and wedding chapels?”

    Zosia’s eyes do that glimmer-thing like she’s asking for the challenge. “Your point?”

    “They require mental stamina.”

    “Like I said this morning, I have great stamina. I can go all night.”

    The urge for Carol to roll her eyes is only subdued by the fact that her pulse beating in every part of her body.

    “Are you always this insufferable?”

    Zosia’s eyes widen almost indiscernibly. “Careful, Detective,” she says lowly, taking a deliberate step forward. She places her hand on the metal bar next to Carol’s and their fingers brush together. Neither of them pulls away. She tilts her head down ever so slightly. “That almost sounds like a compliment.”

    A spark of electricity runs through Carol’s body and the space between them burns brighter for it. She looks up at Zosia, who even in flat shoes, towers over her. From this close, she can smell the faded scent of her perfume, hidden behind the nicotine and tobacco, and the way it’s clung to her skin like a memory. It’s intoxicating.

    “That’s a low bar,” Carol whispers, her voice suddenly caught in her throat.

    “Then maybe someone should set it higher.”

    Carol swallows. She can’t think of her next words fast enough. But luckily, or unluckily, the sudden pitter-patter of footsteps and a man’s voice from inside the stairwell pulls Carol and Zosia apart. Carol’s heart is beating both somewhere in her ears and in between her legs; and Zosia takes a step back, running a hand through her hair. The door swings open and Tim from the nightshift steps out. He’s on his phone.

    “Yo, isn’t your shift like, hours over?” He says, hanging up the call. He obliviously inserts himself between Carol and Zosia, pulling a pack of his own cigarettes out of his pocket.

    “Uh, yeah,” Carol stutters, still unable to look away from Zosia. “I’m actually on my way home right now.”

    “Mhm, me too,” Zosia echoes with a forced smile plastered on her face for the benefit of Tim. “We have a long day tomorrow.”

    “Right on,” Tim says, rummaging through his pockets. “Yo, anyone got a light?”

    Zosia rolls her eyes and reluctantly tosses her lighter at him. She takes a step to her side so she can look at Carol.

    “Goodnight, Detective,” she says, as if Tim were suddenly completely invisible, and her smile is uncharacteristically timid.

    Carol returns a smile in similar fashion. “Goodnight, Lieutenant,” she replies, and just like that they head in opposite directions.

    +

    Carol drives home in a daze. The radio is nothing but murmurs and sounds and the neon lights whip past her in a muddled blur.

    What the fuck was that?

    She squeezes her eyes shut at a red light. Maybe it was a dream. Maybe she had fallen asleep in her office and just imagined the whole thing. But her blazer smells like Malboros and her fingers still burn from the warm touch of Zosia’s.

    And for as much grievance that Zosia has given her in the little time they have known each other, one thing Carol has learned about her is that she has been deliberate and real in every word she says and action she takes.

    Still, she’s not sure how she got to this point so quickly because not too long ago was she bemoaning the mere thought of having to spend time with her.

    So maybe she still doesn’t know a fucking thing about her, and perhaps she never will, but all Carol knows is that for the first time in a long time, she’s excited about a stakeout.

    She’s just…excited.

    The light turns green but she doesn’t go until the car behind her honks. It’s not until she catches her reflection in the windshield that she realizes she’s been smiling. It surprises her so she fights it at first, clenching her jaw tight, but soon realizes she can’t.

    Because for a day that had dawned so bleak, it blooms with hope and warmth before the dusk.

     

    Chapter End Notes

    I had a lot of fucking fun writing this chapter so I hope you guys enjoy reading it :D

    Chapter 4: the stakeout

    Chapter Notes

    First person to tell me how many times Carol scoffs or rolls her eyes gets to kiss me ;)

    The sunlight spills into Carol’s bedroom; a sharp blade slicing through the curtains landing square between her eyes. It wakes her up loud and unforgiving.

    For a moment, she lays perfectly still, slowly blinking her eyes into focus and letting the soft but incessant thumping behind her temples dissipate. Not booze related this morning, but too much caffeine, not enough sleep.

    Carol rolls to her side and glances at the clock on her nightstand. 7:58AM—two minutes before her alarm goes off. Typical.

    On most, if not all mornings, she thinks of Helen. Their wedding picture on the dresser across the bed is the first thing she sees. She thinks about how the empty space next to her should be occupied by her wife, but instead it’s cold and bare. Sometimes she’ll wake up next to her bright pink vibrator, though that’s hardly a consolation.

    This morning she doesn’t think of Helen. Not immediately anyways.

    Instead, Carol thinks about whatever the hell last night with Zosia had been. The quiet, heated banter, low and amused, mixed with the unexpected intimacy that she hadn’t realized sharing a cigarette under the night sky could bring.

    Then there’s the stupid little smile she had donned on the drive home that now, in the washed-out morning light—too bright for its own good—Carol feels entirely embarrassed by.

    This is someone she works with for fucks sake. Her Lieutenant. Her extremely tall, rude, pompous, fuckable Lieutenant.

    “Snap out of it,” she tells herself, throwing the covers back and sitting up.

    Groggily, she tiptoes to the bathroom, hardwood cool beneath each step, then turning into tile. Through the open window, the neighbour’s dog barking gets muffled into the too loud and too close rumble of a lawn mower.

    Carol moves through her routine on autopilot. Toothbrush, shower, hair, and this morning in particular, the faintest, almost accidental dusting of make up. Her state of the art Swiss-made espresso machine sputters and hisses downstairs in the kitchen while she shrugs on a pair of light denim jeans, a faded grey UNM Lobos t-shirt, and the plaid button up shirt that’s helped her blend into every lesbian bar from Phoenix to Denver. A uniform by no means, but the ideal outfit for when she needs to stay unassuming and be expected to camp out in a car for god knows how long.

    Stakeout day.

    Carol should be thinking about the team briefing which is soon approaching. The case notes, radio checks, gear, and everything in-between. Instead, her mind continues to betray her and slip sideways to last night.

    That perpetual, dangerous glint in Zosia’s eyes tinged with a certain kind of sadness that Carol wonders if Zosia knew she had picked up on. The way that Zosia had stepped so close that Carol could smell her perfume cutting through the tobacco and smoke, leather jacket open just enough to expose her throat and collarbone. It was no mistake that their fingers had brushed together and neither pulled apart. Or at least that’s what Carol pathetically tells herself.

    In the kitchen, Carol absently butters a slice of toast. The morning news murmurs from the TV in the living room while she tries to push the memory away of Zosia’s dark eyes, sharp in the light, and the way her words had been so precise and calculated.

    “She’s just a flirt,” Carol tries to convince herself with a mouthful of bread, mumbling into the empty kitchen, as if someone were there to hear her. “Hot and she knows it.” She rolls her eyes.

    Still, it’s not enough to explain the way she lights on fire anytime Zosia so much as glances at her. Or the way she had laid awake for much of the night replaying and overanalyzing every cadence of their last few encounters.

    Thankfully, Carol’s phone chimes on the counter, giving her a most welcomed distraction. It’s an email from the Chief outlining some changes to the day’s original plan. Carol scans through it quickly. The team briefing is cancelled due to a last minute tip that Mateo Peña is on the move and that surveillance needs to start as soon as possible. Something about coordinates and radio frequencies, and then something in the last line of the email that due to some boring logistics, her and Zosia would have to be in two separate cars to ensure maximum efficiency and coverage of the motel.

    Carol wrinkles her nose at this, unsure if she wants to be upset or pleased. It’s just that last night she had looked forward to today and now, without the veil of the nighttime to hide in and a clear mind, she decides she was simply too caught up in the moment and this is definitely for the better.

    Or maybe.

    Then she looks to her watch.

    Shit.

    Carol scrambles. She had planned to stop at Sprouts beforehand to pick up snacks and drinks, but now it looks she like may not have the time. She rummages through her pantry for anything of sustenance.

    Peanuts.

    Raisins.

    Crackers.

    And…jerky.

    Almost enough for a sad charcuterie board.

    “Really?” She sighs. Where had all her groceries from the weekend gone? It’ll have to do.

    Quickly, she fills her yellow travel mug, throws all her stuff into her a bag and she’s out the door.

    +

    Outside, the air is still cool. The sky, an uninterrupted blue, only promises heat later. Carol slips into her Dodge Charger, the one that had been issued to her years ago by the department specifically for days like this. Still, it carries the stale scent of a pine air freshener. Carol hurriedly flips through her notes for the address of the strip mall parking lot that she’ll call home for the next several hours, and then tosses the brown file folder, edges already soft and worn, onto the passenger seat next to her. No need for GPS today, she knows exactly where it is.

    Carol flies down I-25, the Sandia Mountains looming wide in her rearview mirror. Traffic, fortunately, is thin this morning, commuters already at work, and mostly just city busses hogging up the right lane. She’s about halfway to her destination when her phone buzzes in the cupholder. She glances to it thinking it’s Manousos, but it’s not.

    It’s Zosia.

    Her grip tightens around the steering wheel as she forces her eyes to stay forward on the road. The last time Zosia had texted her had been almost a week ago when she had practically ordered her and Manousos into her office for a case briefing. Never great with her manners, that one.

    The second Carol stops at a red light, she snatches her phone.

    Morning, partner. You stopping to get the good coffee or am I?

    Carol blinks at the screen. It’s so unexpected and the words snag at her. She types out a response faster than her brain can actually process it; before the light can turn green.

    I have coffee. It’s fine.

    It’s an incredibly lame response, but almost as quickly, Zosia texts back.

    And you didn’t bring enough for me? :(

    Carol’s mind blanks at a response. She’s halfway to typing out something about being too rushed but Zosia beats her to it, like she doesn’t give a flying fuck about what a double text is.

    Kidding but ‘fine’ is how people describe airplane food so I’m stopping.

    Rude. Carol actually really likes airplane food and the way everything is so compact and well thought out. But she can picture Zosia, one hand on the steering wheel as she taps out the message at a red light, hair blowing in the breeze of the open window, stupid mirrored sunglasses resting arrogantly atop her head when she could just wear them instead.

    Carol doesn’t respond. She shoves her phone back into the cupholder and tells herself that the quick flutter she feels in her chest is just irritation and nothing else. If she tries hard enough she can reset the memory of last night so she can actually focus on her job today.

    It’s just that Zosia has been on the team for hardly three weeks now and each and everyday she strides into the office like this role has been her god given right; like she doesn’t see or care about the way other people watch her. Always laughing too easily when the Chief is around. Always having a goddamn opinion about everything.

    And then there’s the whole being right about everything, which is worse.

    But then again, Carol is also right about everything, which is even worse worse. Because how were they meant to co-exist?

    It’s just after 10:30AM when Carol arrives to the run-down strip mall. The parking lot is already shimmering, the heat radiating off the asphalt. She parks two spots in from the edge of the lot facing the motel’s west side entrance. Behind her is Joyce’s Nail Salon, which is sandwiched between Halliday’s Hardware and an unnamed dry cleaners.

    There are a handful of cars scattered throughout the lot. Little enough to provide some privacy, but enough as to not create much suspicion.

    Carol is in the middle of setting up her equipment—binoculars, telephoto lens, notepad, radio—when Zosia swings into the parking lot in her Ford Explorer. Of course she would make a show of it. A little too fast and furious as she rounds the corner, dust kicking up like a whirlwind, but nails the parking job in one smooth turn. She parks several spots over on the opposite end of the row facing the motel’s east side entrance just as the Chief’s email had indicated.

    The sun catches her hair as she steps out of the vehicle, the deep brown almost glinting black. Her white tank top is too tight under her unbutton chambray shirt, sleeves rolled to her elbows, and her black jeans, ripped at the knees are tucked neatly into her usual combat boots. It’s casual enough to pass as any other woman running errands on a weekday, but more than enough to make Carol’s throat run dry.

    Jesus Christ, she thinks. Zosia would look this fucking good for a stakeout.

    Carol watches her the entire time as she walks over to her car, strutting like the lines on the parking lot are her catwalk. With each step she gets closer, Carol’s pulse quickens at the same rate. She wonders if Zosia had lost the same amount of sleep as she had thinking about last night.

    Zosia doesn’t stop at the passenger window, but instead walks around the car and right to the driver’s side. She taps precisely three times on the window with her pointer finger.

    “Roll down, Detective. It’s too early for miming.” Her voice is muffled behind the glass.

    Carol gets in a quick eye roll before obliging, the window whirring halfway down.

    Zosia stares at her incredulously. “I don’t bite.”

    I wish you would.

    Carol feels that heat building in her stomach. She rolls the window all the way down. “We’re supposed to keep a low-profile,” she says, aiming for something dry and sarcastic, but it lands somewhere more breathless.

    “Good morning to you too,” Zosia says, lifting her sunglasses to her head. Her eyes hold the heat of the morning sun, threads of copper gleaming in the gold. She holds up a cardboard drink holder with two iced coffees. “Relax. I brought tribute. It’s real coffee. Not whatever crime you’ve committed in that thing.” She nods at the infamous yellow travel mug in the cupholder.

    “You don’t even know what it tastes like,” Carol says, indignant.

    “I don’t need to.” Zosia wrinkles her nose. “I can smell it from here. It smells like if burnt toast had an existential crisis.”

    It’s not even that funny but Carol still feels a laugh pushing at her chest. She tries desperately to swallow it, but it comes out anyways in a soft, reluctant huff that makes Zosia’s eyes glint.

    “There she is.” Zosia’s mouth slowly curves into a grin. “I was beginning to think you only had one expression.”

    Carol scoffs. “And what’s that? Annoyed?”

    “Annoyed…irritated…” Zosia ponders. “Occasional despair.”

    “Wow.” Carol reaches for one of the iced coffees, plastic already sweating from the heat, and takes a sip simply just to avoid acknowledging her words. It’s begrudgingly good—strong, just sweet enough, and…is that oat milk? She has half a mind to remind Zosia that her wife, her life partner of two decades is dead. She doesn’t. “Starbucks doesn’t make you a profiler.”

    “I’m an observer of human nature,” Zosia counters. “And your human nature is—” She vaguely gestures at Carol’s face. “Prickly. But it’s fine, we can work on it. You know I like a challenge.”

    Carol feels her cheeks redden under the already scorching sun. She can’t tell if Zosia had winked or just squinted the brightness out of her eyes. But there it is again. This line that’s been teetering the edge between flirty, provocative, and playful. Yesterday and all the days before it had been a challenge. Today it feels…worse? Or better? No, just different. But whatever it is, it makes Carol all too aware of the shape of her own mouth.

    “So where are we staking?” Zosia asks after a beat.

    “Here?” Carol furrows her brows. “What do you mean?”

    “Your car or mine?”

    “Didn’t you read Schrader’s email? We stake in both…for better coverage.”

    Zosia pouts theatrically. “You don’t expect me to sit alone all day, do you?”

    Again, Carol’s chest flutters, but this time it’s harder to pretend it’s irritation. “Just keep your radio hands-free and on the shared channel. And two cars is less conspicuous,” she says unconvincingly. She would also rather be stuck in a box with Zosia because at least that way she’d have something interesting to look at.

    “I would hardly call the twenty feet that separates us better coverage,” Zosia says, resting an arm onto the open window and leaning in slightly but deliberately. “I can see the entire building from here. Both exits.”

    She’s so close again that her hair flutters in and practically tickles Carol’s cheek. It sends a rush of goosebumps trickling down her spine.

    Then again, maybe two cars is better.

    “Get in your car, Lieutenant,” Carol says just above a whisper, hiding behind the title because using Zosia all of a sudden feels too intense.

    Finally, Zosia complies with a sly grin. “Yes, ma’am.” It snakes out of her mouth and slithers through Carol’s ears and settles somewhere deep in the pit of her stomach.

    Zosia struts back to her Explorer, her hips swaying like she knows she’s being watched.

    Alone now, Carol puts herself into work mode, pulling the case folder from her passenger seat onto her lap. She opens it to the pertinent information and waits for Zosia to settle into her own car. A short moment later, the radio crackles and Zosia can be heard letting out a long, drawn out sigh.

    “I’m bored already, Sturka.”

    “That’s dramatic. Just keep your window cracked and your gear ready.”

    “You make it sound like we’re going into active combat.”

    Carol shakes her head and reads out the case notes.

    “Okay, so we’re on the look out for either a dark grey Honda Civic, no plates. Or a white Jeep Cherokee, unknown plates. Peña, last seen in Santa Fe last night, likely heading south to El Paso at some point today and stopping at the motel for a handoff of unknown goods with Varga, but we can assume what. Varga is here regularly, motel staff is familiar with him so we keep eyes on them too. Varga is unpredictable and Peña is a flight risk so we just hang back and log anything and everything.”

    “Roger that,” Zosia says, unamused. “I read the file too, you know.”

    “Reading and listening are two different skills.”

    “What a relief. I was worried you’d run out of ways to insult me.”

    “I can make a list if you want.”

    Zosia scoffs. “I bet you already have one.”

    Carol stays quiet. To Zosia’s credit, she’s not actually wrong. For the first two weeks Carol had kept a list, mentally that is.

    Overconfident.

    Bossy.

    Rude.

    Talks over people.

    Sometimes dresses like she’s going out instead of to work.

    It had been easier to catalog her flaws rather than try to overcome them.

    The hours slide by in slow increments. Eleven. Twelve. One. And every hour or so, Carol and Zosia take turns walking around the strip mall to stretch their legs and go to the bathroom. By two o’clock, they settle into a familiar but monotonous rhythm. Carol logs times and detailed descriptions and Zosia mutters observations and unwarranted commentary through the radio in between stretches of silence.

    And in between all of that, Carol, every so often will glance sideways to Zosia and on more than one occasion, Zosia is already looking back at her.

    “That old housekeeper just stepped out for another smoke break,” Zosia says, exasperated, around 2:30PM.

    “Third time?” Carol asks, jotting it down.

    “Fourth since we’ve been watching,” Zosia corrects. “Who knows about before. It’s like she’s training for the nicotine Olympics. I’m jealous.”

    Carol almost smiles. “I’ll be sure to add that to my notes—oldest Olympian in New Mexico.”

    “I mean look at her.” Zosia’s camera shutters twice in the background. “That woman is practically a historical site. She’ll outlive us all.”

    This time, Carol has to stifle a laugh. There’s that playful banter again that creeps out of Zosia in the most unexpected (and sometimes inappropriate) moments. It had driven Carol insane at first because how could someone be so flippant and still get the job done?

    But now, day by day, Carol learns that Zosia’s work is immaculate. Her logs are precise, her instincts are sharp (her wit even sharper), and she never misses a single detail. Safe to say her consecutive Detective of the Year awards hadn’t been a fluke.

    The levity isn’t negligence, it’s scaffolding.

    The time continues to drag on and by almost four o’clock, the heat inside the car grows thick and heavy. Carol has long ditched her plaid over shirt and wipes away the thin layer of sweat that covers the back of her neck. Her AC cannot keep up.

    Zosia must feel the same because at the same time, a loud whooshing comes through the radio.

    “Turn it down,” Carol says, “you sound like you’re in a wind tunnel.”

    “I’m melting,” Zosia complains. “I’m surprised you haven’t combusted in there yet.”

    “I’m fine,” Carol feigns, now wiping at her forehead. “It’s not that bad.”

    “Liar. I can literally see you sweating.”

    Carol looks over at Zosia who waves at her; Carol rolls her eyes.

    Then, after a beat Zosia says carefully, “You can always just come sit over here. My AC is better and I have better snacks. Big Boss doesn’t have to find out.”

    Carol’s pulse jumps into her throat and if she weren’t already hot enough, now she definitely is. She pictures herself slinking into Zosia’s passenger seat and how close they would be in that confined space (even though it’s bigger than hers), and how their elbows might brush together on the centre console every now and then while they fight over the radio controls.

    It’s ridiculous.

    “Who says you have better snacks?” is all Carol can feebly manage. “And you chew too loud.”

    Zosia scoffs. “I do not. You can’t even hear me chew.”

    “Yes I can. What are you eating? Kibble?”

    Zosia’s scoff turns into a laugh, a soft amused sound and it makes something in Carol’s chest loosen, despite herself.

    “Wow, your powers of perception are truly unmatched, Detective Sturka.” Zosia’s voice drops half a register, but the smile is still in it. “Imagine what you could accomplish if you harnessed them on something other than finding new reasons to hate me.”
