    Maybe not Zosia’s intention but the word hangs heavy between them.

    Hate.

    Carol quickly picks up her binoculars and scans across the motel, like she’s spotted something. She can feel Zosia’s eyes on her and her chest tightens again.

    “I don’t hate you,” she says, hushed, before she can tether the words.

    “You sure you don’t have a list of reasons in that notebook I always see you scribbling in?” Zosia’s tone is halfway hesitant, like she’d been working up the courage to say something and the only way she knows how to is through sarcasm. “I know I can overstep sometimes.”

    Carol doesn’t expect that. At all. Zosia swaggers all over the office like no one can touch her; confidence so bright it’s practically reflective so the idea that Zosia might actually feel vulnerable or humbled or need some kind of validation doesn’t fit the shape that Carol had assigned to her.

    “What’s with your obsession with lists?” Carol asks pointedly. And then softer, “and no I don’t have one.”

    “A spreadsheet then,” Zosia says. “A colour-coded post-it note system. A detailed bullet journal—“

    “Stop.”

    Silence crackles over the airwaves. Then there’s a rustle, like Zosia is shifting around in her seat. Carol can hear her own heart beating in her ears.

    “Look, I just—“ Zosia’s voice is soft again, only this time it has lost its usual polish.

    “No, seriously, stop,” Carol interrupts. “Look. The Civic. No plates.”

    Quickly and at the same time, they both sit upright and grab their binoculars.

    A man steps out of the car.

    Zosia grabs the radio mic and flicks to the public channel. “Male, black hoodie, blue sweatpants,” Zosia says, her voice snapping back to business. “White sneakers, black duffel bag, looks empty. I can’t see his face. Time 16:09.”

    Carol’s pen moves in overtime. “Got it.”

    “No plates,” Zosia continues, “but driver side front bumper is broken and driver side back passenger window is missing tint, duct tape around the edges.”

    They watch in unison as the man runs up the motel stairs to the second floor and enter room 2D. He’s inside for all of two minutes before racing back down the stairs with his duffel bag looking fuller now. He paces anxiously around the Civic without actually getting in and that’s when a black Chevy Silverado speeds into the motel parking lot.

    Carol continues to scribble her notes while Zosia continues to radio the details to the other units nearby. Through the open window she can hear the consistent shutter of Zosia’s camera.

    “New Mexico plates,” Zosia reads quickly. “L-P-K…one…four…nine. Windows are too tinted.”

    The truck stops only for a second. Enough time for the man to toss the duffel bag into the truck bed and then hop in after it.

    “Heading south on I-25,” Zosia concludes, breathless from the rush.

    “Copy unit two,” Manousos’ voice comes through the radio. “Unit three tailing. Stay put. White Jeep Cherokee with male description fitting Varga spotted just outside of Santa Fe. Might be heading your direction soon.”

    Soon. Carol hates that. In detective talk, that could still mean hours.

    “Copy,” Zosia breathes. She switches back to their shared channel. “Did you get everything?” she asks Carol.

    The adrenaline spike fades and Carol leans back into her seat. It leaves her buzzing and hollow all at once, her hand shaking more than she’d care to admit.

    “Every detail,” Carol confirms.

    “Good.”

    Carol lets the static of the radio and her heartbeat settle back down. She anxiously waits to see if Zosia might continue with what she had started saying earlier.

    Nothing comes and for the first time today, the silence eats away at her.

    By five o’clock, the sun begins to dip behind the mountains colouring the sky orange with wisps of white clouds. The streetlights start to cast long shadows stretching tall across the road. Still, the heat pushes against the car windows, begging to come in.

    Carol’s stomach grumbles loudly—the peanuts and jerky did not, in fact, hold her over. Her mental stamina begins to wear thin but she would never tell that to Zosia, who also looks like she’s beginning to fade. Carol watches her for a moment and the way the sunlight colours her golden.

    “Zosia,” Carol says with some conflicting mixture of hesitation and confidence that the adrenaline rush had left her. Zosia doesn’t answer, just looks to her lazily from across the way, eyes locking through the open windows. Carol completely begrudges the next words out of her mouth. “I’m…coming over.”

    A lopsided smile stretches across Zosia’s mouth and Carol hears the doors of the Explorer unlock.

    +

    The cool air hits Carol like a ton of bricks in the best way possible. She throws her stuff into the back then quickly settles into the leather seat as the quiet hum of the idling vehicle settles between them. She supposes the department splurges on you when you’re a hot shot from Las Vegas.

    It smells nice in here, fresh, unlike Carol’s Charger. There’s a vague new car smell, but it’s mostly just Zosia’s perfume, mixed with the smell of something sweet and citrusy, maybe a snack or candy that she had been eating.

    Zosia looks more than pleased at herself that Carol has finally caved, while Carol tries to convince herself that this move was solely for practical reasons—global warming, engine emissions, shared resources, blah blah blah.

    And not because she has a sudden interest in what Zosia might have to say.

    Or that somewhere, somehow, something has shifted since yesterday.

    Now the space between them feels charged, like the air before a rainstorm over the Sandias, like it did last night.

    “Here,” Zosia hands Carol a muffin wrapped tightly in cling wrap. Her eyes have not left Carol since the moment she entered the car. “I could hear your stomach on the radio. I told you I had the better snacks.”

    Carol fights both the blush in her cheeks and the urge to roll her eyes, but she doesn’t argue; gladly takes the muffin. “So now do you believe I could hear you chewing?”

    Zosia lets out a small, disgruntled laugh and rolls her eyes back, as if Carol being able to hear her chew is the worst thing that could happen. “I hope you’re not allergic to blueberries.”

    A moment passes while Carol indulges in the muffin, letting it settle her hunger. She distractedly watches the motel while Zosia answers a phone call from the Chief, who asks for updates.

    Then, maybe because Carol had manifested it, their arms do brush together on the centre console as Zosia reaches up to adjust her rearview mirror and Carol tries to get comfortable. The contact is warm and flush and it lingers for way too long; the goosebumps that erupt on Carol’s arm with little to no warning give everything away.

    “Cozy?” Zosia flicks her eyes up to keep watch in the mirror of the suspicious looking man who exits the dry cleaners. Her voice is steady, but the corner of her mouth twitches upwards.

    “Shut up,” Carol says with the last bits of muffin in her mouth, but there’s no malice in it. She quickly pulls her notebook and pen onto her lap and sets her binoculars on the dashboard.

    They sit quietly like this for a stretch. Watching, logging, snacking. Zosia really does have the better snacks, Carol particularly enjoying the chocolate covered dried mangoes.

    Then, another time their arms brush together again as Carol readjusts herself in her seat. Only this time, she pulls away quickly, unable to meet Zosia’s gaze but it still sends a shockwave of electricity through her body. She tucks her hands under her thighs and sits ramrod straight, her knee bouncing up and down.

    “Are you always this tense on stakeouts?” Zosia asks after too much silence has passed.

    Carol looks to her and then back to the motel. “Why don’t you ask Manny?”

    “I’m sure he gets a different side of you.”

    Carol’s not sure what Zosia means by that—she can probably infer—but she isn’t sure that she wants to know either.

    “He doesn’t talk as much as you.”

    “I haven’t said anything in almost…ten minutes.” Zosia tilts her head towards Carol waiting for her to look over again.

    Carol does. “You’re talking now.”

    In a movie this is where they would kiss to shut each other up, she thinks. Zosia, with her long arms would reach over and grab Carol by the back of the neck with one hand and guide their mouths together, the other arm balancing on the centre console for support. Carol would feign surprise but then melt into it, all breathy and desperate, hands grabbing at the fabric of Zosia’s shirt, pulling her as close as she can. Then one thing would lead to another and they’d find themselves on top of each other in the back seat.

    But this isn’t a movie.

    This is work.

    From this close she can see the fine lines in the corners of Zosia’s eyes, the kind you get from laughing or squinting into the desert sun too much. Or the kind you get when maybe life hasn’t given you an easy journey.

    The eye contact lingers, makes her feel tingly.

    “You’re thinking too loud,” Zosia says, slowly peeling her gaze away. “I can hear the gears turning.”

    Carol snorts, face warm. She refocuses and flips a page in her notebook that she hadn’t actually been writing in. The audacity of Zosia to think she actually knows her. Bullshit.

    “And what exactly does the great Lieutenant Zosia Nowak think I’m thinking?”

    Zosia shrugs nonchalantly. “That maybe you like me more than you let on.” Her gaze, now full of heat, flickers back to Carol who is staring blankly at her notebook. Carol doesn’t need to see it to feel it. “That you’re wondering if I’m as arrogant as I seem.”

    Heat crawls up Carol’s neck and her ears flare red, heart beating in her throat. She meets Zosia’s gaze again, chin raising, equal fervor returned.

    “You’re not as mysterious as you think.” It’s an outright lie.

    “Am I wrong?”

    The question hangs there, simple and loaded. Outside a lowrider cruises past them, bass thumping loudly through the ground. Carol’s pulse matches the beat.

    “No,” she finally manages, quieter than she means to. “You’re not wrong.”

    Zosia’s breath hitches, barely audible, but Carol sees the way her hands tighten in her lap, and again she notices the tan line on her ring finger. Then Zosia exhales, slow and controlled, like she’s reigning herself in.

    “Good to know.”

    “Don’t get cocky.”

    “Too late.” Zosia’s grin is softer now, her eyes edged with something real.

    A small commotion in the motel parking lot pulls them out of the moment, reminding them that they’re still on the job. Carol lifts her binoculars up, watching as two young women, clearly under the influence stumble around, presumably waiting for a ride.

    She jots it down in her notebook anyways, though her focus suddenly feels frayed at the edges, unmoored from its usual steadiness. It’s been pulled off course by the echo of her last words with Zosia. By the way it stirs something tender and uncertain in her.

    +

    By seven o’clock, Carol is slumped low in the passenger seat, eyes strained and following every figure or vehicle that crosses the now dark and poorly lit motel parking lot. Nothing of interest has happened, the Honda Civic remains abandoned, and no one has radioed for what feels like an eternity. She has a feeling this stakeout is a bust.

    Her back is sore, her neck is sore, and she just can’t sit any longer. She doesn’t bother anymore to hide the fact that she’s exhausted—this has been one of the longest stakeouts she’s been on.

    Condensation starts to fog the windows, the engine having been cut once the cool had returned to the air.

    She glances over at Zosia who looks equally as worn, her pen making more scribbles in her notebook than actual words.

    “So how’s that stamina going for you?” Carol asks, the sodium glow of a streetlight flickering above them. “Can you still go all night?”

    Zosia sits upright and stretches her arms over her head, her shirt lifting up and exposing her stomach in the process. Carol can’t help but stare.

    “I’m just getting started,” Zosia says, voice tired and strained, but her grin doesn’t fade.

    “You really are insufferable.”

    Zosia quirks her mouth to say something but right on cue, as if he’d been listening, Chief Schrader’s voice crackles over the radio, gravelly with fatigue but firm as usual.

    “All units, suspect is heading eastbound on I-40. Opposite direction, soon to be out of our jurisdiction. Nightshift will circle back periodically to the motel. Stakeout over, everyone go home and get some rest.”

    The words ring like music in Carol’s ears as she exhales long and hard, leaning back into her seat. Her body feels like one solid knot untangling itself.

    Next to her, Zosia lets out a low whistle, allowing a rare flicker of relief to cross her face. “Thought we’d be here until tomorrow,” she says, rubbing at the bridge of her nose. As much as she tries to hide it, there’s fatigue in her eyes.

    “Mhm,” Carol hums in agreement. “Feels weird to stop.”

    Zosia smiles, small and crooked, and reaches over Carol’s lap and flicks open the glove compartment.

    “Smoke?”

    “You’re a bad influence.”

    “You can always say no.”

    +

    Outside, leaned up against the hood of the Explorer, Carol and Zosia smoke in silence, the weight of the day having taken its toll. They stand close, but not touching even if the space between them begs for it. Together, they watch as the cars zip by them on the road, whispers of nicotine billowing from their mouths.

    “We survived the day without killing each other,” Carol says after a while.

    Zosia smiles. “Did you really think we would?”

    “Absolutely, yes.”

    “And now?”

    Carol blows a puff of smoke in Zosia’s direction. “Jury’s still out.”

    Zosia wafts it away. “Asshole.”

    For the first time, they laugh together and something about that makes Carol feel like she’s misjudged Zosia.

    “Hey,” Zosia says a few minutes later, halfway through her cigarette. “I don’t know how permanent Schrader is going to make this but if we’re going to be working this closely together, there’s something I want to tell you and I need you to just listen.”

    There’s a slight but noticeable shift in her tone that Carol thinks she recognizes from yesterday and it makes her feel uneasy, but silently, she urges Zosia on.

    “My son, Aleksander, would have turned eighteen yesterday.” Zosia says it point blank, no hesitation, like it’s been desperate to spill off her tongue.

    Would have. It feels like a blow to Carol’s chest. Sudden and unexpected, she doesn’t think the silence that follows could feel this heavy, but it does.

    “He died twelve years ago.” Zosia’s voice now etched with the tenderest of aches. “He was six. Car accident. I was driving.”

    The words land between them with a dull, impossible finality. Carol turns to Zosia whose face is all too calm, like she’s had to tell this story one too many times. She stares at something across the street, her mouth curving, but it’s not quite a smile, not quite a frown. Something in-between. Pensive.

    The stillness that follows doesn’t invite questions so Carol continues to listen.

    “I don’t cry anymore,” Zosia goes on softly. “I did for so long and eventually you just…run out. The body can’t keep doing it forever.” Her voice doesn’t waver and she keeps her gazed trained on something in the distance.

    Carol hates that she understands more than she should. She feels a slow and painful tightening in her chest. The admission opens a familiar hollow—one she’s carried from almost ten years ago when her world had also split apart on a day that had no right to take what it did.

    She wants to say something, anything, but every sentence she tries to form collapses under the weight of something they can’t undo.

    “I still dream of him,” Zosia continues. “He’s always six. Always laughing, always the age he was right before it happened.” Her voice stays strong but her hand trembles as she brings the cigarette back up to her mouth. “Then I wake up and it’s quiet again.”

    Carol looks at her, at the worn steadiness of her face, and then sees something that she does recognize: the exhaustion of surviving something that should have ended you. And that behind Zosia’s bravado, there is simply a person who has asked very little from the world and yet carries the burden of it.

    “The quiet is the worst,” Carol finally says before she can leash the words. “It makes you hear everything you've lost.”

    Zosia looks at Carol and nods, her eyes soft with a recognition that feels almost like a touch. “People tell you it gets easier, but it just gets quieter. You learn to live around it.“

    “Or sit on the edge of it with someone,” Carol adds.

    Something unreadable and warm flickers between their gazes. Zosia shifts, closing that last inch of space between them until their shoulders press together. The contact is light is tentative, but it’s also solid and human.

    “Maybe that’s the only way,” Zosia offers. “Not alone.”

    Carol’s pulse thrums at the base of her throat. Not just from their conversation (or any of the conversations they’ve had today), but from the simple fact of Zosia’s weight against her arm. It’s decisive; then something comes over Carol. She lets her hand move, slow and unobtrusive, until the backs of their fingers brush together.

    The spark that ignites between them is undeniable. Zosia doesn’t pull away. Instead, she turns her hand, just slightly, fingers curling just enough to fit against Carol’s. No, not holding hands, no big gesture, just the deliberate contact of their hands together—an agreement to simply touch.

    An understanding.

    They stay like that for a while, until the embers of their cigarettes burn out, until the sun completely disappears behind the mountains and nestles them in the moonlight.

    “Are you hungry?” Zosia asks after the gravity of the moment lifts. “For real food.”

    Carol hadn’t thought about it for a while, the cigarette and emotions suppressing her appetite.

    “Starving, actually,” she admits. “I need a coffee…or a drink.”

    Zosia grins. “I saw a little dive bar not too far from here.”

    “Dave’s Dive,” Carol says too quickly, as if she’s not familiar with the New Mexico bar scene. “They have a decent menu,” she says again, more casual this time.

    “Spoken like a true local. Meet you there?”

    If anyone had asked Carol this morning how she envisioned this day going and ending, having dinner with Zosia would not have made the list.

    “Yeah,” Carol nods, “meet you there.”

    *****

    The second time Carol sees Zosia outside of work, it’s on purpose.

    Well, she supposes this is outside of work, since they’re now technically off the clock.

    The door to the bar swings shut behind Carol, the tired jangle of the bell ringing in her wake. The air inside is thick—beer and aged wood mixed with the smell of something delicious from the kitchen. Neon lights buzz along the wall, tinting the cracked vinyl booths in yellow and blue. The jukebox in the corner plays The Goo Goo Dolls—only half the room seems to notice.

    It’s mostly empty—expected for a Tuesday night—filled with what Carol presumes are regulars hunched over their drinks at the bar and a few other people and couples scattered throughout. The bartender polishes the same glass over and over like he has nowhere else to be.

    It doesn’t take long for Carol to find Zosia who had arrived first, nestled into a tiny corner booth at the back of the bar. Their eyes meet before Carol reaches the table.

    “Jeez, hiding from the Cartel?” Carol says, sliding into the booth, perpendicular to Zosia. Their knees accidentally knock under the table.

    “Something like that,” Zosia shrugs. “Just wanted some privacy.”

    Carol clenches her jaw. She runs her fingers over the well worn grooves of the table. If Zosia had been any other woman in Albuquerque, or any other woman in the world, she would have already tried to make a move, but she doesn’t. She can’t.

    “So, drink?” Carol asks, as if she’s forgotten the reason they came here. “First round’s on me. I owe you, remember?”

    Zosia smirks. “Haven’t forgotten.”

    “Requests?”

    “Dealer’s choice.”

    Carol returns from the bar sometime later after having to fight for the attention of the bartender who seemed more interested in watching the game on the TV. She holds two sweating pint glasses, the lights from the jukebox colouring the foam pink.

    “Special delivery,” Carol says, setting the glasses onto the table and sliding back into the booth. They immediately form a ring of condensation.

    Zosia perks up and tucks her phone away, out of sight. “Thank you,” she says, picking up a glass and holding it out to Carol. Carol picks the other glass up and they clink, eyes holding together.

    Carol takes a long sip, like it’s water and she’s been out in the desert all day and watches the way as Zosia does the same, only Zosia’s face wrinkles with apprehension.

    Zosia raises an eyebrow at her, dubious. “What is this?”

    “It’s beer,” Carol says, affronted. “It’s cold. It has bubbles. What more could you want?”

    “Sounds like you’re describing champagne,” Zosia says, tapping her finger on the side of the glass like she’s checking the ripeness of a melon. “Maybe something that’s seen a hop in its lifetime?” She looks to Carol with an amused smile. “This is like…witness protection beer.” Carol blinks at her. “Beer that’s in hiding,” Zosia confirms.

    Carol’s mouth hangs open in disbelief. “First of all, it’s PBR. Second of all, you said dealer’s choice.” She takes another sip in solidarity with her choice.

    It makes Zosia laugh, who lifts her own pint in compliance, sipping slow, eyes locked onto Carol’s. She grimaces, then smiles. “It’s fine,” she tries. “Tastes like…college.”

    “I thought ‘fine’ was what airplane food was,” Carol snarks back, nudging Zosia’s knee under the table. “And I had a great time in college so I’m not sure what you’re complaining about.”

    “I’m starting to get an idea of what the coffee in your yellow mug tastes like.”

    Carol suppresses another eye roll. “Keep drinking it,” she says, “it’ll grow on you.”

    “So does mould.”

    Carol purses her lips but it’s only met by Zosia nudging her knee back.

    “Next round is on you, then.”

    +

    The food comes quick after ordering it. Carol watches the way Zosia spears a fry with a fork before dipping it into the ketchup with exaggerated care and popping it into her mouth.

    “This definitely beats my better snacks,” Zosia says, eyes on Carol.

    Carol half smirks, half shakes her head, picking a fry out of the basket with her fingers. “Barely,” she quips. “I guess at least the company tonight is…tolerable.”

    Zosia snorts, reaching for her beer, which she almost looks like she’s enjoying now. “Tolerable? That’s high praise from you. Should I get that embroidered on a pillow?”

    Carol scoffs for the umpteenth time today. “Only if it comes with a muzzle.”

    “Oh, please,” Zosia sits back, arms crossed, in mock offence. “If it weren’t for me tonight, you’d be at home doing what? Alphabetizing your spice rack?”

    Carol lets out an accidentally too loud ha. “Spice rack? That’s your big detective insight? What next? I knit sweaters for my gun?”

    Zosia quirks up an eyebrow. “I wouldn’t be surprised.”

    When Zosia leans forward, her long fingers tracing up and down the condensation of her glass, Carol notices it again—the pale indentation circling her ring finger, the ghost of where something heavy used to sit. She looks away, but not fast enough.

    “You can just ask, you know,” Zosia says.

    Carol blinks, feigning innocence. “Ask what?”

    Zosia flashes her a knowing look. “About this.” She lifts her left hand, wiggling her ring finger in the air. “I know you’ve noticed."

    Guilty. Carol lets out a strangled breath. “It’s not any of my business.”

    “It’s fine,” Zosia says, picking up her beer and staring into it like maybe she’d find some courage at the bottom. “My fiancée, she cheated on me. We were supposed to get married a few months ago.”

    “Oh.” Carol watches her and the way her eyes go soft at the edges again. “Sorry,” she mutters.

    Zosia shrugs, her shoulders tensing before relaxing. “I’m not. Anymore, that is. I was unhappy long before it even happened so it was for the best.”

    Carol nods, but her thoughts are about a mile ahead of her, too far to catch.

    She. Zosia said she. It’s stupid, maybe, but the word folds itself over and over in her head. She’s not entirely surprised by that given everything that’s led to the moment they’ve found themselves in now. And especially because Carol has never met a straight woman in her life that has ever exuded the same energy as Lieutenant Zosia Nowak.

    But to hear it out loud finally gives Carol the validation that none of this has been in her head.

    Zosia leans in close to make herself heard over the sudden change in music, her breath faint against Carol’s cheek. “You’re doing it again.”

    “Huh?” Carol quickly shoves another fry into her mouth as a distraction.

    “Thinking too loud.”

    “Oh, sorry.” She swallows. “I, um, just wasn’t expecting that.”

    “The cheating?”

    “Sure, that too.” Carol smiles crookedly.

    Zosia lets out an amused smile. “Don’t tell me you’re actually surprised by that.”

    Now Carol feels embarrassed, but only slightly. She throws back the rest of her beer.

    +

    “Can I ask you something?” Zosia says when the music changes to something quieter, slower, less bass. Her voice is deliberately gentle now, almost cautious, like she’s turned the volume down on herself too.

    “Sure?”

    “Have I ever crossed a line? With you?”

    Carol’s fingers still on her refilled pint glass, which had been tapping to the beat of the music. “What do you mean?” she asks, too careful.

    Zosia takes a long sip, almost unable to meet Carol’s gaze for once. “Today, yesterday, any other day, really. I know I like to—“ she pauses, takes a breath, “—tease or banter or whatever, but maybe you’re not really into that. And I just thought—maybe you drove here wishing you hadn’t. Or maybe you thought I told you about my son just for some sympathy points.”

    Carol tucks her hands into her lap, nervously picking at the skin around her thumb. She stays quiet for a minute, actually allowing herself to process Zosia’s words. The sincerity of the question deserves the same grace. She likes this vulnerable Zosia, she thinks, it continues to humanize her after all the dehumanizing Carol’s done to her in her mind.

    “I didn’t regret driving here,” Carol finally says. “And I definitely don’t think the second thing either.”

    “Okay,” Zosia breathes, “good start.”

    Now Carol takes a breath, long and slow. She blames the alcohol for what she’s about to say because the words come out faster than she can stop herself. “I drove here and tried not to think about the way you’ve been looking at me.” It feels like she’s stepping off a ledge, the wind about to be knocked out of her.

    Silence. Sharp and stunned.

    “Oh,” Zosia says, small and wondering, like a new piece of evidence had been offered to her. “I was trying, or hoping not to make you uncomfortable.”

    “I was uncomfortable,” Carol admits. “Just not for the reasons you think.”

    The music thumps like muffled background noise, like the only thing they can hear is each other.

    Zosia lets out a breath that sounds like it’s been sitting in her chest for days and then stares at Carol, unrelenting. “You’re really bad for my ego, you know that?”

    Carol’s eyes widen, bewildered. “How?” Her body starts to tingle again and she tugs at her collar of her shirt as if it has suddenly become too tight.

    “Because I spent the longest time thinking you hated me. And then I thought I had misread that. Then you say something like that and my brain is just…static.”

    Carol wishes she had a stronger drink. “Maybe the feeling is mutual.”

    “Maybe?”

    She stares back at Zosia with the same intensity and watches her cheeks flush bright red before her, even through the neon glow. A tiny smile threatens at Zosia’s mouth, not a smirk, but something smaller and a little bit more dangerous.

    “For the record,” Zosia says slowly but brazenly, “I was flirting. I’ve been flirting. Incase that wasn’t clear through all the…banter.”

    “Oh, it was clear,” Carol says, her heart beating so fast it might burst out of her chest. “That’s kind of the problem.”

    “Why is it a problem?” Zosia’s tone is careful, no pressure in it, just a simple question hanging in the hot air between them.

    “I mean, other than the fact that we work together?” Carol says. “Because you’re right. Just last week I couldn’t stand you and now here I am, sitting next to you in this tiny ass booth deciding if I misjudged you or if my judgement just…sucks.”

    Zosia goes quiet for a long moment, pushing her pint glass out of the way. She places her arms on the table, fingers interlaced, and leans in closer to Carol, her knuckles just a hair away from Carol’s elbow, which also rests atop the table.

    “You didn’t misjudge me,” Zosia says. “I am arrogant. I am annoying. I do talk to much.”

    “You forgot messy and presumptuous.”

    “See? Unmatched perception.” There’s a ghost of humour in it, but underneath, something raw. “It’s just that I like working with you even if it hasn’t come across that way. You’re sharp. You care. You see things other people miss. And me? I just run my mouth when—“ Zosia stops like she’s thinking about what to say. And then, “Do you remember when I told you you weren’t what I was expecting?”

    Carol leans in, her elbow making contact with Zosia’s hands. “Yes, and do you finally have a real answer for me?”

    “I wasn’t expecting to be so intrigued by you.” Zosia says it almost to herself, the words slipping out like a confession.

    Carol’s breath gets caught somewhere in her chest and throat and she can’t contain the fluster that spreads across her face.

    “Well, that makes the both of us.”

    A server coming to clear the dishes from their tables interrupts the moment, the mood immediately shifting to something lighter. The music becomes loud again, Carol too aware of every clink and clank and voice around her.

    “Sorry ladies,” the server says, reaching in-between them for the empty bottles. “We’re closing up early tonight, do you mind settling up at the bar?”

    Carol sits back, running a hand through her hair. Cool air rushes against the outside of her thigh where Zosia’s knee had been pressed into since their second beer.

    As she walks up to the bar, Zosia trailing somewhere behind her, Carol’s body buzzes with the promise of chaos.

    It buzzes until they get outside where they lean up against their cars, parked side by side. Carol leans on her driver side door, Zosia on her passenger side.

    “Whatever just happened in there doesn’t change the job,” Carol says, though it’s more to herself than anything. “It can’t.”

    “Right, of course not,” Zosia answers almost too quickly. “I would never expect it to.”

    Carol nods, eyes to the ground, then back up. There’s a brief moment that follows where neither of them actually knows how to end the night; where they just stand in silence looking at each other. Zosia looks different now, Carol thinks. Less her hot Lieutenant that she wants to hate-fuck; more a person she wants to learn.

    But also fuck. Or kiss. Or whatever.

    She rummages in her pocket for her car keys. The Charger beeps unlocked. “Guess I’ll see you tomorrow then, Lieutenant,” she says, trapped in Zosia’s gaze. She’s just so tall.

    Zosia grins, eyes hooded in the darkness. She gives Carol the same two finger salute she had once given her. “See you tomorrow, Detective Sturka.”

    In her car, Carol waits as Zosia drives off first. She reverses quick out of the parking spot, and Carol watches through her rearview mirror as her tail lights disappear down the street with her.

    Carol doesn’t leave immediately. She can’t; her body and her brain are functioning at two different speeds and it feels safer to just let her thoughts unravel in the dark.

    Zosia’s voice keeps replaying in the quiet. Each time a measured calm before saying something risky.

    Maybe you like me more than you let on.

    I was flirting.

    I wasn’t expecting to be so intrigued by you.

    Carol’s head feels like its spinning out of control and it’s not from the booze. Everything she had conceived Zosia to be now rearranges itself on a shelf in her mind and it makes her feel like she’s discovering something new about her own self, which at the age of 53 terrifies her.

    The sound of a loud bang makes Carol flinch. She peers into her mirror, heart racing—just a dumpster lid being shut—but her pulse doesn’t settle right away.

    She turns the key in her ignition, headlights cutting through the gravel haze, and finally pulls out. The road ahead looks the same as always—empty, still, waiting.

    But Carol knows better.

    This is just the calm before the storm.

     

    Chapter End Notes

    This chapter is nearly 10k words long and respectfully, I will never be fucking doing that again lol.

    But as always, love you all for reading/liking/commenting. It gives me the willpower to write more.

    Come be my friend on twitter @nutmeg_101

    Chapter 5: cosmic balance sheets &amp; improvised medicine

    Chapter Summary

    Zosia yearns. Carol burns. It's all fine.

    Chapter Notes

    The morning light falls through the kitchen windows at a slant, the same way it always does when Carol makes her coffee. Dust motes drift lazily, suspended in the honeyed glow of the sunbeams and she can almost believe that time hasn’t done what it has—that in exactly one month, the calendar won’t have turned ten whole years since Helen’s laughter had last filled this home.

    Carol sets out two mugs on purpose, mismatched ones; the green striped one that Helen had loved and her own blue one, chipped at the bottom from years of use. The sight of them together tugs at something deep inside her chest, like a bruise that has never fully healed and might not ever. Then she hesitates for a moment before putting Helen’s back in the cupboard, handle facing left, just like Helen had liked it.

    She remembers one of the last afternoons they had danced in here, barefoot on the cold tile while the record player in the living room crooned Ray Charles. Helen’s hair had smelled faintly of shampoo and rain as she spun, laughing at how uncoordinated Carol had been.

    They had been too naive, unaware of what the future held, to realize how perfect that moment had been—two mugs of tea cooling on the counter, a storm gently tapping at the windows, and a love that felt impossible to lose.

    Now when Carol thinks of that day, she tries to hold onto the sound of Helen’s laugh instead of the silence that follows it. The laughter is fading around the edges, soft as smoke. Sometimes she replays it just to make sure it’s still there, tucked away in the corners of her memory where even time has to tread lightly.

    The coffee machine beeps, sharp and sudden. Carol turns it off and fills only one cup. The quiet that surrounds her feels both cruel and sacred, like the echo of footsteps fading into time.

    She lifts the mug, inhales the steam, and whispers into it, “I miss you.” It’s not an answer, but it’s enough to make her chest ache in a familiar way—a way that means she still remembers.

    But lately, remembering doesn’t feel so simple anymore.

    The ache is knotted with something else, something alive and restless. Almost five days in a row now she has opened her eyes thinking of Zosia. Not Helen’s smile or the way it felt to be held by her. But instead, Zosia’s sharp grin, her deep brown eyes, and the way even the softest touch from her feels like an entire electrical current coursing through her body.

    Carol can’t remember the last time, if at all, she had felt like this since Helen. The flicker, the pull, the sudden awareness of being seen. Zosia is still so new to the team, to her life—too bold by half—and yet somehow, after one single stakeout and dinner, has been able to draw the softness out of her.

    Carol presses her palms against the countertop, granite cool beneath her skin, forcing slow and steady breaths. She feels the guilt nag anyways, hot and unreasonable, curling low in her stomach.

    How dare she think of someone else when Helen’s memory is still woven into every breath she takes, every mug in the kitchen.

    And yet, the thought of Zosia flutters loudly in her mind—a sound that refuses to be silenced.

    Carol takes a sip of her coffee, now bitter and lukewarm. The room feels too quiet again, too full of ghosts. She tells herself it’s nothing, just another lonely morning’s trick on her heart. But when she closes her eyes, it’s not Helen’s laugh that lingers, it’s the sound of Zosia’s voice, soft and steady.

    It leaves her feeling unbound, suspended somewhere between guilt and wanting. And for the first time in nearly a decade, Carol longs to desire.

    *****

    Carol is one of the first people to arrive to the office. She rides the elevator in silence, counting each ding until she reaches ten. The bullpen is almost empty during the turnover from the night shift to the day shift, just the sound of the photocopier wheezing from down the hall. Good. She likes it like this, though her sleep patterns usually deprive her of such bliss. But today it gives her more time to settle her bearings before Zosia (and everyone else) arrives.

    She reminds herself of last night and that this is work. Her goddamn career.

    Behave like it.

    She trods into her office and slings her bag and blazer onto the coat rack crammed behind the door. Her desk looks the same as always. Neatly stacked reports, pens of every colour sprouting out the pen holder she had stolen from Val’s desk, and the picture of her and Helen from their last vacation in Mexico, sun-kissed and in love.

    There is comfort in the ordinary. It gives her structure. She adjusts a stray corner of paper, checks the time, and tells herself this is just fine. Her routine is her armour; worn it all her life.

    “Oh, so she is alive.”

    Carol looks over from her computer to see Manousos standing against the door frame, arms crossed, eyes narrowed. She narrows her eyes back but then remembers the missed call notification she had seen on her phone last night followed by the voicemail which she subsequently also forgotten to listen to when she got home.

    The look on his face tells her she’s about to get an earful.

    “Shit. Manny. I…got sidetracked last night.”

    Manousos narrows his eyes further and plops himself down on the chair across Carol’s desk with flourish. His gaze grows more questioning.

    “You do one stakeout without me and suddenly you can’t pick up a phone call? I thought—I don’t know what I thought, actually.”

    Thought she was neck deep into a bottle of vodka? Thought she had passed out crying under Helen’s tree in the backyard again? The list goes on.

    “It was one phone call, Manny. I’m sorry.”

    “Carol Sturka. You always pick up so when you don’t pick up, I worry.”

    Carol stifles a groan. He’s right. Manousos knows her in a way that most people don’t. He had barely known her when Helen died and yet somehow still managed to pick up the pieces for her even though that hadn’t been his responsibility. So the very fucking least Carol can do is answer a damn phone call. It’s the thing she hates and loves the most about him.

    “I know, amigo,” she says, reaching across the desk to give his hand a reassuring squeeze. “Won’t happen again.”

    “Better not.” Manousos leans back in his chair and folds his arms. “So are you going to tell me what happened yesterday then? Or are you going to make me pry it out of you?”

    Carol half shrugs and vaguely waves her hands in the air before spinning in her chair to face her computer in an attempt to hide the way her expression changes. It’s feeble.

    “Nothing happened. It was fine. She’s…not that bad.”

    Manousos scoffs but not without vindication. “I told you all you needed was time. So what happened?”

    “Why do you keep asking what happened? Nothing happened. It was a stakeout. We sat together and—“

    “You sat together even though Schrader said not to.” It’s not a question and Carol doesn’t need to look at Manousos to know he’s smirking.

    “It was like a nine hour stakeout, Manny, what did you expect us to do? We got…bored.”

    “If you say so. But I know from the fact that you can’t look at me that you are hiding something and I will find out,” he says, standing up and edging back towards the door.

    Carol doesn’t doubt that. Manousos has an uncanny ability to read her like a book—or maybe she’s just not that subtle. It’s definitely the latter. Now Carol looks at him, a tell tale crease between her brows.

    “You know what? You’re just as annoying as she is.”

    Manousos waves a hand. “I hardly doubt that. And by the way, ‘not that bad’ is how you described that tuna sandwich you brought for lunch last week.”

    Carol huffs, throwing a crumpled up receipt at him.

    “Get out.”

    Manousos simply taps the doorframe as he exits, his smugness radiating. “See you in the briefing room in fifteen minutes,” he says, voice trailing away with him down the hall.

    +

    The briefing room smells of lemon scented disinfectant and dry erase markers, the same as it does every morning. There’s chatter as always, Manousos and Koumba cracking jokes about things that Carol can’t be bothered to concern herself with, and everyone else in their own bubbles.

    Carol shimmies into her seat that is tucked uncomfortably close and in-between Margaret and Rick, who almost looks like he had forgotten he was supposed to come to work today. Hair disheveled, a single shirt button misaligned. She sets her folder and notebook onto the table, telling herself that today is just another day. Another meeting.

    Only today, the room feels smaller and tighter as Carol anticipates the arrival of Zosia.

    Carol hears her before she sees her; even without her heels the rhythm of her boots against the hard floor is unmistakable.

    Then the door opens.

    Zosia arrives exactly on time and not one minute later. Crisp white button-down tucked neatly into her pants, hair pulled back today like she means business, and a set of small diamond earrings and red lipstick that Carol has never seen her adorn before.

    Oh for fuck’s sake.

    Carol shifts around in her seat; it’s suddenly much warmer in the room. Zosia hasn’t noticed her yet, her eyes still cool, expression unreadable. It doesn’t stop the Chief from beaming from ear to ear as if his prodigal child, here to save the day, has descended from the heavens. Something about that, despite everything that’s happened in the last few days, makes Carol laugh wryly to herself and roll her eyes.

    It’s no wonder Zosia walks around here the way that she does.

    Then, finally, after Zosia settles into her seat at the opposite end of the table, she looks up and whether it’s on purpose or not, traps Carol in her gaze. Carol stills like a deer in headlights, as if she had somehow expected to not look at her new partner at least one time today. The uptick in Zosia’s mouth and the glint in her eyes might go quiet and unnoticed by anyone else in the room, but to Carol, it’s the loudest sound in the room.

    ‘Morning, partner,’ she mouths from across the way.

    Carol feels like her head is going to orbit into space.

    Quickly, she flashes a soft smile back telling herself to not make things weird. Then she shoots her eyes down at her notebook, pretending to write something. Though her pen stays perfectly in control, her face might tell a different story.

    The chief finally begins talking, his voice low and monotonous over the hum in Carol’s chest. She can feel Zosia across the room, her presence like heat penetrating through glass. When the Chief’s droning and mumbles becomes too much, she risks a glance at Zosia who, unsurprisingly, is already looking at her. It lasts only a second, maybe two, but Carol is suddenly all too aware of prying eyes.

    Stay professional. No distractions.

    When she finally looks away and across from her, Manousos has his arms folded again, overly relaxed in his chair with a stupid fucking grin on his face like he’s watching a movie only he understands. Carol tries to subdue the fluster by clearing her throat and kicking him under the table.

    He grunts.

    “Something to add, Manny?” The Chief asks, looking over at him.

    Manousos shakes his head. “No sir. Just paying attention.”

    “Carol?”

    Carol freezes like a kid whose been tattled in class for talking too much even though she hasn’t said anything. She promises herself she had been listening but as such, it evokes a challenge in her because she’s fifty-three years old and not about to let her bald, potbellied Chief make her feel like any less. She decides exactly then, right on the spot, that maybe the team does need to know what she’s thinking.

    “Actually,” Carol states, standing from her seat and walking over to the whiteboard, confidence exuding from her every pore. She only bumps into the back of one chair. “I think we need to tighten the timeline on Peña and Varga and whoever was driving that black Chevy, because why was it never reported? There’s too big of a gap from the both times Varga had been seen in the last 48 hours so we need to recheck all logs and cross-department reports.”

    Her tone is clipped and poised as she moves around the whiteboard, tapping the relevant sections with the tip of her pen. But as she looks back over her shoulder, she catches Zosia’s gaze, bright, unwavering, and deliberate.

    The directness of it unsettles her in a way that makes her lose her train of thought and her next words catch in her throat.

    “And, um,” Carol clears her throat, “CID will look into the informant contact from the other day. Any overlap needs to be flagged immediately. And any other questions or concerns can be directed to Chief Schrader and,” she pauses and swallows, “Lieutenant Nowak.”

    Carol clicks her pen shut and promptly returns to her seat. She’s never been one to fluster at team briefings, she’s been doing them for years, knows them in her sleep. But today, under the heat of Zosia’s attention, she wobbles.

    The briefing goes on for another ten minutes while the Chief delegates cases and tasks, all the while Carol forces herself to focus on the whiteboard behind him, and not the fact that she can still feel Zosia’s eyes on her.

    When the briefing ends, chairs scrape back and papers shuffle. Carol stands, heart steady again, face neutral. She can’t quite discern if Zosia had been too audacious and obvious, or if her own nerves are making her see things that aren’t actually there. She walks towards the door without looking back until she’s in the hallway.

    It’s cooler out here, just barely, but the air feels lighter. Carol keeps her pace even with her folder and notebook tucked under her arm like a shield. She can still hear the footsteps and chatter clearing out of the briefing room and she’s about halfway to her office when Zosia calls out after her.

    “Detective Sturka.” Her voice is low and suddenly too close to Carol. “Leaving so quickly?”

    Carol stops. Every word they shared last night comes flooding back. She let’s out a small breath before slowly turning around. When their eyes meet, suddenly everything around her goes fuzzy.

    Zosia stops a few steps back, a professional amount of space between them because they aren’t alone. Her expression is steady and controlled, but there’s a faint uncertainty in her eyes, like she’s testing the rules for fractures.

    “What happened to ‘partner?’” Carol says coolly.

    “You’re still a detective, aren’t you?”

    “Depends on the time of day.” It comes out entirely more coy than the sarcasm she had been aiming for and it causes the tiniest of cracks in Zosia’s facade. Then quickly and deliberately Carol adds, “I’ll grab the case notes from Schrader and then we can head to the scene.”

    “I didn’t mean to distract you in there,” Zosia says somewhere in between a tease and an apology.

    Carol scoffs, eyes rolling dramatically. Bold of Zosia to think she would be the cause of her falter. Even if she was.

    “Well, you did.” Carol tries for confrontation but it’s weak and it comes out breathless.

    “Sorry,” Zosia says, inching just a hair closer and leaning her shoulder against the wall, her eyes sparking with amusement now, a smile ghosting at her lips. “It’s just that I like when you speak in meetings. The authority is—it suits you.”

    Everything is fine until she pauses, like she’s calculating her next words. And then softer with an uncharacteristic bashfulness, “Also, you yourself look…very distracting today.” She tugs lightly at the bottom of the sleeve of Carol’s shirt, her fingers brushing against Carol’s wrist.

    Goosebumps.

    Carol blinks. Though her pulse is frantic, she can’t help but feel a little bit smug. From the corner of her eye, she glances at her own reflection in the window next to her. The shirt she’s wearing hadn’t been an afterthought—tight in all the right places, tracing all the lines she means to—it had been chosen with the quiet certainty that one particular set of eyes would find her.

    “Zosia,” she whispers, panic setting in, glancing down the hall where voices still linger. “Don’t. We said—“

    “I know,” Zosia interrupts softly. “Job first. I remember. It’s just that you made it very hard to concentrate in there.”

    The words land with a delicate precision, the kind that threatens to unravel the professional wall Carol had built up this morning. She had not expected Zosia to come in blazing—if anything she figured Zosia might just straight up ignore her and pretend that nothing has changed.

    Now, their agreement from last night seems to hang by its edges.

    And what about you? The hair? The lipstick? Is it a show just for me?

    Carol grips her notebook tighter, eyes still darting around for anyone to walk by at any second.

    They’re not even doing anything.

    “You need to tone it down before someone hears.”

    Zosia’s expression warms. It’s not smug, but fond. “Wouldn’t want to start a rumour,” she says, stepping closer, just enough to make the air feel different. “I meant what I said, though.”

    Carol swallows and forces a steady breath. She looks at Zosia, eyes narrowed, but her prickles quickly fade away.

    “You are impossible,” she breathes.

    “Only around you.”

    All at once, bodies filter into the hallway from both directions. They each step back.

    “Meet me in the parking lot when you pull yourself together, Lieutenant,” Carol says as the stream of officers and others pass by. Without waiting for a reply she adds, “and I’m driving,” her fingers brushing against Zosia’s hips as she unclips the car keys that hang from her belt loop in one fluid motion.

    Zosia stares after her, mouth agape somewhere between disbelief and longing, the jingle of her car keys fading away with Carol’s steps.

    +

    By the time Carol and Zosia arrive to the crime scene, the street is already lined with patrol cars, blue and red lights flashing like beacons leading them the way. Garnet Street is lined with low houses and gravel yards with curious neighbours peering out from behind curtains or pretending to be doing yard work.

    The house at the very end looks sunken into itself; a porch railing missing, a bar and paint on the exterior peeling like paper. Around it, the yellow police tape flutters in the breeze.

    “Charming,” Carol says as they pull up, shifting the car into park.

    “Sure,” Zosia answers, “if you like empty take out containers and beer bottles as lawn ornaments.”

    “That’s my favourite aisle at Home Goods.”

    Zosia snorts and Carol can’t help but smile to herself.

    Outside, two officers wait by the edge of the driveway. Carol and Zosia flash their badges and introduce themselves.

    “You were first on the scene?” Zosia asks, shielding her eyes from the sun.

    “Yes, ma’am,” Officer Lewis, as confirmed by his name tag, says. “Call came in around 9:30 AM. Got here around 9:45. Neighbour noticed the front door was left open as they were leaving for work. Peaked inside to see if anyone was there and that’s when she saw the body in the living room.”

    “Interesting,” Carol says, glancing towards the porch light that still flickers weakly in the daylight. “Anyone inside when you arrived?”

    Officer Park shakes her head. “No signs. We did a sweep, just him. ID says Jason Chan, but the address doesn’t match this one.”

    Carol and Zosia exchange a look. Zoe Diaz’s boyfriend.

    Zosia pulls her gloves out. Carol watches her, that faint pull of interest growing in her eyes as she surveys the property. It’s a little thing, but it’s that no-nonsense passion for the job that makes her so attractive. Among other things.

    “Have you spoken to the neighbours?” Zosia asks.

    “Couple across the street said they heard a man yelling and glass breaking around four AM but didn’t see anything. Next door neighbour said she could hear a woman’s voice around the same time but couldn’t make out what she was saying.”

    “Okay,” Carol says firm and pointedly. “Get CSI here and started on the perimeter, block off the backyard, and we’ll get started inside.”

    On the way to the front door, Zosia softly nudges her arm into Carol. “Damn, Sturka,” she whispers. “Have you always been this authoritative or am I just now noticing?”

    Carol tries to play it off coolly but she feels the tips of her ears grow hot and herself a little taller. “First of all, that’s Detective Sturka when we’re on duty,” she warns, but there’s no bite behind it. If anything, it sounds more like a dare because Zosia’s eyes light up like a Christmas tree. “And second of all, you tell me, since you’re always watching me.” She quickens her pace and walks ahead of Zosia, but she doesn’t need to look back to know Zosia is grinning.

    They stop just outside the door to put their gloves on and Carol looks to Zosia who is in fact very much grinning.

    “So are you going to focus once we get in there,” Carol says, “Or are you going to keep flirting with me?”

    “That depends,” Zosia says, pulling her gloves into place. “Are you going to flirt back?”

    Carol rolls her eyes and that only adds fuel to Zosia’s fire.

    The moment they step inside, they’re met with the overwhelming smell of beer and copper. The hallway is narrow, the wall a faded yellow colour now tainted by a single smeared handprint of blood.

    The interior looks very much like Carol had envisioned, like a storm had passed through. Broken bottles lay scattered across the living room floor, brown and green shards of glass glinting in the hazy light. The coffee table is turned over with one leg snapped in half and a white powdery substance sprinkled throughout the carpet.

    And there he is, Jason Chan, the victim, sprawled on the floor on his side next to the couch, a small pool of blood having collected just by his head.

    Zosia approaches first and crouches by the body careful not to touch or move anything. “Blunt force trauma?” She’s quick to ascertain, her penlight shining on the victim’s forehead. “There’s a welt and a cut here.”

    Carol jots it down in her notepad. “Too messy for a planned assault,” she says. “Too emotional.”

    “You can almost feel the argument,” Zosia agrees, standing now.

    A shard of glass crunches under Carol’s foot as she walks around the body. “And the lack of cleanup.”

    “It was spontaneous,” Zosia says, eyeing the blood splatter pattern on the carpet. “Funny how breakups and murder start with the same kind of shouting.”

    Carol gives her a sidelong glance. “Is that supposed to be some kind of romantic insight?”

    Zosia half-smirks back at Carol. “Maybe.”

    “Zoe Diaz has hardly been dead,” Carol murmurs, carefully inspecting a broken bottle neck on the couch with her gloved hand.

    “Who’s to say Mr. Chan over here was monogamous?”

    Carol scoffs, and then tries her hardest not to react when she feels Zosia’s hand, gentle and warm, on her hip as she shuffles behind her and into the kitchen, their bodies brushing together.

    Outside, the rumble of the CSI van pulling up rattles the thin walls of the house and more bodies and voices can be heard congregating in the yard. Carol re-approaches the body and kneels beside it. “He’s been dead for about three hours,” she says, looking at her watch. “There’s no signs of forced entry, no defensive wounds. Clearly someone he knew.”

    Zosia joins her, crouching again. Their shoulders brush together and even over the bleak sight of the lifeless body before them, Carol feels a rush of warmth surging through her body.

    This is not the time.

    “You say that so calmly,” Zosia says, turning towards Carol. “Like you already know.”

    “It’s just deduction,” Carol says, meeting Zosia’s gaze.

    Zosia wobbles and steadies herself against Carol, a hand briefly settling on her knee. Then her lips flicker into a half a smirk. “Your powers of deduction are kind of hot.”

    Carol tries to not let her mouth twitch. “You really need a filter sometimes.”

    “Why?” Zosia leans forward purposely this time, her face as close to Carol’s as it’s ever been. “You seem to like me unfiltered.”

    Before Carol can answer, Officer Lewis enters the house and appears in the hallway behind them.

    “CSI is here and have started their perimeter sweep,” he says. “Lieutenant Davids wants an update at the van when you have one.”

    Carol stands first, halfway grateful for the interruption, halfway frustrated. “Few more minutes,” she says.

    Officer Lewis nods and slips away.

    Zosia stands again, the seriousness back on her face. She looks down at the body and then back to Carol. “So, Detective, do we think our suspect got away clean?”

    “Not with that handprint on the wall,” she says, matching Zosia’s tone.

    Zosia nods, her voice a bit softer now. “Rough way to spend a night.”

    “Tell me about it,” Carol murmurs under her breath.

    Her exhaustion suddenly catches up to her in that moment. Multiple sleepless nights just spent thinking. Thinking about the woman in front of her, thinking about her dead wife. At least Helen wasn’t taken away in a body bag like Jason Chan is about to be.

    Zosia turns her head and looks at Carol, their eyes, as always, catching for a beat too long. “Sturka,” she says with a softness that makes Carol feel something in her chest. “Are you okay?”

    Carol feels her throat tightening before she can stop it. How does Zosia do it? Make her feel, amidst a bloody crime scene, like she’s the vulnerable one.

    “I’m fine,” Carol finally says after too long. “Just tired. Didn’t sleep much last night.”

    The way that Zosia looks at Carol and smiles tells Carol that maybe she had also stayed up last night thinking about the same things.

    Zosia takes a step forward, closing some of the space between them. “Well, when we’re done here, you can take us to that cafe on Forth Street since you wanted to drive so badly and I can buy you a coffee. Or are you going to pretend you don’t like spending time with me?”

    Carol fights it but she can’t help but smile back. “I think I made it pretty clear yesterday that I do. But lets see how you do with the report first.”

    “That’s a hard bargain.”

    “Then get to work.”

    “Yes ma’am.”

    They finish up quickly and with little distractions, moving around each other like they’ve been partners for years. Carol wonders if this is just how it’s going to be. A delicate charade wrapped in (somewhat) professionalism. Crisp voices and polite smiles when others are around, but beneath the surface, something tender and dangerously alive simmering. “Accidental” fleeting touches that mean nothing and everything. And their not-so-subtle flirting, a thin veil for what really hides underneath it.

    The drive to the cafe is short but mostly quiet. Zosia taps away at the report on her laptop in the passenger seat, the radio playing some trashy pop song, while Carol focuses on the road and pretends to not notice from her peripheral all the times Zosia sneaks a peak at her. Then, at a red light, they both look over at the same time and Carol realizes that putting the job first might not be as easy as she thinks.

    *****

    On Thursday morning, Carol finds herself on her tiptoes, arm fully outstretched as she tries to pin a photo to the evidence board in line with all the others. She fusses about, not being able to pin it straight, and looks to Manousos who is at his desk in the bullpen across the way laughing at her struggle. Quickly and quietly, she flips him off.

    “You’ll wear a hole through the cork if you keep doing that.” Zosia’s voice suddenly comes from behind her.

    With an arm still outstretched over her head, Carol feels the tiny skip in her pulse. “You were the one who pinned these so high,” she says, her voice strained with frustration.

    “Not my fault I can actually reach,” she says, her tall frame casting a shadow over Carol’s shoulder. “Need a hand?”

    Carol doesn’t even have time to object because Zosia simply reaches up, her knuckles brushing past Carol’s temple, takes the pin out of her hand and tacks it to the board with infuriating ease. All the while Carol tries to block out the intoxicating smell of Zosia that fills that tiny space between them.

    “There,” Zosia says. “All done.”

    Still, Carol finds a reason to roll her eyes although lately there’s more affection behind it than anything else. She turns slightly, meaning to thank her, but Zosia is still standing close enough that they bump into each other and Carol can see all the flecks of brown in her eyes. For just a second too long, out in the open, they both still and the sound of the bullpen fades away.

    Carol manages a breathy, “thanks,” as they quickly step back and Zosia cheeks shade pink.

    After the moment passes and Zosia leaves, Carol can’t even be bothered to turn around to see the look on Manousos’ face.

    She knows.

    Later that day, Zosia finds Carol in the copy room. Carol tells herself it’s just a coincidence and not because she had taken the long way so she could purposely walk past Zosia’s office holding a very visible stack of papers.

    From the corner of her eye while mindlessly stapling sheets of paper together, Carol watches Zosia as she feeds her own papers into the copy machine. She looks entirely too serious for the simple task of making photocopies; her back pin straight, brows furrowed, and her jaw clenched. Something about the juxtaposition makes Carol laugh.

    “If you’re trying to intimidate the toner, it’s working.”

    Zosia doesn’t look over but Carol sees the way her lips quirk up. “And yet it only jams on your watch.”

    “Oh, no, that’s because it loves me. Naturally.” Carol shrugs. “Some of us are irresistible to machinery.”

    “Uh-huh, I see. So that’s why the microwave stopped working the other day when you were in the lunch room,” Zosia quips back as she places her finished stack of papers onto the counter next to Carol. “Must be exhausting.”

    Carol laughs softly, the air catching in her throat. “You have no idea.”

    Finally Zosia looks at Carol and the stillness between them starts to hum. Zosia reaches past Carol to grab the stapler at the same time Carol steps forward to move around Zosia and in one swift movement, they collide together in an almost silent tangle—too close and too sudden.

    Their faces brush together, half a breath shared, and the edge of a heartbeat trapped between them.

    Carol freezes, feeling the way Zosia’s breath ghosts across her cheek. Her whole body erupts into goosebumps and the hairs on the back of her neck stand up. The moment stretches long and wide. It’s fragile and electric at the same time until one of them, it’s hard to say who, moves just enough to let it break.

    Zosia straightens first before settling the stapler back down. “S-sorry,” she mumbles, her voice betrayed by the slightest tremor and her cheeks even redder than they were at the evidence board earlier.

    Carol stares at the floor, her heart beating loudly in her ears. She looks everywhere but at Zosia. “No, it’s, uh, fine.” She clears her throat, running a hand through her hair. “Occupational hazard of…crowded rooms,” she blabbers.

    “Right,” Zosia says, searching for Carol’s gaze but Carol still can’t meet it. “Occupational hazard.”

    Thankfully or not, Koumba enters obliviously, whistling to himself.

    “Ah, hello ladies,” he says bright and cheery as ever, setting his own stack of papers onto the counter. “Copy room must be the place to be this afternoon.” He pats the top of the photocopier with his hand like it’s a dog. “Sounds like she’s working hard. Carol, did you jam it again?”

    “What?” Carol finally looks up, the blush still high in her cheeks. She furrows her eyebrows towards Koumba. “No.”

    It makes Zosia laugh. “Actually, I’m just on my way out,” she says to Koumba. “Have at it.”

    On her way out, Zosia makes a point to pass directly in front of Carol, who is standing against the counter with her arms at her side. As she does, she lets her fingers lightly graze the inside of Carol’s wrist. And if Zosia notices the way Carol shivers, she doesn’t say a word.

    Carol stands there staring blankly at the wall while pretending to listen to whatever Koumba is talking about while the copier whirrs on and on.

    *****

    On Friday afternoon, Carol and Zosia drive back from the Medical Examiner’s office. The results from Jason Chan’s autopsy had come through and it has been confirmed that blunt force trauma to the head was the cause of his death. Unsurprisingly, much like his girlfriend, excessive amounts of meth as well as alcohol had been found in his system. And fingerprints that had been pulled from some of the broken beer bottles match that of Nacho Varga.

    Carol keeps her hands steady on the steering wheel, insisting that she drive again, while her podcast about literary scandals fills the silence; comforting, predictable, and if Zosia has anything to say about it, it’s “hopelessly dull and soul-numbingly boring.”

    From the passenger seat Zosia yawns loud and dramatically, as if to drive the point home.

    “Subtle,” Carol says dryly, eyes focused ahead.

    “Sturka, I’m dying,” Zosia groans, her head lolling against the window. “For the love of god can we please listen to something with a beat?”

    But before Carol can answer, mischief washes over Zosia and she’s leaning forward, her arm crossing the small expanse between them as she reaches for the screen, her laughter delicately bubbling into the confined air.

    Carol reacts instinctively, having had this battle with Helen many times, her hand shooting out to block Zosia’s. But instead of pushing Zosia away, her fingers land directly over hers—warmth meeting warmth—and they involuntarily curl around each other, falling to the centre console.

    It’s long enough for Carol’s pulse to stumble, for Zosia’s mirth to fade into silence, thick with something unnamed. And Carol’s mind scrambles for her usual logic; coworkers, boundaries, professionalism; but her body betrays her when she feels the way Zosia brushes her thumb over her knuckles.

    One heartbeat.

    Two heartbeats.

    Three heartbeats.

    Neither wants to let go, but they have to.

    So they do.

    Carol’s hand burns for the rest of the day.

    *****

    Carol sprawls out on the couch, her paperwork blanketing her like a quilt. Above her, the lamplight spills warm over her half drank glass of whiskey. Every report sits open on her laptop but she’s no long reading them anymore.

    She pushes her glasses to the top of her head and looks to the clock under the TV.

    11:03PM.

    Suddenly her phone buzzes on the cushion next to her and she would be lying to say that she doesn’t perk up when she sees Zosia’s name light up on the screen.

    Zosia: I know you’re still awake…

    Carol bites the inside of her cheek to stop herself from smiling.

    Carol: What’s it to you?

    Zosia: Please don’t tell me you’re working this late on a Friday night

    Carol pushes the papers that clutter her lap to the side, sitting up. She takes a sip of her drink, the alcohol tingling at her lips.

    Carol: And what if I am?

    Three dots pulse on the screen. Then stop. Then pulse again. Carol can almost picture Zosia frowning at the screen in the way she does when she reads something she doesn't want to. But maybe this time her hair is loose and messy, maybe she’s in bed, maybe her bra is off, maybe she’s in her underwear.

    Her phone buzzes again.

    Zosia: Your dedication to work is borderline masochistic

    Carol: Says the woman who stayed at the office until midnight the other day

    Zosia: That’s different. I was interrogating someone. And you were literally there too

    Carol: Is that what you call it when you charm someone into a confession?

    Almost an entire minute passes before Carol sees those three dots pop up again. Not that she had been counting. She takes another sip of her drink, relishing the way it burns on the way down.

    Zosia: Charm? So you were watching me then

    Carol feels the staccato in her pulse. It’s just a text message but somehow she still feels seen.

    Carol: I was observing. It’s part of my job, remember?

    Zosia: Right. Purely professional motives

    Carol hesitates one too many seconds. Then she huffs out a breath mixed with a laugh.

    Carol: Entirely procedural

    Another minute goes by but it feels like an eternity. The buzzing of the fridge from the kitchen gets louder and louder.

    Zosia: Maybe next time we’re both working this late…we can tackle it together. Two heads, one deadline…efficiency…

    Carol is glad for the fact that Zosia can’t see her right now. Hot and flushed, a little bit tipsy. Efficiency her fucking ass.

    Carol: So you are working then

    Zosia: I might be

    Carol taps her fingers against her knee while she considers her next response. In most other situations, she wouldn’t put the thought into it. But with Zosia, it feels different. It is different.

    Carol: Seems counter productive. We’d talk too much and get nothing done

    Zosia’s response is almost instant.

    Zosia: You think we’d talk? Interesting

    Carol stares at the message longer than she means too. Maybe Zosia’s drinking too. Maybe she’s even bolder behind a screen. Her own drink goes forgotten on the end table, cursor blinking on the open report—unimportant now. Her pulse intensifies, harder and faster all throughout her body, but especially between her legs.

    Zosia: You ok over there?

    Carol breathes, slow and steady. The lines are getting blurry and they are heading towards dangerous territory.

    Carol: Go to sleep, Zosia

    Zosia: Only if you do the same

    When the conversation ends, Carol tosses her phone to the other end of the couch where it wedges itself between the cushions. She groans loudly, hands in her face as she falls back dramatically and papers go flying everywhere.

    She continuously reminds herself how inappropriate this is, what a nightmare this would be if anything were to actually happen between them. But the more she tells herself no, the more she realizes it might be beyond her control.

    *****

    It’s late Saturday night when Carol gets a call from Chief Schrader saying there might be potential movement at the house Jason Chan’s body was found in. He apologizes profusely for ruining her weekend, but insists he always wants the the best on the case, as if sitting in a car and taking notes requires that much brain power.

    Normally Carol might not mind, but she’s long been in her pajamas and was just about to crack into the bottle of wine that’s been chilling in her fridge.

    Now, hours later, her and Zosia have been in the car together for so long that even the radio has given up. Just the soft static and a blinking clock that insists it’s 1:37 AM.

    Carol slouches low, passenger seat all the way back, feet up on the dash. She knows it’s late and Zosia is tired because she’s barely said a word in the last hour.

    “I swear,” Carol sighs, finally peeling her feet off the dashboard and sitting up, “if we have to sit outside one more suspect’s house eating stale pretzels in the dark like feral fucking raccoons, I’m putting it on my resume.”

    Zosia tears her gaze from the sleepy house down the street and glances over, one brow lifting.

    “What?”

    “I don’t know. Detective by day, raccoon by night.”

    “More like disgruntled owl. Judgmental. Big eyes. Aggressively nocturnal.”

    “I am not judgmental.” She pauses, remembering that she did very much judge Zosia at the beginning. “Usually…”

    Zosia scoffs. “Your job is literally to judge,” she says. “You’re doing it right now. Judging this house. And the porch light, and that poor, innocent shrub.”

    “Looks guilty if you ask me,” Carol mutters, stuffing another pretzel into her mouth. “And so are you. Judging, I mean.”

    Zosia huffs out a laugh, but it drifts off sooner than usual. She taps the steering wheel with the side of her thumb, a faint restless rhythm. Even through her tiredness, something feels different about her tonight.

    “Do you ever get tired of it?” She asks, seemingly out of nowhere.

    “The…shrub?”

    “No, genius. This. The late nights. The…living in your car between crime scenes and everything else.”

    Carol considers playing it off. Habit, muscle memory. But instead she watches Zosia’s profile, the way the orange glow of the streetlight catches the faint scar near her hairline and then sparkles in her earring.

    “Sometimes.” She shrugs. “But then I remember the alternative is… brunch with civilians. Asking what everyone does for a living and pretending I don’t want to leave my own body.”

    “That’s bleak,” Zosia says, now smiling. “But accurate.”

    “You asked.”

    “I did.” Zosia goes quiet again, fingers still drumming. “I used to think this job was going to save me.”

    Carol blinks. This isn’t where she had expected the night to go considering they had spent the whole week aggressively flirting and “accidentally” touching each other but she’s learning that there’s something about the darkness that brings out the truth and vulnerability in the both them.

    “From what, jury duty?”

    “Pfft. From myself.” Zosia’s voice is lighter than the words. “From the version of me who couldn’t fix anything or keep anyone safe. I thought if I worked hard enough maybe the cosmic balance sheet would stop being so lopsided.”

    The sound of a car whirring by them fills the pause.

    “And how’s that working out?” Carol asks, gentler this time.

    Zosia’s laugh is small. “I’ve had to start my life over three times and now I’m camped out in a car on a Saturday night at almost two in the morning and eating pretzels that probably expired during the Obama administration. So, you tell me.”

    “Well at least your company tonight is tolerable.”

    A quiet moment passes. “It’s more than tolerable,” Zosia says, no facade.

    Carol lets out a pent up sigh louder than she means to. It’s by no means a huge admission or a surprise, the feeling having been mutual for a while now, but it’s enough to make her mind race. She watches her closely again. Bored Zosia is all commentary and ridiculous analogies. This version—careful, a little raw—shows up less often, like some rare desert animal.

    “You do know that’s not how it works,” Carol says, holding out the bag of pretzels to Zosia. “The balance sheet. The universe doesn’t give refunds.”

    “I know,” Zosia says, reaching her hand into the bag. “It’s just… do you ever feel like if you stop moving, all the things you’ve outrun finally catch up?”

    The question slides under Carol’s skin like a needle. She thinks of her own house—quiet, tidy, full of objects that had belonged to someone who no longer exists outside of photographs and muscle memory. She thinks of the bed she still can’t sleep in some nights.

    “Yeah,” she says. “I’ve felt that.”

    Zosia looks at her then, really looks, eyes searching. “I forget, sometimes,” she says slowly. “That I’m not the only one who lost—who had everything go sideways.”

    Carol’s first instinct is to shrug it off, to make a joke about their unofficial sad ladies club, Tuesdays and Thursdays, bring your own tissues and trauma. Instead, she tilts her head, meeting Zosia’s gaze head-on.

    “You don’t have to forget,” she offers. “I just don’t advertise. It’s terrible for my mysterious reputation.”

    “Right,” Zosia says, lips quirking. “Wouldn’t want people to find out you have feelings. Might ruin the brand.”

    “Now you get it.”

    They share a faint smile that feels like a handshake. Or something more intimate.

    “It was cancer, by the way.” Carol lets out a long breath looking out her passenger window, but through the reflection she can still see Zosia. “The aggressive kind. Doctor’s had given her nine months, it took her in four.”

    Another moment of silence passes, a shared ache between them. This time it’s just the static from the radio and the bag of pretzels rustling on the console.

    Zosia shifts in her seat, angling toward Carol. “When Aleks died,” she says, the words soft but steady, “everyone wanted to fix it. They brought casseroles like my grief was a fucking potluck. After a while I just stopped telling people. It was exhausting, watching them try to say the right thing.”

    Carol’s chest tightens. Doesn’t she know it. “Let me guess. Lots of ‘everything happens for a reason’ and ‘he’s in a better place’?”

    “And my personal favourite,” Zosia says, grimacing, “‘At least you still have your career.’ Like the FBI was supposed to hug me.”

    “The FBI can’t even hug its own budget,” Carol says. “You were doomed.”

    Zosia snorts, but her eyes are shinier now. “You got casseroles too?”

    “Lasagnas,” Carol corrects. “Endless fucking lasagnas. I started sorting people by noodle type.”

    Zosia raises an eye brow. “Noodle type?”

    “Mhm.” Carol’s mouth twitches and her heart twinges at the memories. “People who brought fresh lasagna and stayed to actually sit with me, I kept around. The ones who dropped off a frozen brick and fled? Not so much.”

    Carol watches as Zosia processes the words.

    “And where do people who bring takeout fall?” Zosia questions. “Hypothetically.”

    “Depends,” Carol says. “Do they eat it with you on the floor with a bottle of wine at two in the morning while you both pretend watching bad TV counts as coping?”

    “That’s oddly specific.”

    “Hypothetically.”
