    Zosia watches her, something warm threading through her expression. “And where do partners who sit with you on stakeouts and talk about cosmic balance sheets land on the noodle scale?”

    Carol pretends to consider it. “Somewhere between ‘acceptable’ and ‘menace to society.’”

    “Wow, high praise again from you.”

    “You’re climbing the ranks,” Carol smiles to herself. “Slowly. Like mould.”

    Zosia laughs, then sobers, thumb still tapping the wheel. “You know,” she says. “I told you about Aleks before I told my last partner anything real. Took him almost a year to find out I even had a kid.”

    “That just means you have terrible boundaries.” Carol means it lightly, but her chest feels tight.

    “I mean it,” Zosia says. “I don’t know how you did it but you made it… easier. To say the ugly parts.”

    Carol looks back at the house, more to break the intensity than out of duty. Her palms feel sweaty, tucked into her lap. “I’m just sitting here making fun of shrubs, Zosia.”

    “Yeah,” Zosia nods. “And somehow I feel less like a walking open wound and more like a person when I’m with you. It’s annoying as hell.”

    “Sorry to improve your life without permission,” Carol says, but her voice goes low. She had not anticipated that they would be unearthing such hidden layers about each other tonight when previously the only layers she had been concerned about were the layers of Zosia’s clothes coming off.

    Carol feels Zosia’s eyes bearing into her.

    “What about you?” Zosia asks.

    “What about me?”

    “What did this job save you from?”

    Carol’s instinct again is to deflect. Say something like, “daytime television” or “a respectable sleep schedule.” The words are lined up on her tongue, sharp and ready. But Zosia is still looking at her with this unyielding persistence, and the car feels very small.

    “It gave me somewhere to be,” Carol says finally. “After Helen died.”

    Zosia’s expression softens.

    “I couldn’t stay in the house,” Carol goes on, staring at the fire hydrant across the street. “Every mug, every book, every stupid pair of socks had some memory attached. I’d walk past the couch and it was like being hit by a truck.” She pauses, trying to stop the tremor in her voice. “Work was loud. Ugly. But it was somewhere I didn’t have to remember what it felt like to have a future that wasn’t just… getting through.”

    Zosia’s fingers still on the wheel. When Carol looks over at her, it feels like the space between them wants to shrink even more. Zosia has a look on her face like she’s debating if she should reach over and touch Carol and Carol wishes she would.

    “Does it still feel like that?” Zosia asks. “Just getting through?”

    Carol hesitates. The honest answer sits there, heavy and fragile. “Less,” she says quietly. “Since you showed up and started reorganizing my evidence boards and emotional landscape without asking.”

    Zosia’s laugh comes out a little shaky. “My greatest crimes.”

    “Those are just the ones I can prove,” Carol says. “I’m building a case.”

    “Good luck,” Zosia murmurs. “All the best evidence is circumstantial.”

    They fall into silence again, but it’s different now. Softer, like the air after a storm, yet pulsing with a heated undercurrent.

    Carol’s heart doesn’t beat faster this time, it beats harder.

    Zosia clears her throat. “If I ask you something, will you promise not to make a joke for, like, ten seconds?”

    “That’s a long time,” Carol says, straightening herself in her seat. “But fine. Timer starts now.”

    “Do you ever want more than this? I don’t mean promotions and bigger caseloads. I mean—” Zosia gestures vaguely at the narrow space between them. “More than just partners who share tragedies and pretzels.”

    Carol’s heart stutters now. She know’s exactly what Zosia means. The question has been hanging in the air for weeks dressed up as banter and almost-kisses and touches and glances that lingered too long to not mean anything. Each day it becomes harder and harder to ignore.

    She also knows what it would cost to admit wanting anything. The last time she’d let herself want, she’d ended up picking songs for a funeral playlist.

    “Ten seconds are up,” Carol says softly.

    Zosia’s mouth curves. “Is that a yes or a dodge?”

    “A strategic retreat. We’re on duty.”

    “Stakeout,” Zosia says. “We’re sitting in a car watching an unconscious house. The most exciting thing that’s happened in the last hour is you declaring war on shrubbery.”

    “The shrub started it.”

    “Sturka.”

    When Carol doesn’t say anything, Zosia adds, quieter but without any reservation, “I meant what I said that night at the bar. About being intrigued by you. Although at this point it’s more than just intrigue, but I think you already know that.”

    Carol simply nods. The acknowledgement feels almost too real and now it can’t be taken back.

    They turn back to the quiet house in tandem, the night still long, the job still the job. But along the way, something shifts; pieces of themselves laid out on the dashboard between the crumbs and case notes, examined and not shoved back into the dark.

    After a minute, Zosia speaks again, lighter now. “So, question,” she says. “If the universe doesn’t do refunds, does it at least do store credit?”

    Carol lets out an amused scoff, the tightness releasing from her chest. “What, you want to exchange your trauma for a different model?”

    “Maybe just upgrade the accessories,” Zosia says. “Less guilt, more… possibilities.”

    Carol glances at her, her mouth, the tired hope in her eyes. “I think,” she says slowly, “we might already be working on that.”

    Zosia’s smile spreads, warm and real. “Good.”

    “Don’t get used to me being this sincere,” Carol warns. “I have a reputation to uphold.”

    “Oh, I know,” Zosia says. “On Monday I’ll go back to pretending I don’t notice when you save me the last donut.”

    Carol shakes her head. “That’s just professionalism,” she counters. “Can’t have my partner fainting from low blood sugar.”

    “Mm-hm,” Zosia hums. “You’re very noble.”

    “It’s tragic.”

    They fall quiet again, but this time the silence feels more like an open door instead of a locked one.

    After a while, Zosia nudges the pretzel bag towards Carol. “By the way, raccoons aren’t feral.  They’re wild,” she says. “You on the other hand…”

    Carol scoffs in mock offence. “I’ll show you feral.”

    “Oh, I’m sure you will,” Zosia laughs. “So do you want the last one or what?”

    Carol takes the bag, their fingers brushing  a small, deliberate contact that sends a line of heat up her arm.

    “Yeah,” she says. “I do.”

    *****

    It’s late Wednesday afternoon when it finally rains.

    Heavy grey clouds swell over the city after weeks of blue skies and punishing heat. Far off, low rumbles of thunder quiver through the air. The raindrops slash at the windshield, fat and spiteful, as the wipers thrash back and forth, hopeless against the downpour. Ahead of Carol and Zosia, the road dissolves into a glassy blur.

    “Maybe you should have driven,” Zosia says.

    When Carol looks over, Zosia’s hands are white knuckled to the steering wheel, overly focused on staying within the lines. She’s shaken from the aftermath of this morning.

    Traffic accident. Single fatality. A child.

    Carol had insisted she could take the call with Manousos or Koumba, but Zosia had insisted back that she was fine to go. In the month that Carol has known Zosia, she had never seen her as such a shell of herself. Her gaze tunnelling inwards, distant and guarded.

    She had been quiet and distracted the rest of the day.

    “I still can,” Carol offers. “If you’d just pull over.”

    “No,” Zosia breathes. “We’re almost there.”

    “You’re more stubborn than I am,” Carol says and that seems to make Zosia smile and relax.

    Frank’s pawn shop has been hit again. Not a brick through the window this time, but a small fire set to the backdoor, which luckily for Frank, the rain had assisted in putting out.

    Now Carol and Zosia are stuck out here in the back alley with nothing but their paper thin raincoats which offer little to no protection, searching for what seems to be non-existent evidence.

    “This is ridiculous,” Carol has to practically shout over the rain as she wipes her hair out of her face; what’s even the point of a hood if it doesn’t stay on? “I can’t even see anything. Why are we even out here?”

    Zosia’s huff can be heard over the rain. “You’re right,” she shouts back. “Let’s go. We can come back tomorrow.”

    Just as they turn to leave, a loud shuffling noise from behind the dumpster draws their attention. They both look and a man appears, drenched, in nothing but shorts and a t-shirt, and stops before them.

    “Hey,” Carol calls out.

    The man doesn’t move until Zosia flashes her badge at him, telling him to come forward. And he does, alright. He bolts towards them quickly. Carol places a hand over her gun but he’s too fast, knocking Zosia in the shoulder and spinning her off balance. Zosia tries to take off after him but the pavement is too slick and she can’t see with the rain and her hair in her eyes and that’s when she collides head first into a metal pipe jutting out from the brick wall.

    “Motherfucker,” Zosia hisses, her hand immediately reaching for her forehead as she doubles over.

    Carol feels her heart sink into her stomach, her pulse beating as fast as it ever has but not in a good way. She tries calling for backup but the man is too far gone and she’s more concerned with Zosia who has blood trickling down her face.

    “Jesus Christ,” Carol breathes, running to Zosia before she has the time to fall the ground. She places an arm around her, steadying her to her feet. “Let’s go. There’s a first aid kit in the car.”

    “No, I’m fine,” Zosia insists, wiping the blood away with the back of her hand. She sways, leaning into Carol. “It’s just a scratch.”

    “You can’t be serious,” Carol scoffs, gripping her arm tighter, the rain unrelenting. “You are not fine. You just split your fucking eyebrow open. You could have a concussion.”

    “I don’t.”

    “Zosia. Now.”

    Back in the car, Carol frantically rummages through the first aid kit while they both sop with rainwater but there’s nothing in it except for a few pieces of gauze and latex gloves. Some budget the department has.

    “This is all there is,” Carol says, handing Zosia the gauze. “There are napkins in the glove compartment. And I think…I think there’s a towel in the trunk.”

    “Stop, please. I told you I’m fine,” Zosia insists again, holding the gauze to her forehead which instantly turns red. Her tone is more pointed and ragged than usual. “Can we just go back, I’ll deal with it there.”

    Carol fusses, growing impatient with Zosia’s steadfast act of invincibility. Maybe if the situation were reversed Carol would react the same way but Zosia won’t even look at her, her ego seemingly more bruised than anything else. Carol knows there’s no point in arguing.

    “Fine, but I’m not filling out an incident report if you bleed out in here.”

    Zosia almost smiles.

    The car ride back to the office is silent. Carol drives, obviously. No radio, no conversation. Just the rain splashing against the windshield and the usual sounds of traffic. There are sparks of lightning now, thunder cracking in tow. The few times that Carol looks over at Zosia, she’s staring out the window, quiet and pensive, arm resting against the window holding the gauze in place.

    “You need to change the gauze,” is all Carol says.

    Zosia does.

    Finally, back at the office, when the adrenaline simmers into something more gentle, Zosia slips quietly away to the bathroom while Carol pretends not to notice. She shouldn’t be, but there’s a part of Carol that feels hurt that Zosia pushed her away like that, as if she were too good to show any weakness.

    Carol paces around her office trying to wring herself dry to no avail but when too many minutes pass and Zosia hasn’t walked past yet, Carol decides fuck it, and grabs the first aid kit from under her desk.

    She enters the bathroom slowly, stopping at the doorway. She watches Zosia for a moment, tall and slender leaning over the sink, paper towel held to her head. Her blouse has been shed, collar stained pink and crumpled onto the counter leaving her exposed in just a plain white camisole. Above her, the fluorescent lights flicker while lightning streaks behind the frosted glass window, each burst washing the bathroom in cold, white light.

    “So are you planning on bleeding to death in here?” Carol asks, slowly approaching. “Or are you finally going to accept my help?”

    Zosia barely looks over. “I said I was—“

    “Fine?” Carol sets the first aid kit onto the counter. “You almost got knocked out by a metal pipe. You're not fine. You’re hardheaded.” Her voice is quiet but firm, but Zosia doesn’t move.

    “I’ve been hit by worse,” she says.

    Carol sighs. “Is that supposed to make me feel better?”

    But when Zosia still doesn’t look over, Carol steps close, forcing Zosia to feel her there. Zosia tenses just for a second before relaxing, the stiffness leaving her shoulders.

    “Sit,” Carol says.

    After a moment, Zosia complies even if she’s reluctant. She turns and perches herself onto the counter. It’s low and her and Carol are exactly at eye level, and for the first time in what feels like hours, their eyes meet. Zosia’s gaze is hardened at first; squared with too much pride. It’s equal parts infuriating an magnetic. But then it softens, her eyes dark and piercing like she’s looking for something in Carol.

    Carefully, she inches closer until she’s just flush against Zosia’s knees. She reaches up, her hand wrapping around Zosia’s wrist, pulling it away from her face. The contact is warm and tender and Carol can feel her own fingertips pulsing against Zosia’s soft skin. “Let me see.”

    It’s not as bad as she would have thought. The blood is mostly dried around the edges and it’s not too deep, just a neat little slash above her brow. Still, the sight of if makes Carol’s stomach twist because she hadn’t realized how much she would hate seeing Zosia hurt, even if Zosia can’t admit to it.

    “Sorry,” Zosia finally says. “For being a stubborn bitch.”

    Carol lets out a small laugh through her nose. “Yeah, you were.” She pulls out an antiseptic wipe from the first aid kit and dabs it onto the cut. Zosia winces and flinches, and instinctively Carol reaches up with her other hand and gently stills her by the chin. She’ll pretend she doesn’t see the way the pulse in Zosia’s neck quickens. “Stop squirming or you’ll make it worse.”

    Their faces are close now. Too close. Carol can see the slight smattering of freckles on Zosia’s cheek and a faint scar on her temple, the way her lashes flutter when she tenses. She can smell the rain on her and the last traces of her shampoo mixed with the natural musk of her skin. And when the lightning flashes and lights the space between then, Zosia’s eyes come alive, hot and bright.

    Carol feels it in her whole body.

    “You’re enjoying this, aren’t you?” Zosia asks, her voice low now, a teasing edge around the pain.

    Carol doesn’t look away, a hand still on Zosia’s chin. “I might be. You’re cute when you’re not pretending to be bulletproof.”

    The words slip out before she can even stop them and Zosia laughs, small and surprised, and it fills the tight space between them. It’s a sound so sweet that it sends a ripple of heat down Carol’s spine.

    Zosia holds still, breathing a little faster now. Carol smooths a bandage into place, fingertips brushing across Zosia’s cheek and the air between them changes. It’s thick and charged, like it’s a heartbeat away from something irreversible.

    Carol’s thumb brushes the corner of her mouth almost by accident. Almost.

    Zosia swallows, then her lips part, barely. “Is this part of the treatment plan?” Her voice is barely above a whisper.

    Carol tries for a smile but it’s faint and unsteady, her pulse erratic. “Improvised medicine,” she whispers back, eye’s flickering down the Zosia’s lips and settling there.

    There’s a fleeting silence that follows and Zosia leans forward just enough to feel the full contact of Carol’s hand on her face. She closes her eyes and spreads her knees apart, allowing Carol to step closer.

    The space between them catches fire. Carol closes her eyes and she’s so achingly close that she can feel Zosia’s breath ghosting hot across her lips. Between them, Zosia grabs a fistful of Carol’s shirt, keeping her close; and Carol’s breath hitches when her fingers make contact with her stomach.

    “Carol.”

    Carol all but whimpers. Two syllables she’s known all her life suddenly with a renewed meaning as it slips out of Zosia’s mouth and into hers; desperate, like a prayer.

    Her resolve starts to slip away like sand sifting through open fingers when Zosia brushes her nose against hers.

    It’s then, their lips almost touching, that Carol becomes too aware of the fact that they’re not completely because at any second, someone could walk through the door. And she knows that if she kisses Zosia now, she won’t be able to stop.

    Lightning flashes between them again and Carol allows herself to linger for one heartbeat longer. Then, her chest aching, she forces herself to step back, her hand dragging down the middle of Zosia’s chest.

    Zosia’s eyes are still closed, face red, breaths shallow. When she finally opens them, they’re still dark and full of yearning and it makes Carol want to do something reckless.

    “I…I should go. I think.” Carol barely chokes out. “You should, um, ice that.”

    Zosia doesn't say anything and Carol doesn’t expect her to. So Carol does the only thing she thinks she can do.

    She leaves.

    +

    That night, Carol lays wide awake in her bed.

    She presses her fingers to her lips, replaying the way Zosia’s breath had felt against them.

    The way their noses touched.

    They way she had said her name.

    Carol doesn’t sleep.

    At all.

     

    Chapter End Notes

    The mental/emotional toll this chapter took on me lol...

    As always, love ya!

    Chapter 6: kiss me beneath the milky twilight

    Chapter Summary

    Gravity, or something.

    Chapter Notes

    The ground is still damp when Carol arrives to work. The rain had only finally stopped a few hours ago, early into the morning. The sky is still scattered with leftover gloom, though the sun is  threatening to make an appearance, warm golden rays starting to peak through the clouds.

    Carol sits in her car long after she had shut the engine off, her hands nervously fidgeting in her lap. The parking lot is the same as it is every morning; white lines, the same cars in the same spots, but this morning everything in Carol feels rearranged.

    She can still feel it. Zosia’s breath dancing hot across her lips, the way she had grabbed Carol by the shirt, anchoring her in place, and their noses brushing together in a way that felt even more intimate than a kiss.

    And then there was the way Zosia had been looking at her. God. Carol can’t forget that, eyes so dark with want that it makes her dizzy thinking about it.

    She leans her head against the headrest and exhales loudly. “I’m so fucking screwed.”

    Carol had waited all night to see if Zosia’s name would light up her phone, but it never did. But why would it? She was the one who walked away. Then, Carol had thought that she should be the one to reach out to Zosia, but every time she pulled up their message thread, she couldn’t actually bring herself to press send.

    Sorry I didn’t kiss you.

    Sorry I chickened out.

    Sorry I fucking left you sitting there.

    If Zosia is upset, Carol would see it right away. The clipped voice, the tight smile. But what if she isn’t upset? What if she acts like nothing had happened? That would be arguably worse, Carol believes. And she’s seen Zosia do it before. Mask her mess of feelings with short sarcasm or deflection and move on like she hadn’t turned Carol’s world sideways.

    Carol’s stomach knots. She pictures walking into the break room, the sound of cheap coffee percolating amongst the morning crowd, and the two of them bumping shoulders next to the fridge like strangers.

    Across the parking lot, a crow hops along the edge of a puddle, black feathers glossy against the one spot the sun is hitting. Carol watches for a moment and then flips down her sun visor. She stares at the dark circles under her eyes from her lack of sleep and pulls the concealer out of her bag. And for the first time ever, she finds it difficult to look at the crinkled picture of Helen, her chest suddenly feeling tight. Quickly, she flips it back up.

    She exhales slow and steady this time and grabs her bag and travel mug. She can’t hide in her car all day.

    Carol rounds the row of cars and thats when she spots Zosia’s Explorer parked right by the edge of the walkway; lights on, engine idling, and Carol can see her in there. Her head is tilted down, presumably looking at her phone. Or maybe…thinking.

    Carol freezes mid-step, her mind caught between two places. She could just keep walking and pretend that she doesn’t notice her, but Zosia, attentive to a fault, would see her and know she was avoiding her. But there’s something about Zosia’s presence, even if it’s behind glass, that pulls at Carol like gravity.

    So Carol crosses the few yards that separates them, her shoes scuffling across the pavement. She hesitates at the passenger side window, her reflection superimposed over Zosia’s face, and then raises her hand tapping lightly at the glass.

    Zosia looks up and for just a split second, the world between them stops. Then she unlocks the door, tossing her bag from the passenger seat to the back.

    Carol slips in, shutting the door behind her and immediately the air fills with everything unsaid between them.

    “Hi,” Carol says gently, eyes flickering to Zosia. She can’t read her the way she normally can.

    Zosia looks at her almost expectantly at first, like she’s waiting for some kind of explanation or waiting for Carol to react first so she can react back accordingly. After a moment of tentative eye contact, a small but genuine smile grows across Zosia’s mouth and Carol lets out a breath she hadn’t realized she was holding.

    “Hey,” Zosia finally says.

    Carol loosens her grip around her mug and the silence slowly starts to crackle. She looks to the neat little bandage that’s been cut to delicate precision to fit the curve of Zosia’s brow line, now haloed by a purple bruise. Even still, she’s an image of beauty, the kind that lingers quietly in Carol’s chest long after she’s looked away.

    “How’s, uh, the battle wound?” Carol asks, unsure of what the actual right thing to say is.

    “Oh,” Zosia says, like she’s surprised by the question. “It’s sore, but doesn’t sting anymore.”

    Carol nods, small. “That’s good. You had me really worried.”

    Zosia’s smile warms now, etched with her familiar teasing. “Were you this protective with Manny too, or am I just special?”

    Carol lets out a bit of a strangled laugh, relief tugging at her shoulders. “I never needed to be. He never ran head first into an inanimate object.”

    Zosia scoffs. “Low blow.”

    They both share a quiet laugh.

    It doesn’t feel like anything’s change, their banter slipping back into place like it never left. A brief silence settles between them where they can’t look away from each other, as if tethered together by the memory of last night’s almost-kiss. Carol idly wonders if the people walking by can feel the pull between them.

    “So we have that interrogation later—” Carol begins, but she stops mid-sentence when she notices the way Zosia is just looking at her, like a thousand words waiting to spill off her tongue.

    “Can you humour me for just a second,” Zosia says, shifting in her seat and angling towards Carol. “And tell me if I’ve been misreading everything between us.” Her eyes stay steady and patient on Carol.

    Oh. Carol stills, but only for a second because she’s processing the words. But when she does, she shakes her head, zero hesitation. “No. Not even a little bit.”

    “Okay,” Zosia practically sighs. “Good to know.”

    This vulnerable and honest version of Zosia continues to surprise Carol. The heat builds low in her stomach before spreading up to her chest, then neck. “I…” she starts, her voice getting caught in her throat and then having to look away, “Panicked. Last night.”

    Zosia all but laughs as if to say, yeah no fucking shit. But what she says instead, softer and gentler, is, “You know, I thought about it later. Well, all night actually. And kissing you in the bathroom at work while I was covered in blood didn’t seem very romantic.”

    Carol feels the weight of every word; the unexpected heat of them enveloping her, the burning of her lips. But knowing, out loud, that Zosia’s been up all night thinking about the same things makes her feel like she’s not insane.

    “That might be a kink to some people,” Carol says, deflecting.

    “Oh?” There’s a pause after Zosia says it where the spark returns to her eyes. “You say that like it’s yours.”

    Carol rolls her eyes as if she hadn’t just walked herself into that. “Quiet, you.” It’s somewhere in between a laugh and huff, but there’s a certain type of affection in it that’s only reserved for Zosia.

    Then, after a few too many beats of silence and Carol’s mind running in circles, she mumbles towards the passenger window, “I thought about it all night too. And I’m sor—“

    “Don’t,” Zosia says immediately, her tone earnest. She places a hand on Carol’s arm, drawing her back. The touch lingers for a moment before she tucks her hand back into her lap. “There’s nothing to apologize for. It would have been reckless and unprofessional.”

    Right. Unprofessional. Somehow hearing that from Zosia makes everything feel a little bit more real. A little bit more scary.

    “Nothing about any of this is professional,” Carol offers.

    Zosia simply smiles at her. “No, it’s not.”

    They both exhale at the same time and the synchronicity of it makes them laugh. Carol’s quick to open the door, stepping out. “Hurry up, Lieutenant. We’re going to be late.”

    Carol is halfway to the front door when she hears Zosia’s quick footsteps trying to catch up to her, like she couldn’t bear for a moment to not be next to Carol.

    And it’s when they cross the doorway together, Zosia’s knuckles brushing against Carol’s, that it’s safe for Carol to say that last night hasn’t changed a thing between them. If anything, it’s only intensified it.

    +

    A single dim light hangs over the centre of the metal table. Across from Carol and Zosia sits Eddie Langdon. He has been confirmed the driver and owner of the black Chevy Silverado. His fingerprints have also been pulled from Jason Chan’s crime scene. Now Chief Schrader has brought him in for questioning.

    Carol’s eyes are narrowed at him while next to her Zosia has his file open, tapping her pen lightly against the table, calm, eyes sharp.

    “Listen, sweetheart,” Eddie says, leaning back in his chair. His voice is heavy with smoke and he sports a grin carved out by arrogance. “You can flash those big brown eyes all you want but I already told your two buddies over there everything.” He gestures towards the two way mirror where Manousos and Koumba are no doubt watching them from.

    Zosia’s expression doesn’t budge. “You said you were home at midnight. But cameras and doorbell footage have your truck only one block away from the crime scene at twelve-thirty.”

    Eddie scoffs, incredulous. “Maybe I like to drive. Clear my head. You ever do that, sweetheart? Or are you too busy playing detective?”

    Carol’s jaw tightens and her hands ball into fists. The sound of his voice, ugly and grating as it lingers over sweetheart, snaps something taut inside of her. Zosia is unflappable, coolness personified, but that doesn’t make it any easier to watch someone talk down to her. No one’s ever spoken to Manousos like that, or even to herself while Manousos had been at her side.

    Fucking misogynistic prick.

    “That’s funny,” Carol says, her patience already wavering thin. “Because your license was suspended three weeks ago. So unless you’ve learnt how to teleport or are driving illegally without a license, your fun little drive didn’t happen.” She leans forward, eyes meeting his straight on. “Want to try again?”

    Eddie’s smirk falters. “What, you think you’re clever?”

    “No,” Carol replies, voice cool. “I think you’re careless.”

    Zosia flicks her gaze towards Carol; a quick glance, but full of electricity, and Carol catches it for just a second before focusing back to the sorry looking scrotum in front of her. Eddie shifts in his chair, suddenly not looking so comfortable.

    Carol presses on, deliberate, and her pulse quickens at the fact that she knows she has him trapped. “Let me guess. You were heading out to ‘clear your head’ but somehow cellphone towers have you pinged right outside the Jason Chan’s crime scene. And let’s not forget your fingerprints are there too. So maybe you should clear up your story instead.”

    He blusters, fumes of defensiveness filling the air. Still, Carol doesn’t flinch. She takes joy when the truth starts to corner someone, it’s like watching rust peel back from metal. But what really anchors her is the silent steadiness beside her. The way Zosia straightens, eyes glinting. Pride. Admiration, maybe. Whatever it is, Carol feels it.

    By the time Eddie breaks, muttering some half-formed excuse, Zosia closes the file with quiet finality. Carol stands, slow and sure, letting her anger settle into professionalism.

    “Interview suspended,” she says. “We’ll continue when you remember how to talk like a grown man.”

    Outside, as the door to the interrogation room shuts behind them, Carol exhales hard trying to control her adrenaline. Zosia turns towards her, lips curving into a tiny, soft smile that these days hits a little harder than it should. Quickly, Zosia passes off the file to Manousos and Koumba who re-enter the room to escort Mr. Langdon out.

    “Didn’t realize you would take that so personally,” Zosia says once they’re alone.

    Carol looks to her. “Only because he forgot who he was talking to.”

    For a moment, silence, just muffled inaudible voices from beyond the window. Zosia’s smile lingers and Carol realizes her pulse hasn’t slowed at all.

    They barely make it three steps into the corridor before the adrenaline actually hits Carol full force. She can still hear Eddie Langdon’s hideous voice in her head, low and oily, wrapping itself around sweetheart like he was doing Zosia some kind of favour. She flexes her fingers once, shaking out her hands as if she could fling the last of him off her skin.

    Zosia closes the door behind her with a soft click, then leans back against it for a second, eyes shut. Her shoulders rise and fall on a slow, measured breath. When she opens her eyes, she’s composed again, of course she is, but there’s colour high in her cheeks, a flare of heat that wasn’t there before.

    “Nice work in there,” Zosia says. “You had him scrambling.”

    Carol huffs out a breath that might also be a laugh. “He scrambled himself. I just nudged.”

    Zosia smiles again, small and private in a way that she never seems to wear for anyone else. “You nudged hard.”

    They start walking down the hall together, side by side. The bullpen buzzes ahead, phones ringing, printers whirring, the Chief laughing loudly in the break room. But for a minute it all feels muted and distant. Carol is hyper-aware of the sound of Zosia’s boots against the floor and the smell of her shampoo that she had gotten too close to last night.

    “Are you okay?” Zosia asks, attuned to Carol’s mood.

    The question is casual enough on the surface, but Carol can hear the thread under it. Concern, maybe, and something else. Something that makes her want to stand a little taller.

    “I’m fine,” Carol says. “He was just a prick.”

    “That’s generous,” Zosia replies dryly.

    Carol glances sideways at her. “He had no right to talk to you like that.”

    Zosia meets her glance, quickly, then ahead again. “I can handle a little condescension.”

    “I know you can.” Carol’s voice is rougher than she intends. She clears her throat. “That’s not the point.”

    They reach the end of the hallway, past the bullpen, to where the back stairwell is. Carol had inadvertently led them there, while Zosia had unconsciously followed her there like it was the most natural thing to do.

    Carol pushes the door open and Zosia follows closely behind. The door shuts with a loud thud that echoes through the stairwell. Carol descends about half way down the first flight, then stops. She sits, leaning one shoulder against the cool metal railing. Zosia stops next to her, but leans up against the wall on the other side. She folds her arms loosely over her chest. Not defensively, just relaxed and grounded.

    “So what is the point?” Zosia asks quietly.

    Carol looks up at her. Even from here, she can see the faint smudge of fatigue beneath Zosia’s eyes, much like her own, and the crease between her eyebrows when she’s concentrating too hard on something.

    “He was trying to get under your skin.” Carol says. “Trying to knock you off balance. Make you look smaller so he could feel bigger. And I’m not…I can’t let that slide. Not from him. Not from anybody.”

    Something flickers in Zosia’s expression. There’s another shift between them, almost imperceptible. The sound of a door opening and closing on another floor echos loudly and the stairwell seems to shrink.

    “You know,” Zosia says, now joining her on the step. She leaves an uncomfortable amount of space between them considering how close they got last night. Her eyes search Carol’s face. “You got angrier than I did.”

    “Yeah, well.” Carol shrugs, trying for casual, feeling anything but. “I have less patience for assholes.”

    Zosia shakes her head. “That’s not it.” Her gaze drops briefly to Carol’s mouth, then lifts again. Carol notices. “You went for him the second he started in on me. You didn’t even let it roll.”

    “He was wasting our time, Zosia,” Carol says, but the excuse sounds thin even to her own ears.

    Zosia’s lips curve, slow and knowing. “Sure. Strictly about time management.”

    Carol feels the heat crawl up the back of her neck. Only Zosia could get her to blush in a situation like this; always finding the cracks and pressing gently until something gives.

    “You hold your own just fine,” Carol says, a little gruff. “You shouldn’t need anyone to—“

    “I know I don’t,” Zosia interjects, soft but firm. She moves in closer now. Almost flush to Carol’s side, but not completely. Enough to spark the heat between them. “That doesn’t mean I didn’t like watching you do it.”

    Silence settles, thick and charged. Carol’s pulse kicks up, thudding in her ears. She hasn’t misread that, has she? The warmth in Zosia’s voice, the way her eyes lingered a little too long?

    “When you called him careless,” Zosia’s voice is hushed, “you sounded…scary.”

    “Is that supposed to be a compliment or a complaint?”

    “It was…impressive.”

    The word hits Carol harder than it should. She’s been called a lot of things in her career: dogged, intense, intimidating. And impressive. But coming from Zosia, it feels different. Like a hand on the centre of her chest, steadying and unsteadying all at once.

    Carol looks away for a second, focusing on the crumpled up piece of paper on the floor, a trail of dirt left behind by someone’s shoes, anything that isn’t the heat of Zosia’s eyes.

    “We needed him off his script,” Carol says, “and he only slipped when he felt cornered.”

    “And you like it,” Zosia says, “when they slip.”

    Carol’s mouth tilts into a humourless half-smile. “Of course I do.”

    Zosia hesitates, then shifts in that last inch, shoulders and knees pressed together. It’s nothing and everything at the same time. Carol goes still, aware of every single point of contact, of how easy it would be to turn, to lean, to close the distance that had begged to be closed last night.

    “You know what I liked?” Zosia asks, voice low.

    Carol swallows. “Tell me.”

    “The way you looked at him when he called me sweetheart.” There’s a thread of delight in Zosia’s tone now, something fierce and intimate. “He thought he’d found a button. He didn’t realize the button he was pushing was yours.”

    Carol rubs her palms over her knees. “I don’t like men talking down to my partner,” she says, unintentionally emphasizing the word ‘my.’

    Zosia’s breath hitches so slightly that Carol almost misses it. “I also like it when you call me your partner.”

    “Well, that’s what the paperwork says.” Carol forces her voice to stay steady.

    Zosia tilts her head, studying her. “Is that all it is?”

    The question sits there, naked an dangerous. They might as well have already crossed the line even if nothing has actually happened; even if it feels like everything has happened. Carol’s tongue feels thick in her mouth. Half a dozen answers crowd her throat, some safe, some far from it. Before she can pick one, Zosia rescues her and ruins her in the same breath.

    “For the record, it was…extremely attractive watching you dismantle him piece by piece.”

    Carol’s heartbeat stutters, then surges. “Attractive,” she echoes.

    “Mhm.” Zosia’s gaze flickers down to Carol’s lips again, this time slowly and not at all subtle. “Sexy, even.”

    The stairwell feels smaller than ever and Carol wishes they were anywhere but at work right now. Again, she becomes acutely aware of the fact that if anyone were to open the door right now, they would see a Detective and Lieutenant sitting way too close together, breathing just a little too shallow. She should move back. Say something flippant. Di-fucking-fuse.

    Instead, she tilts her head down and pulls Zosia’s gaze back up and holds it, feeling the slow, inexorable pull in her chest.

    “Zosia,” she says gently, a warning to herself as much as anything.

    Zosia simply smiles, soft and genuine, but undoubtably edged with hunger. “Relax, Sturka. I’m not going to jump you in the stairwell.”

    The image flashes through Carol’s mind so vividly that it almost steals her breath. She swallows it down, along with the entirely inappropriate responses that want to follow.

    “Good,” Carol manages. “HR would have a field day.”

    “Terrible paperwork,” Zosia agrees, amusement sparking in her eyes.

    They sit together for a moment longer, allowing the tension to simmer between them like a live wire. Then Zosia straightens as if she can tell they’re both on the precipice again of doing something that can’t be taken back.

    “Come on,” Zosia says, businesslike again, though the warmth never leaves her voice. “Let’s log the interview while I can still remember every time you made him flinch.”

    Carol breathes. “I wasn’t keeping score.”

    “I was,” Zosia says standing up now. “I liked the part where you called him careless. And the part where you reminded him his license was suspended. And the part where you said we’d continue when he remembered how to talk like a grown man.”

    “That was for your benefit,” Carol says before she can stop herself.

    Zosia leans a hip on the railing in front of Carol and Carol has to look all the way up at her. Her smile is small but blazing.

    “I know,” Zosia says, “that’s why I liked it.”

    Tentatively, she holds her hand out for Carol, palm up, no pressure. Before Carol can think better of it, she reaches forward and lets her hand fall into Zosia’s. Their fingers wrap around each other warmly and Zosia pulls her to her feet.

    Carol swears she can feel their pulses thrumming together.

    It’s not until they’ve made it up the stairs and Zosia is about to pull the door open that they let go but not before giving Carol’s hand a deliberate squeeze. The cool air that follows in the wake of the warmth of Zosia’s hand sends a line of goosebumps up her arm.

    Zosia steps out first and doesn’t look back, but Carol doesn’t need her to. She can feel the connection, thin as a tripwire, strong as steel, and it’s going to snap eventually.

    She can already hear the echo of it.

     

    *****

     

    Carol studies the evidence board in the briefing room with great intensity. Pictures of Zoe Diaz and Jason Chan pinned to one side, and Nacho Varga and Eddie Langdon on the other. In between there are still so many spaces to the filled. She reads the case files over and over again until all the words start to lose meaning. Zosia’s been in meetings with the Chief all day so it’s just Carol and her trusty yellow coffee mug.

    That’s when Manousos pops his head in the room.

    “Hey, got a minute?” he asks.

    Carol looks over at him. “For you, amigo, I have two.”

    Not only does Manousos close the door behind him, but he locks it too and the silence that follows him forces a confused laugh out of Carol. “What, are you gonna fire me or something?”

    Manousos folds his arms and doesn’t sit. Telling enough. “I’d have to be your boss to be able to do that, but I’m going to ask you something straight up.”

    Carol tilts her head, wary, though she might have an idea what he might say. “Okay…”

    “For the love of God, what is going on with you and Zosia?”

    Yep. There it is. The question she’s been rehearsing non-answers for. Carol’s mouth opens, then closes. “What do you mean ‘what’s going on’?”

    Manousos raises an eyebrow, impatient. “Don’t play coy. You two flirt like you’re auditioning for a fucking rom-com. The only thing missing is background music.”

    Carol scoffs, feeling the blush in her cheeks. “Manny—“

    “You guys are not hiding it as well as you think. You are, cómo se dice, eye-fucking. And if I can see it, others will too.”

    Carol stares at him, bewildered, as if he wasn’t hitting the nail on the head. “There’s nothing to see,” she says, her voice cracking on ‘nothing.’

    Manousos doesn’t buy it. Of course he doesn’t. “It’s so obvious. You exchange a look, the room changes. She makes you laugh and everyone notices. You haven’t laughed like that since…” his voice trails off.

    Helen. Carol’s body burns alive. Shame, defensiveness, and something else she’s not willing to name right now. “You’re imagining it,” she says unconvincingly.

    Manousos exhales through his nose. “Carol, you are a lot of things, but you are not a good liar.”

    That stings, mostly because it’s true. Carol looks down at her hands, at the faint smudge of ink on her thumb, then her expression softens, her armour slipping. “I don’t even know what it is,” she finally whispers. “We…get each other, I guess. It’s not like…there’s nothing we’ve said out loud. It’s just…” she catches herself, pressing her lips together.

    He waits.

    “Gravity, or something,” she murmurs to herself. “I keep telling myself I’m in control, but I don’t think I am.”

    The room falls silent and Manousos nods slowly. Understandingly. “Look, I’m not here to shame you for having feelings.” He steps forwards and places his hands on Carol’s arms. “I just want you to be careful. This is your career. Her career. Don’t make a mess of it.”

    Carol gives him a half smile that doesn’t quite reach her eyes. “You know, you were the one who told me to give her a chance, so really this is your fault.”

    “Yeah, I know,” Manousos grins now, letting go of Carol. “And you’re welcome. Because isn’t this the better alternative than hating her?”

    Carol rolls her eyes at him, but as always, he’s always fucking right. “Whatever,” she huffs.

    “Anyways,” he says heading towards the door, “I will pick you up tonight at seven for Kenny’s retirement party. Be ready.”

    “Right,” Carol nods. She had almost forgotten with everything that happened this week. “See you at seven.”

    With that, Manousos leaves her. It’s then, alone in the quiet with her thoughts, the case files and coffee cold on the table, that it finally dawns on Carol that this…thing…with Zosia isn’t a game anymore.

    It’s real.

    +

    Carol stands inside her walk-in closet, hands on her hips, as if all the clothes in there have personally offended her. Work clothes, jeans, blouses; so many clothes, none of them right. The venue is nowhere special or elegant, just a local bar not too far from the office, but it’s the first time in a while she’s had a reason to dress up so she wants to do it right. She tugs out a black sweater, frowns, and tosses it to the floor. A floral dress: too much. A silk blouse: too obvious. It’s not really about looking good for the party. It’s about respect for Kenny, and appearances, and—

    She sighs. She’s not fooling anyone. It is absolutely about Zosia.

    Half an hour later, the floor looks like a boutique after a clearance sale. Carol sits in the middle of it surrounded by yes-maybes and absolutely-nots. Finally, she finds a simple green dress that she had only ever worn once to Val’s engagement party, the one with the deep side slit, and short enough that falls to the top of her knees, but rides a little higher when she sits down. And the top half, sleeveless, plunges just deep enough that it means she won’t be able to wear a bra with it.

    By the time she finishes her hair, soft waves framing her face, and swipes on a touch of lipstick, and earrings, she barely recognizes herself. Not flashy by any means, but definitely trying. She exhales into the mirror, equal parts dread and excitement buzzing beneath her ribs.

    When the doorbell rings, she slips on her heels to find Manousos leaning in the doorway, appraising her with one arched eyebrow. Even he deserves a double take.

    “Dios mío,” he says, teasing. “If I didn’t know better, I’d think you were going on a date tonight and not to a retirement party.”

    Carol rolls her eyes, but can’t stop her grin. “Don’t start.”

    “Oh, I’ve already started,” he says, gesturing to her outfit. “The dress, the hair, the…neckline. Very strategic, by the way. This has ‘I’m going to see Zosia in about thirty minutes’ written all over it.”

    She tries to protest it, but it falls short. She ends up half laughing, a nervous fluttering tightening in her stomach. “It’s not too much, is it? Maybe she won’t even notice.”

    “Oh, Carol,” he says as they head out. “If she doesn’t, she’s legally blind.”

    +

    The party is already buzzing and alive when Carol and Manousos arrive. Laughter rolls from the tables near the bar while ‘80s rock songs play loudly through the speakers. Someone had tied a silver “HAPPY RETIREMENT!” banner over a crooked dart board, next to the billiards table, and half the department seems to already be at least one drink in.

    “Guess we got here right on time,” Manousos says handing Carol a drink. Whiskey on the rocks. “Looks like Schrader is already on his third beer.”

    Carol takes a sip of her drink, then sets it on the bar. She tells herself she isn’t nervous as she scans her eyes around the room, but doesn’t see the person she’s looking for. Then, she smoothes a hand over her dress feeling the hem brush just at her knee, and hopes no one had noticed the way she had checked her reflection twice on the way in. The top feels a little lower than she’d normally risk, and her heels a bit too high, but still, it feels nice—intentional.

    First, she spots Kenny, who wasn’t hard to spot at all, surrounded by well-wishers, laughing and smiling as someone hands him another envelope and Carol makes a mental note to make her way over there when the crowd disperses.

    Then, as she’s in the middle of a conversation with Rick and Margaret, she feels the air shift.

    It isn’t anything dramatic, but it’s enough. That small, inexplicable pull that makes her glance towards the door, and then her breath catches.

    Zosia has arrived.

    She stands in the doorway, light from the street haloing her as she pauses to take in the room. And good lord. Carol has only one thought and it’s that she looks like a dream that forgot to fade.

    Her short, red strapless dress clings to her body like a secret; her open loose blazer adding just enough edge. Then her legs, God, her legs, bare and endless in those heels, luminous in the bar’s amber light. Then when Carol’s eyes meet the delicate expanse of her collarbone, the soft rise of skin and breath, it sends her short circuiting. Tonight she’s all contradiction: softness carved from fire, power wrapped in poise.

    Zosia doesn’t just walk into the room, she changes the temperature.

    Her gaze sweeps across the bar, pausing the moment it finds Carol. She smiles slowly and knowingly, the kind that makes Carol’s heart trip, and then she starts walking. Heads turn, conversations lull, but Zosia only sees her.

    Maybe a coincidence, maybe it’s not, but Rick and Margaret disappear.

    Carol forgets how to speak. Her fingers tighten around her glass in reflex as she brings it to her lips, pretending not to notice the stares, or the quiet ripple of interest Zosia leaves in her wake as she makes her way across the room.

    Zosia’s gaze doesn’t waver once. It paints over Carol with agonizingly slow, deliberate intent; her mouth curves slightly as her eyes dip, lingering where they shouldn’t, but where Carol wants. The heat in that look is unmistakable. It’s shameless, yes, but there’s something tender beneath it like she wants to feel seen, not just looked at.

    When she finally stops in front of Carol, her smile is sin and sunlight all at once; and her lipstick is a shade of red that Carol can only dream to find smudged on the inside of her thigh.

    “Hi,” Zosia says, smooth as honey.

    Carol’s heart batters against her ribcage. The bass, the chatter, Kenny’s laughter; all of it blurs into the background. She can only focus on the scent of Zosia’s perfume, which is different tonight; it’s earthier, woodier. And then there’s the way her dress is so tight it leaves almost nothing to Carol’s imagination.

    “Wow,” Carol manages to squeak out. It comes out low; too rough and exposed.

    Zosia’s grin widens just a fraction more. She leans in close over the music, the ends of her hair brushing at Carol’s shoulder, a hand low on the small of her back, as she whispers into her ear, “You look fucking incredible.”

    Carol swallows hard, praying that her knees won’t give way. Still, there’s a tenderness behind it all that disarms her completely, cutting through all the charge and heat with a quiet sincerity.

    “And you…” Carol inches back only enough to let her eyes flicker over Zosia helplessly. “Jesus, Zosia,” she whispers, “you didn’t have to dress like that.”

    Zosia can’t wipe the smile off her face, her eyes fluttering down to Carol’s lips. “I did,” she says gently, “and I’m glad you like it.”

    +

    The music from the bar’s side speakers continues to pulse loudly into the night. Kenny is holding court by the cake table, flushed red with scotch and attention, while somehow, Carol and Zosia have found themselves trapped at a high-top table with Rick and Jeff.

    Rick is describing, in microscopic detail, his new patio renovation. Carol, propped on one elbow, nods along with feigned attention.

    “Concrete slabs,” Rick is saying, “you wouldn’t believe what a difference they make for drainage.”

    “God, I live for good drainage,” Carol deadpans.

    Jeff barks a laugh. “See, that’s what I always say.”

    Across from her, Zosia presses her lips together to hide a chuckle. She’s clutching a sweating gin and tonic, nodding politely, but her eyes keep drifting to Carol. And to her mouth. And to her chest. Every time she catches herself, she takes a sip to cover it.

    Carol notices and each time, she watches as Zosia tilts her head back, the gentle swallow of her throat, and then the way she licks her lips. It drives Carol crazy.

    “So, then,” Rick goes on, gesturing with his beer bottle. “I figure I’ll add a pergola. Shade in the summer, you know?”

    “Romantic,” Carol mutters, eyes flicking to Zosia for half a second too long. “I mean, uh, good for barbecues.”

    Zosia’s smile betrays her. “Yeah. Very…practical.”

    Jeff jumps in. “Rick’s all about practicality. That’s why my wife makes me do all the decorating.” He grins, oblivious.

    Carol laughs a bit too loud, and Zosia’s laugh follows, softer but breathless, like a match striking.

    For the next few moments, the chatter blurs. Kenny’s booming voice across the bar, someone dropping a glass, Rick still talking. Carol’s pulse feels too close to her skin. Zosia’s fingers brush condensation on her glass, tracing idle shapes. When Carol’s gaze settles on it, she looks away quickly, pretending to check her phone.

    Then somewhere under the table, their ankles brush together when Carol shifts in her seat and Zosia looks at her, eyes smoldering. The contact is soft and warm and Zosia only moves her foot closer, and Carol lets her.

    Rick says something about mulch. Jeff nods solemnly. Zosia tries to contribute; a harmless comment about soil acidity, maybe. But Carol interrupts with another joke, anything to pull Zosia’s eyes back.

    They both know the game they’re playing. Pretending to listen. Pretending to belong in this conversation. Pretending not to stare.

    And God help these two idiots, failing spectacularly.

    Rick has moved onto talking about power washing, and even Jeff looks like he wants to escape. Carol catches Zosia’s eyes over the rim of her glass and a flicker of silent agreement passes between them: we’ve suffered enough.

    “I’m gonna grab another drink,” Carol says suddenly, setting her whiskey, mostly just melted ice now, onto the table.

    Jeff raises a finger. “Oh, if you’re going—“

    “I’ll come with you,” Zosia cuts in, already setting down her now empty glass. Her voice sounds almost too casual for it to actually be casual.

    They stand a little bit too closely at the bar, but it’s fine because they just blame it on the crowd of people pushing them together.

    “Jeff looks ready to chew his arm off back there,” Carol murmurs closely into Zosia.

    Zosia turns and smiles at her. “Poor guy. He was just trying to be polite. You kept cutting Rick off mid-sentence.”

    “Please, that was mercy.”

    Their laughter mixes, quick and electric. Then, as they wait to be noticed by a bartender, Zosia glances all the way down Carol’s dress, her eyes feasting. “This is a bold choice to wear to a retirement party,” she says, her fingers brushing down the fabric of Carol’s plunging neckline, dangerously close to bare skin.

    Carol nervously flits her eyes around, looking to see if anyone’s watching. “It’s just a dress,” she breathes.

    Then before she realizes what she’s doing, she slowly pulls one side of Zosia’s blazer back to shamelessly get a better look at her dress (and other things) and it exposes a small freckle on the upper curve of her breast. It’s so bold of her the when she looks back up at Zosia, Zosia is biting the corner of the lip and Carol almost says ‘fuck’ out loud.

    “It’s not just a dress,” Zosia says so lowly that her voice feels like it belongs to Carol. “You know exactly what you’re doing.”

    Carol lets out a small, incredulous laugh, but she knows Zosia sees through it. “I’m just standing at a bar.”

    “Mhm,” Zosia’s hand slides lightly over Carol’s arm, hidden by the bar, and then back to her side as if she’s testing how much she can actually get away with. “Very innocent.”

    Carol feels hot again, her pulse hammering low in her stomach and between her legs. “Need I remind you this is still a work function.”

    “Oh, I know.”

    That should be the end of it, but the crowd pushes them even closer together and now they’re practically hip to hip. And then, almost casually, almost instinctively, Zosia reaches up to tuck a loose strand of hair behind Carol’s ear.

    The touch is so light that it’s almost painful.

    Carol holds her breath. She looks past Zosia a little more urgently now, over the heads of the crowd, quickly checking the room. They’re surrounded by colleagues and Chief Schrader is a little too close for comfort, even if he’s lost to his own world. Carol feels the heat intensifying within her.

    Zosia notices.

    “Everything okay?” she asks, half innocent, half wicked amusement.

    Carol stares at her. “You know exactly what you’re doing.”

    “I don’t exactly see you objecting.”

    Carol opens her mouth to say something, but the bartender turns towards them. She orders quickly for the both of them. Another whiskey for herself, and a gin and tonic for Zosia. She doesn’t even ask Zosia, just assumes, but Zosia doesn’t seem to mind at all.

    “You’re blushing,” Zosia says after the bartender has their order.

    “I am not.”

    “You are.”

    After a few moments, the bartender hands them their drinks and they decide it’s best for the next little while they mingle with other people because people are starting to stare. Carol finds Kenny who is more than happy to exchange stories about his nearly fifty years in the force, while Zosia finds herself just mingling with whoever is willing.

    The evening settles over Zosia’s apartment in slow, amber layers, the last of the daylight thinning at the edges of the windows until the glass reflects more of the room than the city beyond it.

    Carol stands at the sink with her hands braced on either side of it, staring at nothing in particular while the kettle on the stove gives a low, nearly forgotten hum.

Touches that lasted the right amount of time.

    She had meant to leave after coffee.

    Then after breakfast.

    Then after that second cup because she couldn’t bear to leave Zosia alone with a sink full of dishes and Carol had fallen into helping without thinking about it.

    Then after lunch, which had been sandwiches delivered from a nearby deli, enjoyed on the couch, half dressed in a post-coital bliss.

    It had turned into a lazy conversation where Zosia had laughed at something Carol said, and where Carol had watched the sunlight move across her face and thought, with a kind of stunned softness, that she could stay like this all day.

    Which she had.

    Too easily. Too completely.

    Now it’s evening and the first edge of reality has finally begun to cut through the warmth.

    Carol looks down at her hands and realizes she’s been clenching them. She makes herself loosen her fingers. She’s spent years knowing how to keep herself contained, how to keep work and feeling from crossing over into one another, and still this had happened so naturally and quickly she doesn’t even notice the moment she had stopped guarding the line.

    And that’s the problem.

    Not what they’d done. Not really.

    It’s how easy it had been to forget what comes after.

    “Carol.” Zosia’s voice is gentle, low enough to keep from breaking the quiet completely.

    Carol turns. Zosia is leaning against the far counter, one hip angled into it, watching her with that calm, unflinching focus she carries so well at work. But this isn’t the Lieutenant standing in a briefing room. This is something softer. Bare-shouldered under an old grey shirt, hair loose now instead of pinned back, expression open in a way Carol is still getting used to.

    “Hmm?” Carol hums, though she already knows what Zosia is going to say.

    “You’re worrying.”

    Carol lets out a breath that’s almost a laugh, except it carries no humour in it. “Is it that obvious?”

    Zosia’s mouth tilts. “It’s that obvious.”

    Carol looks away again, toward the darkening window. “I didn’t mean to stay this long.”

    “I know.”

    “I should’ve gone home hours ago.”

    “You could have.”

    That answer makes her look back. Zosia’s tone isn’t accusing, just a simple statement, but it lands with the strange tenderness of someone refusing to make her feel bad for what it is, at bottom, a shared choice.

    Carol swallows. “It just felt easy.”

    Zosia’s face softens at that. “It was easy.”

    Carol tries for a laugh, but it’s thin. “That’s what scares me.”

    The words hang in the kitchen between them.

    Zosia pushes off the counter and crosses the room, stopping close but not crowding her, giving Carol enough space to retreat if she wants to. She doesn’t. Zosia’s hand settles lightly over Carol’s wrist, where her pulse has been beating a little too fast for the last ten minutes.

    “Tomorrow is tomorrow,” Zosia says.

    Carol’s eyes flick to hers. “We work together tomorrow.”

    “I know.”

    “You’re my Lieutenant. And partner.”

    “I know that too.”

    Carol’s throat tightens. She hates how much that matters, hates that it matters at all, because it changes the shape of everything. Not what she feels, not what had already happened, but the consequences of it. The careful way they’ll have to move around each other. The possibility that every look, every pause, every extra beat of silence might become something she’ll have to guard against.

    “I’m serious,” Carol says quietly. “This isn’t a small thing.”

    “No,” Zosia says, and her thumb moves once against Carol’s wrist. “It isn’t. But it doesn’t have to break anything.”

    Carol searches her face, needing more than calm words. Need makes her wary. “You say that now.”

    Zosia holds her gaze. “I say that because I can feel you spiralling, and you don’t have to.”

    That lands harder than Carol expects because it’s too close to the truth.     Zosia’s hand slides from her wrist to her fingers, threading lightly through them. “We’re adults,” she says. “We’re careful when we need to be. And tomorrow, we go to work like we always do.”

    Carol gives a small, skeptical shake of her head. “You make it sound so simple.”

    “It isn’t simple,” Zosia admits. “It’s just manageable.”

    Carol lets that sit for a moment.

    The apartment is quieter now. The kettle has gone still on the stove. The city outside has deepened into evening, windows in neighbouring buildings glowing one by one. Everything in the room seems to have softened around the edges, as if the day were gently letting them go.

    Carol looks down at their joined hands. “I don’t want it to be weird.”

    “It won’t be.” Zosia’s voice is certain enough to make Carol look up again. “Not unless we make it weird.”

    “And you’re sure you can do that?” Carol asks, unable to keep the doubt out of her voice. “Separate it?”

    Zosia’s expression changes then, just slightly, enough for Carol to see the reluctance beneath her composure. “No,” she says. “Not easily. But I can try. For us.”

    For us.

    Carol doesn’t know if Zosia intends for that to sound as heavy as it does, but it settles warm and painful at once. Carol has no answer for it. She only feels the room shrink around them, as if the apartment itself understands what it means to be standing at the edge of something neither of them can even name yet.

    She reaches for Zosia before she can think too long about it, her hand rising to Zosia’s cheek. Zosia leans into the touch immediately, eyes closing for just a second as if she’s been waiting all day for this exact contact, as if Carol’s hands hadn’t already been all over her since last night.

    There’s a confusing ache in Carol’s chest.

    “I don’t want to leave,” she admits.

    Zosia gives her a quiet, almost helpless smile. “I don’t want you to either.”

    That should have made it easier. It doesn’t. It only makes the truth sharper.

    Carol lets her hand drop, then turns toward the small chair by the wall where her coat and shoes are. She slips her arms into the coat and stands there for a second, buttoning it slowly, as if stalling might somehow buy her a different outcome. It doesn’t. The evening keeps moving.

    Zosia comes to stand in front of her again, close enough that Carol can feel her warmth. “Text me when you get home.”

    Carol nods.

    “Promise?”

    Carol huffed out a breath, something between exasperation and affection. “Yes, Lieutenant.”

    Zosia’s smile is affectionate. “Thank you, Detective.”

    Carol should have stepped back then but instead she stays where she is, looking at Zosia as if memorizing her in the lamplight, the soft line of her mouth, the tiredness gathering at the corners of her eyes, the way she still looks at Carol like there’s nowhere else in the room she’d rather be.

    It’s then that she leans up on her tiptoes and kisses Zosia. She means for it to be quick as casual, but if today has taught Carol anything, nothing between them is quick and casual. It’s not heated by any means, just lingers in a way that nearly gives Carol the impulse to stay.

    But the world exists outside this apartment. Morning would come. Work would come with it. The questions would come too, maybe silent, maybe not.

    So she forces herself towards the door.

    Zosia follows her as far as the entryway and Carol pulls the door open and the cool air from the hall slips in, carrying the faint smell of old carpet and city dust.

    “Goodnight, Carol,” Zosia says.

    “Goodnight, Zosia,” Carol echoes.

    Carol steps out into the hall and the door closes behind her with a soft click. And as she walks down the corridor toward the elevator, she tries not to imagine tomorrow at work; tries not to picture Zosia across a conference table, across a case file, across this invisible line they had just spent a day pretending wasn’t there.

    She fails.

    But for the first time, there was also something else beneath the worry.

    The memory of Zosia’s hand in hers.

    The certainty in her voice.

    We’ll be okay.

     

     

    Chapter 8: "every act of restraint seems to create its opposite" - nietzche

    Chapter Notes

    The world outside is still half asleep when Zosia wakes up. Pale violet light creeps through the bedroom window, tracing soft lines over her sheets. She sighs quietly as she turns onto her side, the familiar rasp of linens against her skin grounding her in the moment.

Even if just for a night.

    Back in Las Vegas, Carol had been more of an urban legend among detectives: the woman who could unspool a crime scene like a thread, who’d earned commendations that Zosia could only dream of. Figures and reputations are easy to admire from a distance, but Zosia could not have anticipated the woman beneath it. The dry humour and lightning fast wit that slips through the cracks, the way she listens like every word matters. And just her pure unadulterated passion.

    Now with Carol’s scent wrapping around her like warmth in the morning chill, Zosia feels something shift deeper than attraction or infatuation. A small, careful hope begins to unfurl; a possibility that this could be more than just one night. That maybe, if Carol lets her, it could mean something real.

    Eventually, Zosia smiles faintly into the quiet, heart racing, unwilling to let go of the pillow.

    “Sure,” Carol answers coolly. “I’ll be there in a minute.”

    Zosia quirks an eyebrow up. “Really? It’s almost five. I haven’t seen you since lunch.”

    Carol opens her mouth to protest, but then just lets out a nervous breath. She feels the air thickening around her.

    “Fine, I have,” she admits with very little pushing. She takes a step back and leans into the window, the blinds crunching behind her back. She covers her face with her hands. “God, I’m a fucking train wreck.”

    Carol’s pulse thunders. Zosia says the words like they mean more, and of course they do. This isn’t just about partnership, it’s about trust.

    “I know,” Zosia says quietly. “And just for the record, because I know you’ve been thinking about it, I haven’t regretted any part of it. Not for a second.”

    Zosia exhales slowly, letting her gaze drift to Carol’s mouth for just a second too long. “More than you think. You don’t have to worry.”

    Carol’s composure breaks, she smiles breathlessly, still just shy of tears and she hates herself for that. “That’s cruel,” she mutters. “You saying things like that when I’m supposed to act professional.”

    Carol leans forward a fraction, voice soft and daring. “Then maybe I shouldn’t stop?”

    The quiet holds them, charged and intimate for the first time all day, until Zosia clears her throat, glancing towards the open blinds.

    “Yes, Lieutenant.” Carol nods slow and deliberate before turning to leave. The reflection in the glass shows Zosia watching her just a moment longer than perhaps what professionalism would allow.

    —

    Zosia stays perfectly still until the latch clicks behind Carol. Only then does she breathe. The façade she’d worn, the calm authority, the Lieutenant’s poise, slips just enough for her to press her palms against the filing cabinet, grounding herself.

    Through the half-open blinds, she watches Carol out in the bullpen, chatting away with Manousos and Koumba. The careful way she tucks a strand of hair behind her ear, the faint smile she tries to suppress. Zosia feels the tug of it across the room, subtle, invisible, unmistakable.

    Her eyes drift back to the paperwork spread before her. Policy reports, evidence summaries, procedural memos. Dull, rigid things meant to define boundaries. These boundaries are supposed to keep her safe, keep them both safe.

    But the weekend’s softness has rewritten all her instincts: the way Carol had looked at her, whispered her name like she’d been waiting years to say it.

    Zosia sits in her chair and leans back, fingers tapping lightly against the armrest. For the first time in a long while, control doesn’t feel like strength; it feels like armour.

    She glances at the bullpen again. Carol laughs at something someone says; a sound too bright, too easy after the tension of earlier. Relief floods Zosia. Carol is still herself. Maybe that means they’ll be okay.

    Still, Zosia can’t quite shake the magnetic ache of awareness lingering between them. The memory of Carol’s smile across her desk haunts her like static in the air.

    Professional distance is necessary; emotional distance is impossible.

    She closes the file she hasn’t read, tilts her head just enough to see Carol through the glass one more time, and allows herself a small, private smile, the kind she’d never let out in public.

    Tomorrow, she promises herself, she’ll be better at this. More composed. More Lieutenant than lover.

    But the truth settles quietly beneath that thought.

    She doesn’t want to be.

     

    *****

     

    The copy machine whines a flat, mechanical note, painfully slow and loud of enough to pass for mockery. Zosia leans against the counter, arms folded, watching Carol hover over this Godforsaken machine like she can will it into working.

    “If you’re implying that I jammed the machine again, I can assure you I did not.” Carol turns, smiling faintly. She shakes the machine as if that’s going to make it cooperate. “You’d think that someone could invent a copier that doesn’t take three minutes per page.”

    Zosia shrugs. “Technological stagnation. One of humanities eternal flaws.”

    Carol lifts an eyebrow back. “How poetic of you.”

    “I contain multitudes.” Zosia grins because Carol laughs and the sound snaps Zosia’s composure like fragile plastic. It feels almost unjust, how something so brief could reopen every tactile memory.

    Carol’s smile flickers, the kind that never really fully leaves her eyes. “You know, this is…dangerous.”

    “Paper cuts and toner explosions?” Zosia offers, because humour feels safer than honesty right now.

    “Not what I meant,” Carol murmurs.

    There’s a small, electric pause that follows. Zosia steps forward without meaning to, drawn by Carol’s familiar edge between professionalism and want.

    “Every act of restraint seems to create its opposite,” Zosia says. It comes out too soft, too philosophical, and definitely too revealing.

    “You think I don’t read?”

    “Not what I said.”

    “Desperate times,” Zosia replies softly. “Distraction appears inevitable.”

    Carol looks at her then and Zosia knows she’s in trouble. The gaze carries recognition. The same ache Zosia has been living with since they last kissed four days ago.

    Zosia takes another step closer, stopping just shy of her reach, the space between them burning. “Over coffee or Socratic dialogue?”

    —

    Zosia’s been cataloguing photographs for nearly an hour, and every single one of them looks like Carol. Not literally, but in essence. Every sharp line, every subtle colour contrast, every fragment of order born from chaos reminds her of Carol’s mind: methodical, deliberate, precise to the point of torment.

    Zosia: Can you come down to the evidence room? Need a hand

    Carol: With what exactly?

    Zosia stares at the text, half smiling. Of course Carol would see through it.

    They don’t speak for a few seconds; just stand there, hearts still racing, lips swollen, minds spinning. When the door still doesn’t open, Zosia exhales, a shaky laugh escaping before she can stop it. She steps close to Carol again, her hand loosely around her wrist.

    Carol breathing is still shallow, her face scarlet. “Somehow I knew you didn’t need help cataloguing fingerprints.”

    “Not fingerprints,” Zosia says, forcing the calm back into her voice. “Just touch.”

    Carol’s gaze softens at that, and Zosia can see the same war playing behind her eyes. Desire tangled with practicality, affection dressed in restraint.

    Zosia tells Carol to leave first, the scent of her perfume lingering in her wake. She sits on the same table, grinning and frustrated and alive.

    Her willpower had survived. Barely.

     

    +

     

    Carol’s knife slices through a red pepper with satisfying precision, yet she barely registers the motion. Her hands work on autopilot while she tries to distract her thoughts with a rerun of Friends playing on the TV in the background.

    Anything to not think about Zosia because it’s starting to become unhealthy.

    It doesn’t work, but she’s not even surprised.

    She tosses the pepper strips into the pan and frowns when the oil pops. All week, she’s been trying—failing, really—to make sense of whatever this thing between them has become. It’s not just attraction anymore. Attraction would be simpler. She knows how to flirt, she knows how light it can stay. But with Zosia, from day one, it had never been light.

    Yes, they flirt all day. The repartee, the sideways glances, the coded jokes that no one else understands, but each word carries the weight of something deeper.

    And there’s that word again: gravity. When Zosia looks at her, it leaves Carol spinning for hours.

    She stirs the pan too fast, splattering oil onto the counter. “Pull yourself together,” she mutters, wiping it with a paper towel. “You’re not sixteen.”

    Yet it feels exactly like that. Like the kind of ridiculous, involuntary crush she would have mocked in other people, except now she’s the one staying late under the guise of post-lab work just for the possibility of spending more time with Zosia.

    Her phone buzzes.

    Carol freezes, spatula midair. Then, absurdly, her stomach flips. She tries not to smile. Fails.

    Zosia: Made it home?

    Short and simple, yet somehow intimate. Carol stares at the words for a beat before replying.

    Carol: Yeah. Making dinner

    The reply comes instantly.

    Zosia: Don’t forget to feed your brain too. You’ve been running on caffeine and brilliance all week

    Carol feels her face warm. It’s sweet, infuriatingly sweet. The sort of genuine, slow-burn thoughtfulness that Zosia slips into conversation as if she weren’t causing chaos with every syllable.

    Carol: That’s a very flattering diagnosis coming from you

    Another message appears before she can even set her phone back onto the counter.

    Zosia: True things don’t need to be flattering

    Carol draws in a breath, letting it out slowly. The words are nothing extraordinary. No confession, no promise. Yet they still land deep in her chest.

    She leans back against the counter, smiling despite herself, despite the confusion that tangles her insides. Whatever this is, it’s not casual. It’s dizzying, magnetic, a connection she could have never prepared herself for.

    The pan sizzles behind her.

    Carol closes her eyes and wonders if love can start this way. Inconvenient and irresistible, disguised as intellect and laughter, arriving by text while the vegetables burn.

     

    *****

    “Hi, Lieutenant,” Olivia says a little bit softer, a little bit sweeter. “You must be here for the DNA work-up from the Langdon case?” Her eyes quickly flick up and down Zosia. “It’s Zosia, right?”

    Zosia gives her a look somewhere in between surprised and impressed, and then offers her an easy nod. “I guess my reputation precedes me.”

    “Oh, it does,” Olivia says, leaning forward with a grin so bright that it just about blinds Carol. “I saw your name on the file last night and took the liberty of rushing them myself. Didn’t want to keep you waiting.”

    Zosia laughs, low and charming. “We appreciate that. You must be swamped.”

    “Always time for a good detective,” Olivia replies and Carol tries so hard to not roll her eyes that it hurts. Then Olivia slides a clipboard across the counter, entirely too close to Zosia’s hand. Zosia picks up the pen, signs the forms, her mouth quirking slightly as Olivia comments, “You’ve got great handwriting. Neat, confident. Suits you.”

    “Oh, thanks.” Zosia says, chuckling a little. “That’s the nicest thing anyone’s said about my paperwork.”

    Carol’s stomach twists, painfully so. Suits you, really? And she compliments Zosia’s paperwork all the time. She watches Zosia hand the pen back, Olivia’s fingers brushing hers with deliberate slowness. Carol swears if that pen were alive, she would have willed it to lodge itself in the woman’s throat. Choke on that fucking pen, she thinks, signing her own name with her own pen with so much force that it nearly tears the page.

    Olivia hands Zosia an evidence envelope, bright smile still plastered on. “Here you go, Lieutenant. I left my card in there for you with my direct line, you know, in case you have any follow up questions.”

    “I’ll be sure to keep that in mind,” Zosia says, too smooth for her own good.

    I hope you do. God, shut up. She snaps the folder shut before Zosia can say another word. “Thanks. We’ve got everything we need.”
