    +

     

    It’s uncharacteristically dull at the office tonight. The night shift changeover has since been in session for sometime now, but the phones are quiet, and so are the people. Only the hum of the vending machine down the hall and the sudden rainfall outside cuts through the stillness. Carol’s office always feels smaller at night, crowded with files and evidence bags, proof of other people’s lives that somehow never feel as complicated as her own. The blinds are drawn, but the door is cracked open.

    Zosia sits backwards in the visitor’s chair, chin resting on the top of it, sleeves rolled to her elbows. Her laptop glows on Carol’s desk beside her. They had stayed late to go through the case reports that the Chief had left Zosia earlier in the week, but somewhere along the line, their focus slips.

    Carol looks up from her notes from the umpteenth time in a minute and catches Zosia watching her and immediately her cheeks flush. “Why are you looking at me like that?” she asks.

    Zosia smiles and shrugs. “I like watching you work. You’re so…focused.”

    “Oh yeah? You should try it sometime.”

    Zosia lets out a quiet scoff, playfully flicking a paper clip at Carol. “Looks like you’re trying to carve stone again.”

    “Maybe I am,” Carol says, half smiling, despite herself. She taps her pen against the desk, the same one from the lab earlier, then pauses for a moment. “Though I did consider using this as a weapon this morning.”

    Zosia laughs softly, beating her to it. “Against Olivia?”

    “Olivia,” Carol repeats, her face involuntarily wrinkling with disgust, as if the name were poison against her tongue. “She was…enthusiastic.”

    “That’s a kind way to put it.”

    Carol’s jaw tenses. She wants to leave it there, to bury the strange heat that had settled in her chest ever since, but she’s too tired to keep pretending.

    “You knew she was flirting with you.”

    “I did.”

    “And you were flirting back.”

    “I was just being polite,” Zosia says amused, but then quickly changes her tone when she realizes  that Carol actually seems upset. She tilts her head to the side and studies her. “So you’re finally admitting that it bothered you.” It’s less a question, more a statement.

    Carol hesitates, searching for the right words. “Like I’ve been trying so hard to act like whatever this thing is between us doesn’t matter.     Zosia smiles against her lips. “Took you long enough.”

    Carol gives her a half laugh, her heart unsteady in her chest. “You didn’t make it easy.”

    “Maybe I didn’t want to,” Zosia teases, reaching up and brushing a thumb along Carol’s jaw.

    Carol rolls her eyes but doesn’t pull away. “You know, you truly are infuriating.”

    Zosia looks at her as if she’s proud of that. “I know. But at least now you can call me that over dinner.”

    Carol arches an eyebrow. “Are you asking me on a date, Nowak?”

    “Yes, Sturka, I am,” Zosia says grinning. “A real date. Somewhere that doesn’t smell like coffee and toner.”

    “Do we have to eat stale pretzels?”

    “I mean, only if you want to. But I think you deserve better than that.”

    Carol feels something uncoil in her chest after almost ten long years; a rare, unfamiliar lightness. “Then yes,” she says quietly, “I’d like that.”

    Zosia’s smile softens again, this time into something tender. “Good. It’s a date, then.”

    Even though the door is locked, the footsteps outside remind them where they are. Even so, the stillness feels less like silence and more like understanding. Rain taps softly against the window, steady and rhythmic. With one arm still around Zosia, Carol reaches to the side, closing the file folder and Zosia’s laptop, and finally allows herself the smallest, simplest thought:

    It’s okay to want this.

    Chapter 9: countess elzbieta &amp; her champion racing pigeon

    Chapter Summary

    Detective Carol Sturka and Lieutenant Zosia Nowak get silly at the Albuquerque Museum.

    Zosia stares at the egg in her hand for so long she forgets what she was even going to do with it. She’s sock-footed in her kitchen in nothing but an oversized t-shirt and her underwear—she really needs to start wearing pants when she cooks.

    The egg eventually gets replaced by her coffee mug when she remembers its been sitting on the counter behind her, probably lukewarm by now.

    It is.

    Even still, she continues to sip it while she stares ahead, out the balcony window at a flock of passing birds, while feeling like some pathetic lovestruck fool.

    The thing is, Carol has completely consumed her thoughts—in the best way possible.

    It’s just a date, she tells herself. A first date. With a woman who might actually feel out of her league.

    And even more so, it feels almost absurd considering everything that has already happened between them. First dates are supposed to come before tangled sheets and desperate moans in the dark. Before learning the exact cadence of someone’s laugh when they are trying not to be heard through thin walls. Before knowing how they look when they sleep, hair a mess, and eyes softer than they ever are at work.

    And yet, this is still a first date. A real one, out in the open.

    Maybe this is her cosmic balance sheet starting to even out.

    Zosia rounds the kitchen and flicks the TV on for nothing more than just to have background noise, a local news channel chattering away. She sips her coffee again, grimacing at its tepidness, and then walks back to the kitchen, dumping it into the sink. The egg goes forgotten as she refills the kettle, puts away the dishes she had left out last night, and contemplates finally unpacking the cardboard boxes. Spoiler: she doesn’t.

    It’s just that if she lets herself think too hard about Carol, about later, she might—much like Carol—spiral into overthinking and that is a luxury she cannot afford.

    Lieutenants are not supposed to spiral. And they are definitely not supposed to be secretly kissing and sleeping with their partners or subordinates.

    Zosia lets out a long and slow breath, which ends with a quiet laugh as she absently opens a cupboard, looking for nothing in particular. Great judgement, Nowak. Exemplary leadership.

    But even as she thinks it, her mind betrays her, pulling up Carol’s face. The way her eyes crinkle when she smiles or laughs, the way she says “Zosia” like it means something more than just a call across the office.

    God.

    Zosia presses her lips together, shaking her head as if she can physically dislodge the thought. This is a complicated, messy, professional minefield and she doesn’t care, which is a problem. Well, not a problem, per se. Maybe it’s just the truth.

    She moves into her bedroom now, where the morning light pools softly across her dresser. The rest of the apartment is controlled clutter—files, books, a sweater thrown over the back of a chair; but in here the space is different. It’s intentional and quiet.

    Zosia steps close to the photo of her and Aleks, the frame slightly worn at the edges. She smiles every time she looks at it. Six years old forever. Then she opens the dresser and pulls out a glass jar filled with folded scraps of paper, a single tealight candle, and a lighter.

    Her movement is instinctive, practiced after all these years. The soft flick, the brief flare of the flame, the wick catching.

    “Dzień dobry kochanie,” she whispers, her voice gentler than it ever is anywhere else.

    She stands there for a moment, as she does every morning, just watching the flame steady itself.

    Twelve years.

    It still doesn’t feel like a number that belongs in her life. Even to this day, she always wonders what kind of man he would have been. A good one, for sure. But would he have followed in her footsteps, or gone the opposite route? Maybe he’d have been an artist, or a writer. It’s a what-if that will never be answered and it torments her.

    Grief doesn’t fade the way people told her it would, although Zosia has a sneaking suspicion that most of these people haven’t actually experienced the real thing, perhaps only read about it in books.

    It just changes shape; it becomes something she carries differently everyday. Some days it’s a soul-crushing weight clamping down on her chest. Other days it’s quieter, like a shadow that follows her around without asking permission.

    And then there are mornings like this, where it sits besides something else.

    Something bright.

    Zosia reaches for the jar, pulling out one of the folded pieces of paper. She hesitates just for a second, then opens it.

    You would have loved dogs, it reads, her own handwriting slightly uneven from the day she had written it.

    A small smile tugs at her mouth, then her heart. Still true.

    This is something she never skips. No matter how early the shift, no matter how late the night before, no matter if an exquisite woman lay naked, sleeping in her bed.

    A note, a thought, a memory. Something to keep him present in a world that has moved on without asking her.

    She sets the paper back into the jar and exhales slowly. Her mind drifts, unbidden, to the thought of Carol again.

    Ten years for her.

    Different timelines. Different stories. Same kind of silence left behind.

    Zosia had recognized it in her almost immediately even if she had acted like she hadn’t. And it’s not even because Carol had told her on the very first day they had met, which is a little bit crazy when she thinks about it, but then again she had opened her big mouth with some untoward comment, which she still thinks about and regrets.

    But it’s because of the pauses. The way Carol holds herself a little too tightly, like she’s always bracing against something invisible. The way her eyes sometimes go distant in the middle of a conversation and then snap back like nothing had happened.

    Grief recognizes grief.

    And yet somehow, in between stakeouts, paperwork, and awful coffee in the bullpen, they have found something in each other that never needs to be explained. It’s not just attraction, though there’s plenty of that. It’s simply just understanding. A silent kind of permission to feel things they don’t have to justify.

    Zosia sits back on the bed, taking the photo with her. “Is this a terrible idea?” she says softly.

    The little blonde boy in the frame doesn’t offer much in the way of guidance.

    She huffs out a small laugh. “Right. That’s about the response I expected.”

    But the truth is, for the first time in a long time, something doesn’t feel like it’s just about surviving or just getting through.

    Something about Carol makes her feel…awake.

    Not in a dramatic, life altering, movie script kind of way. But in smaller ways. Real ways. Like noticing the way sunlight drifts across the floor; or the pause before someone laughs, when joy is still deciding to exist. Or caring, like actually caring, about what she’s going to wear on a Saturday afternoon to the Albuquerque Museum, and then to dinner afterwards.

    Zosia stands again, placing the photo back onto the dresser. She shakes her head ever so faintly, accompanying it with a reluctant smile.

    Okay, I guess we’re doing this.

    She turns towards her closet, already mentally putting together options. Nothing too obvious. Nothing that screams I spent an hour choosing this, even though she probably will.

    Then after a moment she stops and turns back to the dresser. Her fingers hover near the photo before she lightly taps the frame.

    Wish me luck. Then, after a beat, out loud, “I think you’d like her.”

    And that thought stays with her as she rifles through the closet, the bedroom and apartment filling with soft, ordinary sounds of a morning that for once feels like it might lead somewhere new and exciting.

    —

    It’s just after lunch when Zosia is in her bathroom putting on her make-up. She makes a very conscious decision to not overdo it, just a light dusting of blush, some mascara, and a faint pink lipstick that you’d really have to get up close to see. She has one small, gold hoop earring in when her phone buzzes on the counter.

    Her heartbeat immediately quickens and she grins when Carol’s name appears on the screen, which is crazy because they had already been texting most of the morning. Mostly it had just been Carol teasing her that a cigarette, coffee, and half a protein shake is most definitely not a breakfast of champions.

    Now—

    Carol: So what time are we meeting at the museum?

    Zosia stares at the message incredulously for a moment before frowning.

    Zosia: You’re not meeting me there. I’m picking you up

    Almost instantly there’s a reply.

    Carol: Oh ok

    Zosia: Unless you’re operating some secret drug op at your house you don’t want me to see…

    Three dots appear, then disappear for what feels like a lifetime. Zosia feels a small panic set in, like maybe Carol is having second thoughts. Then the three dots reappear again.

    Carol: Ha ha funny. Didn’t realize I was going on a date with a comedian

    Carol doesn’t know the half of it. Zosia exhales, the tightness in her chest lifting.

    Zosia: But you’re not denying it

    Carol: Guess you’ll have to come see for yourself then. 1208 Cielo Rosado Ct

    She hearts the message, unaware of just how big she’s suddenly smiling.

    Zosia: Be there at 3

    A pause. Fingers hovering over the keyboard.

    Zosia: I can’t wait to see you

    Carol: You saw me yesterday

    She rolls her eyes but Carol hearts both messages back in quick succession.

    Carol: But I can’t wait to see you either

    Zosia feels herself levitating.

    —

    On the way to Carol’s house, Zosia stops at the gas station. It’s already September but the midday heat is always unrelenting. At the pump next to her, a man about as old as the nickel in her pocket blows a puff of smoke out his mouth. Surely he’s too old to be driving. Can he even see? Much less over his dashboard? Zosia stares at him in disbelief.

    “Excuse me,” she waves at him. “You can’t do that here.”

    The man looks over, startled, as if maybe he had heard his final call from the grim reaper.

    “You talkin’ to me?” His voice is as frail as ever.

    Impatiently, Zosia looks around to the distinct lack of other people and then back to him.

    “Yeah, you,” she says pointedly, then gestures to the abundance of no smoking signs surrounding them. “You can’t smoke here.”

    “Sweetheart,” he says taking a last, savoury drag of his cigarette before flicking it to the ground and slowly blowing the smoke in Zosia’s direction—and to his fortune, the smoke doesn’t actually reach her. “Been doin’ this my whole life. Ain’t blown up a gas station yet.” He squashes the butt with his dirty sandal, hangs up the pump, and retreats back into his car like his left hip is made out of cardboard and super glue.

    Zosia narrows her eyes at him, biting back her tongue, but then just shakes her head. Had she not already been in such high spirits about her date with Carol, poor old Colonel Sanders over there might have received a very different version of her. She lets it go.

    She’s just about to get back into her own car when something bright just across the way catches her eye. A flower stand—rickety and wooden, blooming with petals of every colour. It looks out of place against the adobe sand of Albuquerque.

    Zosia’s legs take her in that direction before she can even think twice about it, the gravel kicking up with each measured step. She stops in front of the bushels and pots, eyes wide at all the variety.

    “Occasion?” The burly woman behind the stand asks.

    “Date,” Zosia answers without hesitation.

    The woman looks at her once with an expression clear as day. Shouldn’t the man be the one buying flowers for you? Zosia pays no mind to it, and the woman has seemingly moved on anyways.

    “This one,” the woman says after a moment, and pulls a bouquet of red roses out from a bucket and holds them before Zosia for inspection. “Good, yes?”

    Now Zosia hesitates. Does Carol even like flowers? Somehow in all their joint logged hours, that’s something that’s never come up. But she isn’t about to show up to their date empty handed.

    “No.” Zosia shakes her head. Something about roses doesn’t scream “Carol” to her. Sure, maybe prickly on the outside as she once had been, but she knows her better now and roses are too basic, too cliché. And Carol is more than that.

    Then she spots the perfect bouquet. The tallest, brightest one at the stand.

    “That one.”

    “You sure?”

    “Yes.”

    She pays the lady.

    Carol has already checked the clock three times in the last five minutes, which is impressive even for her.

    2:35 PM.

    She paces from the kitchen to the living room, then back again, picking up things and putting them down with no real purpose. A dish towel gets folded, unfolded, and then draped over her shoulder like she’s about to host a cooking show she has no intention of filming. She opens the fridge, stares into it, then closes it again.

    This is ridiculous and even her reflection in the microwave agrees.

    She tries sitting. It doesn’t even last thirty seconds before she’s up again, smoothing a nonexistent wrinkle on the couch, adjusting a throw pillow by half an inch, and then stepping back like it matters.

    Guess what? It doesn’t.

    She turns around and stares at the liquor cabinet, which almost mocks her. Drink me, Carol. She has half a mind to down and inappropriate amount of vodka out of pure anticipation. And God knows if this were any other woman, she would. She would be so drunk by the time the woman arrives, to which the woman would just turn around and leave. Carol knows this because this has happened. At least twice.

    But there’s something, well, many things about Zosia that make her want to stay present in the moment. She defiantly flips off the cabinet. Not this time motherfucker.

    Then her gaze drifts, inevitably, to the photograph of Helen on the credenza. One that had been taken on their wedding day where she’s smiling so bright, eyes bearing straight into the camera. On almost every day, it brings Carol comfort, but today, her dead wife’s eyes follow her around the room like she knows something.

    “What are you looking at?” She picks up the photo, frame cool against her fingers. Even in stillness, Carol can’t lie to her. “Yes, if you must know, I’m going on a date.”

    She leans a hip against the credenza, thumb tapping along the edge of the frame.

    “She’s young and hot and unbelievably intelligent. You would have called it a beautiful disaster.” Then more sheepishly, as if Helen can actually hear her, “And we work together. And I know if you could, you would definitely be giving me that look.”

    She tilts her head imaging it, clicking her tongue.

    “Yeah, that one. Exactly.”

    Carol exhales softly through her nose, a quiet laugh slipping out.

    And that’s when the doorbell rings.

    She freezes.

    Like, actually freezes. Mid-breath, mid-thought. Everything around her snaps into sharp, immediate focus and her heartbeat lurches into her throat. Her eyes flick to the door, then back to the photograph still in her hands. For a second she just stands there, caught between two versions of her life.

    Then, she moves, but not to put the photo back onto the credenza. She opens the cabinet of the TV stand instead, quick and deliberate, and sets the frame carefully inside. The cabinet door has slits of glass so it’s not exactly hidden, but it’s not out in the open either.

    The doorbell doesn’t ring again, but Carol can feel Zosia on the other side of it.

    She wipes her palms against the material of her pants, fixes the collar of her shirt, takes one steadying breath, and opens the door.

    Zosia stands, smiling, like she had been intentionally placed there. She’s dressed simply but devastatingly. Dark blue fitted jeans, a brown ankle boot with a modest heel, and a simple black halter top that exposes her shoulders. Her hair falls loosely in soft waves.

    Her beauty is painfully effortless in a way that can ruin lives if she isn’t careful.

    And in her hands, now adorned with rings she’s never worn before—

    A bouquet of sunflowers.

    Bright, unapologetic, impossible to ignore.

    Carol forgets how to breathe.

    Completely.

    —

    Whatever breath Zosia has, whatever silly little greeting she’s been rehearsing in her head all morning is immediately gone when the door swings opens.

    Carol stands there like she’s just stepped out of out a quiet, devastating daydream that Zosia doesn’t even realize she’s been building.

    Soft white button-down, sleeves cuffed to her elbows, the top two buttons undone like she hasn’t overthought it, but she absolutely did, tucked partly into soft, beige coloured pants. Her shoes are crisp and white and her hair holds some product, like she had taken the time to style it in a very specific way. Then, something Zosia has rarely seen. Earrings—one small diamond in each lobe, glinting as the sunlight catches them.

    And then there is the goddamn way Carol is looking at her with her stupid fucking baby blue eyes.

    Effortless or intentional, Zosia can’t tell. Either way, entirely unfair.

    This is not Detective Sturka.

    This is simply Carol fucking Sturka.

    And Zosia is momentarily completely wrecked by it.

    She shifts slightly, just enough to betray a flicker of nerves beneath her composure.

    “Hi,” Carol finally says. It’s a little breathless and Zosia enjoys that she can get this type of reaction out of her.

    “Hey,” she says, but doesn’t move. She continues to look at her. Takes her in in a way that she absolutely should not be doing on a very suburban doorstep in broad daylight for all the neighbours to see.

    But Carol looks back. Shamelessly.

    “Wow, Zosia, you look so—“ her eyes drag down Zosia’s body with a slow confidence and then back up. “Good.”

    Zosia feels the rush of the compliment fill her shoulders. Then she says, “Carol, I think this is the first time you’ve left me speechless.” And she absolutely delights in the way Carol’s cheeks turn pink right before her.

    One would think that the fact that they have already seen each other naked would take the pressure off, but somehow it only intensifies it.

    “So, are you going to invite me in?” she says, voice smooth and steady. “Or are we just going to stand here looking at each other all day?”

    Carol blinks, like she’s being pulled back into her body, blush still high in her cheeks.

    “Right. Yeah. Uh, come in.” She steps back and Zosia walks past her, acutely aware that she is  actually inside Carol’s home.

    Zosia holds out the bouquet as she turns. “I wasn’t sure if you liked sunflowers or not.”

    Carol’s face softens and brightens. “I do. I really do.” She takes them, fingers brushing Zosia’s just long enough to register. “Thank you.”

    And then, before Zosia can recalibrate, Carol leans in. It’s quick and light. A kiss meant for her cheek, but it lands just a fraction too close to the corner of Zosia’s mouth.

    For the sex they’ve already had, the heated kisses they’ve shared, this one is—

    It should be nothing, but it’s everything.

    Zosia feels it like a spark that hits every nerve at once, and her heart slams hard against her chest. And Carol pulls back with a faint shift in her expression, like she feels it too.

    “This way,” Carol says, already turning, like if she doesn’t keep moving something might catch fire.

    Zosia follows her, because of course she does. And then…the house.

    It’s big. Open. Quiet in a way that feels lived-in and not empty. Light spills in through the wide windows, catching on the hardwood floors and soft corners and the life that has clearly existed here long before Zosia ever steps into it. Idly, she wonders how much of Helen still lives in here.

    She lets out a low whistle.

    “Jesus,” she mutters, glancing around. “This hallway is bigger than my whole apartment. I should’ve joined Albuquerque PD sooner.”

    Carol chuckles, low and easy, and it does something to Zosia’s spine.

    “Yeah, well,” Carol says, setting the flowers onto the counter as she looks for a vase. “Dead wife’s life insurance policy also helped, so...”

    A beat passes. A wordless but all knowing glance.

    Zosia huffs out a laugh, shaking her head. “That’s morbid.”

    Carol shrugs, a small smile tugging at her mouth. “You started it.”

    Zosia leans against the counter, watching Carol fill the vase, watching the way her sleeves sit rolled on her arms, the way she moves in her own space; like muscle memory, comfortable and certain. She takes a moment while Carol’s back is still turned to her, trimming the stems of the sunflowers into the sink, to look around.

    Carol’s home is softer and more colourful than she would have expected.

    At work, she’s all clean lines, quick decisions, and strictly business. Here, there are little interruptions everywhere. A dish towel slung over the oven handle, not folded, just left there like she’d been in the middle of something. A mug left out with a faint ring of dried coffee at the bottom.

    The counter tells its own story. Olive oil within easy reach. Salt in a small ceramic bowl instead of a shaker. A well-used cutting board propped against the tiled backsplash. And then her state of the art, Swiss-made espresso machine that she swears by.

    Zosia smiles, small and private.

    Before she even realizes it, she has unconsciously migrated towards the bookshelf in the living room, pausing in front of it. And it’s different here, more intimate. The kitchen is of habit. This is choice.

    The spines are a mix. Some worn, some pristine. A poetry collection with a cracked crease down the middle. A novel Zosia had tried to read three times and never finished. A few that surprise her.

    Zosia can’t help herself and pulls a book from the shelf. None in particular, just the first one within reach. The margins are marked with tiny, neat notes. She places it back and then pulls another. Same thing. Carol doesn’t just read, she argues with what she’s reading. She stays with it.

    She places the book back again and traces another spine lightly with her fingertip, like she might learn something just from the contact.

    Carol is contained at work. Edited and predictable in the way that discipline and competence often is.

    But here—she is expansive.

    Messy and warm in a real way that makes something in Zosia’s chest tighten without warning.

    “Get lost?” Carol’s voice comes closely and gently from behind her.

    Zosia turns and Carol is perched up against the back of the couch, arms loosely folded across her chest, lips quirked, brow arched. The moment their eyes meet, the space between them somehow both charges and softens at the same time.

    “You’re different here.”

    “Yeah?”

    “Yeah.” Zosia’s gaze drags, unapologetic. “I like it.”

    Carol holds her gaze too long and Zosia watches the way she swallows.

    “If you keep looking at me like that,” Carol warns lightly, “we might not make it out the door.”

    Zosia steps closer. “Is that a promise or a threat?” She reaches forward, gently unfolding Carol’s arms, then leans in slowly, giving Carol time to stop it if she wants to.

    Carol doesn’t. Instead, her hands snake around Zosia’s waist and pulls her closer.

    Then Zosia kisses her, hands on either side of her face. It’s brief at first, the way Zosia means it to be, but when Carol sighs into it, she can’t help the way her tongue comes forward and brushes against Carol’s bottom lip.

    Carol’s breath hitches slightly and she returns the gesture by capturing Zosia’s bottom lip between her own. But quickly, before it gets too heated, Carol pulls back, but only slightly. Enough to speak. “If we don’t leave now,” her voice rough now, “we are never leaving.”

    Zosia presses her forehead into Carol’s, thumbs brushing against Carol’s cheeks. She could stand here like this all day. “Would that be such a bad thing?”

    Carol nudges her firmly. “Let’s go, Lieutenant.”

    Zosia immediately groans, rolling her eyes as she steps back. “God, Carol. No work talk today.”

    Carol simply just laughs and Zosia eagerly follows her out of the house.

    High on the list of things that Carol Sturka hates is crowds; and yet somehow she has willingly planted herself in the middle of one at the Albuquerque Museum.

    It’s just that when the most beautiful woman she has ever laid her eyes on asks her on a date, who the fuck is she to say no?

    And it would be unbearable here if not for Zosia, who’s been tucked against her side from the moment they walked in. At first, Carol had felt some apprehension only from the fact that they might run into someone from work, but when Zosia threads her arm through Carol’s with the suaveness like it belongs there, that alone softens everything.

    They drift from exhibit to exhibit at no particular pace, Zosia supplying a steady stream of ridiculous, almost certainly made-up commentary about whatever they’re looking at. Carol hums along, laughs in the right places, and pretends she understands, like right now.

    Zosia tilts her head at a random, centuries old painting after having stared at it for all of ten seconds, hands clasped behind her back like she’s the sexiest arts history professor Carol has ever seen, about to deliver a lecture.

    “This one’s famous, you know,” Zosia starts. “The artist painted it entirely using a spoon. He refused brushes on moral grounds.”

    Carol nods thoughtfully, suppressing a laugh. “Mhm. Of course. Brushes are…ethically complex.”

    “Deeply. There was a whole scandal. The spoon was allegedly stolen from a duke.”

    Carol hums, leaning in a little closer to the canvas. “You can tell. The texture. It’s very…spoon.”

    Zosia glances at her, catching the corner of her smile. “You’re taking this very seriously.”

    “I’m trying to respect the duke,” Carol says, solemn.

    Zosia snorts. “Tragic figure. Never recovered from the cutlery betrayal.”

    Carol laughs softly, in a way that’s only reserved for Zosia, then looks back at the painting like she might actually find the story hidden in it. “It does feel a bit haunted.”

    “Oh, it is,” Zosia responds immediately. “That’s not even paint. It’s—“

    “Don’t,” Carol cuts in, still smiling. “I like not knowing where you’re going with this.”

    Zosia grins. “Coward.”

    “Absolutely,” Carol says. “But a happy one.”

    Zosia threads her arm through Carol’s again and they move on.

    Then every time Carol turns to look at her, Zosia is already looking at her with a kind of quiet, knowing attention that makes the noise of the crowd fade and the bodies blur until it feels like it’s just the two of them.

    —

    The next room is bigger and louder. Families, children, occasional bursts of laughter.

    One painting in particular catches Carol’s eye, and she leans closer to the placard, squinting.

    She half scoffs, half laughs. “What on earth…”

    Zosia follows her gaze to the painting.

    It’s odd, the painting. A lopsided scene of a picnic where everything just feels slightly wrong. A dog sitting at a table like a person, a bottle of wine twice the size it should be, and a couple in the back who look like they don’t know where they are. The brushstrokes are haphazard and wide, as if blurring the lines had been intentional.

    “I kind of love it,” Zosia says.

    Carol turns to her, eye brow raised. “You would.”

    Zosia glances back, a small smile tugging at her lips. “What’s that supposed to mean?”

    “It means,” Carol says, gesturing vaguely at the canvas, “you’d pick the one painting in the entire museum where the dog has better posture than the humans.”

    “Aw, come on, Carol. Where’s your whimsy?”

    “Pfft. Whimsy is for children at Disneyland.”

    Zosia giggles, tugging Carol just a little closer so they can take a better look together. The warmth of Zosia sends an electric current rippling through Carol’s body.

    “Seriously,” Zosia says. “Look closer. No one in this painting knows what they’re doing. The guy is pouring wine onto the table, the woman looks like she regrets coming, and the dog is just…thriving.”

    Carol tilts her head, studying it again. “So your takeaway is…be the dog?”

    “Precisely.”

    Carol huffs a quiet laugh. “That tracks, actually.”

    A moment passes, lighter than the ones before, but no less full. Zosia shifts her weight, leaning further into Carol, to which Carol welcomes.

    “It’s kind of comforting,” Zosia adds. “Like, even in something that’s supposed to be nice, everything can be a bit of a mess.”

    Carol’s gaze lingers on the painting, but her focus isn’t entirely there anymore, especially when Zosia’s fingers are lightly scratching at her arm. “So what are you saying? Our lives are a poorly organized picnic?”

    “I’m saying,” Zosia replies, now turning her head to look at Carol, “that maybe things don’t have to be perfect to be good.”

    Carol meets her eyes then, and something in her expression softens. “Sounds like something you’re trying to convince yourself of.”

    Zosia’s smile falters just for a second but it doesn’t disappear, just turns into something more real. “Maybe I’m trying to convince you.”

    Carol hesitates, cheeks turning pink. She turns a little to face Zosia more head on, and Zosia’s hand settles around Carol’s wrist.

    “You know,” Zosia continues carefully, holding Carol’s gaze long and steady, “you’re allowed to want things to go right.”

    Carol glances down briefly and then back up, feeling the warmth slowly bloom in her chest.

    Of course Zosia would be able to find the beauty behind the eccentricity and turn it into something so introspective. The noise of the room fades a little, like it steps politely out of the way. Finally, Zosia lets her hand drop from Carol’s wrist into her hand, fingers threading noncommittally, but deliberately.

    Then, her voice drops, laced with something warmer. “This is a good mess, though.”

    Carol can’t help the way her lips curve. “Are you comparing us to the dog or the wine spill?”

    Zosia pretends to consider it, her eyes flicking to Carol’s mouth for second too long before returning to her eyes. “The dog,” she says. “Definitely the dog.”

    Carol laughs a little louder this time, leaning forward to let her forehead fall onto Zosia’s bare shoulder allowing her laugh to subside. God, she smells so good. And Zosia takes the opportunity to nuzzle her nose into the top of Carol’s head.

    “Come on,” Carol says, tugging at Zosia’s hand. “Let’s keep moving.”

    Behind them, the painting stays crooked and strange and a little bit ridiculous and Carol loves that.

    “I did not say that.”

    “You didn’t have to.”

    Carol rolls her eyes, their food arrives, and her shoulders finally relax.

     

    *****

     

    There are three things that Lieutenant Zosia Nowak hates in this world. Disorder, chaos, and incompetence. That is why she is immediately fucked off when they arrive at the crime scene.

    The car has barely rolled to a stop and she has already noticed the flock of uniformed officers cluttered by the entrance, someone is talking loud enough that it can be heard through the windshield and two young female officers who look like they are fresh of out the academy are leaned up against a forklift smoking a cigarette without a care in the world, like they’re about to pose for a photoshoot.

    “Fan-fucking-tastic,” Zosia groans, bringing the car to a stop. “We’re not even out of the car and it’s already a circus.”

    Beside her, Carol unbuckles her seatbelt and then scribbles something into her notepad. “Well, at least they’ve committed to a theme.”

    Zosia glances at her with a raised brow. “And what theme would that be? It’s certainly not Peace and Serenity.”

    Carol turns her head, expression perfectly flat. “Forklift Chic.”

    “Better get on the phone with Vogue.”

    Zosia’s hand is still over the gear shift when Carol reaches over and softly closes her own hand over Zosia’s. For a moment, Zosia feels her irritation subside because Carol is looking at her with her insane blue eyes that still manages to derail her thoughts and she briefly forgets they are at an active crime scene.

    Carol pulls Zosia’s hand off the gear shift and holds it atop the centre console. Zosia smiles now because it’s impossible to not smile at Carol, and through her sunglasses she glances around to make sure no one’s watching before pulling Carol’s hand up and kissing her knuckles softly.

    “Oh, I believe it,” is all Zosia says before she finally kills the engine. “Come on, let’s go partner.”

    The Duke City Dairy warehouse sits heavy and silent under the Albuquerque sun, early morning heat already shimmering off the metal siding. Zosia’s boots crunch with purpose against the gravel as she approaches the yellow tape fluttering lazily at the entrance. Her badge, clipped to her belt, glints brightly like a freshly minted coin.

    Two minutes.

    Officer Wong is still there, still smiling, and still. fucking. touching. Carol.

    “Yes, Lieutenant.”

    Zosia’s eyes dart quickly to where her hand had been on Carol’s arm and then back up. “I believe you were assigned to perimeter support.”

    “Yes, ma’am.”

    “Then I suggest you return to it.”

    “I was just- “

    “Now.”

    Officer Wong opens her mouth to say something, but smartly decides better of it. Without another word, she scurries away.

    Zosia lets out a breath but doesn’t look at Carol right away but she already knows the amused look that is on her face.

    “Is there a problem, Lieutenant?” Carol asks lightly.

    “No,” Zosia says, meeting her gaze now, feeling slightly foolish like maybe she had overreacted. “Get back to work, Detective Sturka.”

    Carol bites back a smile that tells Zosia she won’t hear the end of it later.

    “Yes, Lieutenant Nowak,” she says, purposely brushing her shoulder into Zosia as she walks away.

    It’s mid afternoon by the time Zosia finally slides back into the driver’s seat of her Explorer. She had picked the wrong shirt to wear today- thick unbreathable cotton that leaves the fabric clinging to her back uncomfortably.     “So,” Carol says casually, buckling her seatbelt as Hozier starts playing through the speakers. “Who was the jealous one this time?”

    Zosia says nothing, shifting the car into drive, but doesn’t deny it either. She lowers her sunglasses from her head over her eyes and pulls out onto the road.

    “I don’t like distractions on my scenes,” she finally says, but she doesn’t even convince herself. From her peripheral, she can see Carol’s head turn towards her.

    “Sure.”

    Zosia stays quiet again and Carol doesn’t push, likely because their positions had been swapped not too long ago. When they stop at a red light, Zosia looks back at her.

    “Watching that,” she says, voice low with a vulnerability she hadn’t expected, “only made it worse.”

    “Worse?” Carol’s brow lifts.

    “It made me want you,” she says matter-of-factly. “Right there.” And she doesn’t even that sexually. Well, not completely.

    Another silence fills the car and she feels stupid for even saying it. The light turns green but she still catches the way Carol smiles before her eyes settle back onto the road.

    “Well,” Carol says, sinking back into her seat. “Maybe if you behave…” she pauses and her eyes flick quickly to Zosia again, “…you can have me tonight.”

    Something about that, as silly and provocative as it is, tugs at Zosia’s heartstrings because that is hold that Carol has had over her since the moment they shook hands for the first time in the briefing room in front of Chief Schrader.

    Zosia finally cracks a smile, eyes still forward. “I’ll be on my best behaviour,” she promises.

    Carol laughs in return. “That’ll be a first.”

    Then, quieter, Zosia says, “stay over tonight.”

    And there’s no hesitation from Carol.

    “Yeah,” she says, hand reaching over the centre console to land in Zosia’s lap, and Zosia’s hand closes over hers, fingers threading together.

    Take me to fucking church indeed, she thinks.

    +

    The last place Carol wants to be after a long and sweaty investigation is back in the briefing room and with Chief Schrader and his eau de cigar, no less. Especially now knowing she’ll be spending the night at Zosia’s, it makes the rest of the afternoon drag by unbearably slow.

    The case files are spread across the table in uneven stacks; photos from the scene, lab work-ups, timelines scribbled in both Carol and Zosia’s handwriting.

    Zosia stands at the whiteboard, arms crossed, brows furrowed, while Carol sits forward in her chair, elbows on her knees, pen clicking restlessly under her thumb. Schrader is leaned back at the head of the table watching them very curiously.

    “The break-in wasn’t random,” Carol says, tapping at a photo. “The entry point is clean and targeted. The suspect knew exactly where to go. That open crate Koumba found is tied to the victim, it’s not a coincidence.”

    Zosia shakes her head immediately. “Or it’s staged to look that way. There was no forced rush or panic. If this was connected to the murder, we’d see an overlap in the method, and we don’t.”

    Carol looks at up her, surprised at the pushback. “We do, though. The timing alone- “

    “Timing isn’t evidence,” Zosia cuts in a little bit too quickly. “It’s correlation. You’re building a narrative around it.”

    “And you’re dismissing a lead because it doesn’t fit your theory,” Carol shoots back, standing now, pen dropping to the table with a soft clatter.

    It feels like the floor shifts beneath her feet and her heart thumps like a deep base line in her chest. She doesn’t like this feeling and somewhere behind her, Schrader’s eyes dart between her and Zosia.

    Zosia’s posture stiffens. “My theory is based on what we can prove.”

    “And mine is based on what’s right in front of us.” Carol gestures to the photos.

    They are at a standstill for the first time and if Carol is being completely honest, it feels bad, even if she knew something like this was always going to be a real possibility. Zosia looks like she regrets this just as much and somehow that makes Carol feel even worse but she knows that this is the job. And right now, the job needs to come first.

    “Carol- “

    “Okay, that’s enough.” Schrader rubs at his face, then leans forward on the table, hands clasped.

    Both Carol and Zosia go quiet, but neither of them can look away from each other.

    “You two are the best detectives I’ve worked with in years,” he continues. “That’s why you’re both here. But right now you’re arguing positions, not evidence. So go back, review everything together and figure out where the gap is before you bring it back to me.” He pauses, running his hand over his bald head. “And maybe leave the ego out of it.”

    Carol stands down first, jaw tight. And Zosia gives a short nod.

    —

    Zosia’s office is way too quiet.

    Carol lingers near the door at first, hesitant to even come in. She replays the argument in the her head trying to figure out where the disconnect was. Eventually she does step in all the way, letting the door click shut behind her. She doesn’t sit, but she does look at Zosia, who is already looking at her from her chair behind the desk.

    It feels strange, this silence. Not necessarily bad, just uncertain.

    “Well that was new,” Carol says it almost like a joke.

    Zosia lets out a humourless laugh through her nose. “Yeah, it was.”

    “I wasn’t expecting you to push back like that,” Carol admits.

    “I wasn’t expecting you to dig in.” The edge in Zosia’s voice is still there, although less defensive and more earnest.

    Carol crosses her arms but not in a way to close herself off, just to feel the physical weight of something. She hates that Zosia is right there, so close yet so far, and that she cannot touch her.

    “I still think I’m right.”

    Finally, Zosia’s mouth twitches. “I know you do…and I still think I am too.”

    Carol laugh is reluctant, but the tension cracks a little and warmth returns to the room. “I’m not surprised.”

    “This is going to happen,” Zosia says, leaning back in her chair. “We’re not always going to land on the same conclusion at the same time. Or at all. We just haven’t had to deal with it yet.”

    Carol takes a moment to absorb that, her eyes momentarily drifting to the files on Zosia’s desk then back. She’s consumed thousands and thousands of hours of TV shows and movies about workplace romances but never thought she’d find herself in the middle of one.

    She steps closer to the desk now, resting her hands lightly against the edge. “So what do we do when it happens again?”

    Zosia considers her for a moment, then supplies Carol with the most honest answer she can. “We argue. Just…better than that.”

    It’s a good answer because it's a real answer. Carol smiles faintly, nodding. “Less in front of Schrader.”

    “Preferably,” Zosia says, tone now with a hint of dry amusement. “And we don’t make it personal. We stick to the work, even if we’re frustrated.”

    “Deal,” Carol answers. Her fingers tap lightly against the desk and then still. “For what it’s worth, I wasn’t trying to bulldoze you.”

    “I know. And I wasn’t trying to shut you down.”

    “Alright then.” Carol straightens slightly. “Let’s figure out which one of us wrong.”

    Zosia lifts and eyebrow. “You’re very confident it’s not you.”

    “Statistically, I like my odds.”

    Zosia smiles now, eyes glinting and crinkling in the corners. “Come here,” she says in a quiet and private way only reserved for Carol, gesturing to the chair beside her desk. “We are sorting this out now because I want you to myself tonight. I don’t want to share you with work.”

    Carol feels that hard and fast in her chest and her stomach does that thing that only serves to reinforce a truth that she already knows. She moves without hesitation, pulling it closer, their shoulders very much pressed together as they both lean over the file. If anyone were to walk in right now, they would certainly have questions.

    Zosia points to a report. “Start with the entry logs again. If you’re right, there’s a pattern we missed.”

    “And if you’re right,” Carol says, flipping to another page, “the staging breaks somewhere in the sequence.”

    They settle back into familiarity. The tension may not have completely disappeared, but now it’s something more productive and collaborative. And most importantly, underneath it is the knowledge that can push against each other and not break.

     

    +

     

    Carol stops at Target for toothpaste, deodorant, and a very specific brand of popcorn that Zosia had mentioned to her once on a stakeout that she loves because apparently, “all popcorn does does not taste the same, Carol.” And she had said it with a grave kind of seriousness usually reserved for interrogations and negotiations.

    She’s not sure how she gets there, but ten minutes later, Carol finds herself standing motionless in the toy aisle holding a box of Lego wildflowers. She stares at it. The wildflowers stare back.

    “Well you certainly have a longer life expectancy than the balcony plant,” Carol mutters quietly to herself, remembering that Zosia had said she wished she could have a garden.

    She looks down at the box of flowers again- bright, indestructible, impossible to over or under water. Technically immortal.

    No. This is absolutely ridiculous. She is a fifty-three year old homicide detective standing in Target debating toy flowers like she’s in the middle of some kind of ethical crisis. She puts the box back on the shelf and takes two steps away.

    She stops. Turns around.

    “Oh, fuck it.”

    Carol marches back, grabs the wildflowers, then after a brief moment of weakness also grabs the tiny Lego succulents because if she’s going to humiliate herself, she might as well fully commit.

    As she tosses them into her basket, a young boy nearby looks at her approvingly.

    “Those ones are cool,” he tells her.

    Carol nods sternly. “So I’ve heard.”

    Carol arrives at Zosia’s apartment only seven minutes after her requested time of 6:00PM. The moment the door opens, she is hit with two things immediately.

    One: The sight of Zosia. In those stupid, threat-to-public-safety grey shorts again and a cropped white tank top that exposes way too much of her midriff and the fact that she is seemingly allergic to bras. Carol’s not complaining though.

She sighs into it, opening her mouth for Zosia because she’s been thinking about this all day. And Zosia smells so good. Freshly showered, hair just barely damp.

    “They looked at me first.”

    “Did you…cook?” Carol asks, eyeing all the dishes everywhere and the pot boiling on the stove. “For us?”

    “Mhm.” Zosia smiles, setting the bag from Target onto the counter and tossing Carol’s overnight bag into her bedroom.

    “What- what did you cook?”

    “You said you really like that miso glazed salmon from the restaurant last weekend so I gave it go. Do you know how hard it is to find miso paste in Albuquerque?”

    “Oh, wow,” Carol says, feeling a sudden rush of emotion. Zosia, cooking for her. Went out of her way to find ingredients for her. “I- yeah, uh, it smells fucking incredible.”

    “I hope it tastes as good,” Zosia says, filling two wine glasses with a heavy pour. She hands one to Carol and they clink. “So what is in here?” She asks, eyes darting to the Target bag.

    “Um, supplies.”

    “That sounds ominous.”

    “It’s mostly popcorn.”

    “Mostly?”

    Before Carol can elaborate, Zosia reaches her hand towards the bag and Carol reacts faster than the speed of light.

    “Don’t.”

    Zosia freezes mid-motion and looks at Carol. “…What are you hiding in there?”

    “Nothing.”

    “That was the least convincing nothing I have ever heard.”

    Carol rubs the back of her neck, setting her wine glass down. “It’s stupid.”

    Now Zosia looks delighted. “Oh, I absolutely need to see it then.”

    “You deserve a garden that survives your care.”

    “You know,” Carol gestures vaguely with a handful of popcorn. “Shrek is just a deeply misunderstood introvert.”

    Zosia glances down at her. “He’s an ogre but okay, elaborate.”

    “Exactly,” Carol says, as if that proves anything. “He’s built boundaries, like literal ones. ‘Get out of my swamp’ isn’t aggression. It’s- it’s emotional self-preservation.”

    Zosia’s fingers still in her hair. “Are you psychoanalyzing Shrek?”

    “I might be,” Carol continues, warming to it. “People project onto him. They see some big, scary ogre, but he’s just a guy who wants to be left alone with his snacks and routine. And honestly, I don’t blame him.”

    Carol leans closer now and Zosia just wants to kiss the wine off her lips.

    “It’s okay, I like it,” Carol says kissing Zosia on the cheek. “And I like you.”

    Zosia doesn’t answer right away. Maybe it’s the wine but she just wants to look at Carol for a moment because even in the dim light with green flashing from the TV, Carol just looks so…handsome. Her words.

    Back on the screen, Shrek rambles on and Carol suddenly sits up straighter. “Okay, wait, I can do him.”

    Zosia is immediately wary. “No…”

    “Yes,” Carol insists. “I’ve been practicing.”

    “You cannot be serious.”

    Carol clears her throat dramatically, like she’s about to deliver a speech to a crowd. She shifts her posture and lowers her voice into something…indescribable. It’s definitely not Scottish. It’s not even consistently human.

    “GET OUTTA MA SWAMP,” she bellows so loudly the neighbours must hear.

    Zosia freezes in pure terror.

    Carol, somehow encouraged by the silence, doubles down. “WHAT ARE YE DOIN’ IN MA… “ she waves her arms around, nearly knocking over the popcorn and wine bottle, “…SWAMP.”

    Zosia cannot watch. Her hands are on her face and she’s peeking through her fingers hoping the couch will swallow her. “That…” she says slowly, “was offensive.”

    Carol blinks, incredulous. “What? No, that was good.”

    “Kochanie, that was not good.”

    “It had layers,” Carol argues.

    “It had problems.”

    Carol frowns. “You’re not being very supportive of my artistic expression right now.”

    Zosia shakes her head trying, and very much failing, not to smile. “That accent does not belong to any country on earth.”

    “God, Zosh, it was nuanced.”

    “It was a crime against humanity.”

    Carol reaches for her wine glass, takes a sip, and then leans closer lowering her voice again like she still has something to prove.

    “DONKAY-“

    Zosia’s eyes widen and she lunges forward now and clasps a hand over Carol’s mouth, pushing her back onto the couch and falling on top of her.

    “No,” she says, but she’s laughing. “You’re done. Absolutely not.”

    Carol’s eyes crinkle above her hand, clearly delighted with herself, but Zosia keeps her hand there for a moment, falling into a fit of laughter. Then after a moment, Carol gently pulls her hand away, but not before kissing the inside of her palm. 

    “You loved it.”

    “I did not.”

    “You did,” Carol insists. “You’re just intimidated by my range.”

    Zosia exhales, shaking her head. She can no longer hide the warmth and fondness in her expression. The way she’s looking at Carol like she’s something both stupid and, well, kind of incredible.

    “You are so embarrassing,” Zosia says.

    Carol shrugs, satisfied. She pushes Zosia off her and then climbs onto her lap, straddling her. She places her arms on Zosia’s shoulders and laces her fingers together behind her neck. “And yet, here I am, still making you laugh.”

    Zosia doesn’t argue. Carol is right.

    On screen, Shrek keeps talking and Zosia tugs her ever so slightly closer by the fabric of her shirt.

    “If you ever do that impression in public, I will deny knowing you.”

    “So you’re saying I can do it in private.”

    “Not if you ever want to get laid again.”

    “Oh, I’m getting laid,” Carol says, closing some distance. “I’m getting laid tonight.”

    Zosia huffs, hands on Carol’s thighs, her body flushing warm.

    “Confident, are you?”

    “Very.”

    Carol’s mouth parts and she leans in slowly like she’s going to kiss Zosia, but instead she turns her head and puts her lips close to Zosia’s ear.

    “Somebody once told me—“

    Zosia groans loudly and immediately, but she’s laughing now. Fully and whole heartedly. She allows herself to fully give in to Carol’s absurdity, to this version of her she didn’t know existed and this version of her that is just so unabashedly…her.

    And it’s then, while Carol is singing Smash Mouth horribly in her ear that a thought arrives to Zosia plainly and without qualm. It is simply that she is falling in love with this woman.

    Chapter 12: the truth about oat milk

    Chapter Summary

    Fluff, sweat, and tears or something.

    Chapter Notes

    It’s sometime after two in the morning, though Carol has not checked the time in a while. All Star by Smash Mouth is long since forgotten. Time feels irrelevant now, instead, measured in breaths, in the slow drag of fingertips, in the way Carol’s body still hums, oversensitive and spent. She had lost track after the fifth orgasm or maybe sixth. After that, everything had blurred into heat and laughter and Zosia’s voice, hot and low against her ear.

    Now there’s only this. Skin on skin, legs tangled, the faint chill of the room cooling the sheen of sweat between them. Zosia lies half-draped over Carol, her face tucked close to Carol’s shoulder, arm slung across her waist, hand kneading into soft flesh.

    Carol’s fingers wander without thinking, her arm trapped under Zosia’s body, tracing idle paths over her back. They pause at the scar there, the long jagged one just to the right of her spine that takes up more of her back than it should, where the skin feels just a little bit different from the rest of her body.

    “What’s this from?” Carol asks quietly.

    “Bar fight,” Zosia answers quickly and casually.

    Carol shifts, squinting at her. “Seriously?”

    “As serious as that knife wound,” Zosia replies and Carol can hear the hint of a smile in her voice. “First year in the force. Got too cocky and started asking too many questions. Really pushed one guy’s buttons when I asked about his mother. Spent the night in the ER.”

    Carol lets out a soft but incredulous laugh, her fingers still tracing along the scar. Somehow, this does not surprise her about Zosia, at all. “Jesus. You were stabbed?”

    “Something like that. The knife never actually entered my body.”

    “Still. So you won, then.”

    “Eventually,” Zosia says, and Carol scoffs. Eventually.

    Carol settles back down now, fitting her body more deliberately against Zosia’s, like she can’t bear for there to be a single inch of space between them.

    “And you?” Zosia’s voice is softer now, etched with sleep and closer to Carol’s ear. “I haven’t noticed any scars.”

    “Then you haven’t been looking hard enough.”

    Zosia hums, amused. “Show me, then.”

    “Here,” Carol says, angling her head to the side and pushing away her hair, exposing a cluster of very fine white scars just behind her ear and along her hair line that’s barely visible in the dim lamp light. “A few years ago Manny and I were at a gas station after a stakeout and a Salamanca shot at us. Windshield shattered, got cut by the glass.”

    Zosia leans up now, tracing a finger over the scars that she looks surprised to not have noticed before.

    “And this,” Carol says, rolling a little bit to her side and exposing the single faint line of a scar just on the side of her breast. “I was chasing a suspect back in like, 2010, and I was climbing a chainlink fence and slipped and the wire cut me. So, nothing heroic.”

    Zosia smiles at this one, craning her head down and sleepily pressing her lips against it. “I beg to differ,” she says, lazily kissing her way back up to Carol’s shoulder, leaving a trail a goosebumps as she goes. “You are a very heroic person.”

    “Mm, no, I’m not,” Carol says like she’s a little shy at that; she’s far from a hero. She’s half expecting Zosia to argue back but she doesn’t.

    Zosia just looks at her with a fondness that is only made more earnest by her tiredness. “Well,” she says after a moment, “you’re my hero, then.”

    Carol tries to fight the pull in her chest, but she can’t. She rolls her eyes instead, because she has to do something with it. “I think all those orgasms have messed with your head.”

    Zosia lets out a quiet laugh through her nose but she’s too tired for rebuttal, and it would be so easy for Carol to just deflect again and make another joke but the way Zosia is looking at her right now, hair a mess, lips still swollen, and her eyes heavy now with sleep - it just sort of leaves Carol without words because she’s realizing this is exactly where she wants to be.

    “Kochanie,” Zosia all but slurs through half lidded eyes because the sleep is taking over her now. “Can we go to sleep now?”

    “Yes,” Carol whispers, pressing a kiss into her forehead, but Zosia is already gone before she can say goodnight.

    No one tells Carol that building lego flowers on a Saturday afternoon would be one of the most fun things she’s done for as long as she can remember. Now here they are, still in their pajamas, sitting on the floor at the coffee table while Zosia has one leg swung over Carol’s.

    The lego pieces are organized into neat piles according to their bag number, while the instruction booklet is propped up against the empty wine bottle from last night. In the background, a playlist of ‘90s nostalgic hits’ plays.

    “That’s the wrong piece,” Zosia says, when Carol can’t get the stem to click together.

    “What? No it’s not,” Carol says, inspecting it.

    “Yes it is, look.” Zosia plucks it from her hand and holds it against the instructions. “This one has three sides, yours has four. It’s also not even the right colour.” She rummages through the pieces for the correct one. “Here.”

    Carol takes it reluctantly, still believing she’s right, but when it clicks perfectly in place, Zosia just hums besides her.

    “Mhm.”

    “Whatever."

    Zosia laughs, kissing her on the cheek, and Carol feels this unbearable swell of tenderness, which has been happening a lot lately.

    About an hour later, all the flowers are complete except for one that looks…not right.

    “Hmm, I don’t know, Zosh. This one seems…biologically incorrect,” Carol says pointing at the lupin where she’s realized half the petals are upside down. “I’m not sure this is what Gregor Mendel had in mind when he started experimenting with peas.”

    Zosia stares at it and then back at the instruction booklet. “Oh, son of a bitch.”

    They both dissolve into laughter, Zosia looking genuinely betrayed by a fake flower. “Design flaw,” she huffs. “The diagram is microscopic.”

    “You investigate homicides.”

    “Correct. Not tiny plastic horticulture.”

    Carol snorts and they rebuild the last flower together. And as they sit there, legs a complete tangle under the table now while Zosia’s hands are on Carol’s while they click in the final pieces together in the correct direction, Carol thinks to herself, terrifyingly, if Zosia were to ask her to stay forever right then, she probably would.

    Carol loves the farmers market.

    She loves the way the open air carries the scent of freshly baked bread, ripe peaches, and flowers all at once. She loves the bright spill of colours from the produce stands and how there is always music drifting from somewhere out of sight. And then there are the samples and the coffee; and god is the coffee good.

    That’s why on Sunday morning when Zosia suggests going, Carol doesn’t even hesitate.

    Now here they are strolling side by side, shoulders brushing as they weave through the crowd. Between them, their pinkies hook lightly together, only for the reason that Carol is always on the lookout that they might run into someone from work.

    At some point, Zosia drifts a little bit ahead of her, drawn in by anything with colour or texture, and Carol lets her, trailing just behind, content just to be in the orbit of her.

    “Did you know the Chimayo chile is so region-specific that it’s only considered authentic if it’s grown within that exact geographic area?” Zosia says, a little too pleased with herself, stopping in front of a chili stall.

    “Are you flirting with me with obscure facts about peppers?”

    “Who would I be if I didn’t?” She picks up a pepper, inspecting it. “Is it working?”

    “It might be.”

    Three stalls and a bag of chiles later, the smell of freshly ground coffee is too hard to deny.

    “Wait here,” Zosia says, already halfway gone. “I’ll get us coffee.”

    Carol ends up migrating to a honey stand because she’s just remembered she had run out the other day. She peruses all the different options, then lifts a small jar up, sunlight catching in the amber, glowing thick and golden. She turns it slowly, reading the label without really processing it.

    “Locally sourced right from Los Ranchos,” the man behind the stand says. “That one you’re holding is lavender.”

    Sure. What does Carol know about organic honey anyways. “I’ll take it,” she says, handing the man cash.

    A moment later, a shadow slips into her peripheral and a cup appears in front of her face. Carol turns and Zosia stands there, holding out the coffee, already watching her with that expectant little tilt to her mouth.

    “Oat milk,” she says, like it physically pains her. “Just for you.”

    “Thank you,” Carol says, taking the cup from her. “One day you will learn to accept that oat milk is the superior choice.”

    Zosia wrinkles her nose. “Well today is not that day. It tastes like someone described milk to a grain.”

    Carol takes a deliberate sip, holding eye contact. “I guess my taste is just more refined than yours.”

    “That’s your Stockholm syndrome talking.”

    Carol scoffs in mock offence, shaking her head, and Zosia simply urges them on.

    At a fruit stand, Zosia stops so abruptly that Carol actually walks into her.

    “Oh my god,” Zosia gasps softly and Carol follows her gaze.

    Mangoes. Whole rows of them, different varieties piled high like treasure. Zosia lights up like she’s never seen a mango before (she has- Carol literally saw her eating one just a few days ago) and steps forward immediately, picking one up, turning it in her hands with reverence.

    Carol leans against the edge of the stall, coffee warm between her palms, and just… watches.

    Zosia inspecting each mango with grave care, asking the vendor questions like where they were sourced from, nodding seriously. Then she grabs three more as if they were going to disappear before her.

    Carol smiles at that; this pure and innocent child-like wonder that she doesn’t get to see at work, and that she’s slowly beginning to learn lives deep within Zosia and just needs a little coaxing to come out. And if nothing else happens today, this would be enough.

    Zosia - giddy over mangoes.

    Somehow, later, they split up without really meaning to. Zosia is off somewhere probably smelling flowers, and Carol drifts toward a tomato stand because for weeks now she’s been wanting to make tomato sauce from scratch. She picks one up, testing its weight, pressing lightly at the skin.

    “Carol?”

    A sudden but familiar man’s voice startles her and she looks up. Her stomach immediately drops when she sees who is standing right there, too close.

    Rick.

    Oh for fuck’s sake. A static dread starts to fill her body.

    “R-Rick,” she stutters, taking a step back and nervously glancing around looking for wherever Zosia had wandered off to. “Hi?”

    He beams at her a little too excitedly. “Didn’t expect to see you here.”

    Carol lets out a short, awkward laugh, her pulse quickening. “Yeah, uh, I mean it’s a farmers market so…people…come here.”

    Real smooth, but Rick doesn’t seem to notice.

    “Beautiful day for it.”

    “Sure. Yeah, it is.”

    There’s a long, drawn out pause that Rick seems completely unbothered by because unlike any normal human who sees a colleague outside of work and runs the other way, Rick would approach her and strike up a conversation.

    Just say something normal for once. “I didn’t know you…came to these,” Carol blurts, eyes still darting around. Where the fuck is Zosia?

    Rick shrugs, grinning. “My wife loves it and I get dragged along. Not complaining though. Great coffee.” He nods towards the now empty cup in Carol’s hand.

    “Right. Yeah. The beans are…fresh.”

    Jesus Christ. She can hear herself but can’t stop.

    “And the tomatoes are- ” she holds one up vaguely, “plump.” Kill me.

    “And you- ” Rick starts but promptly stops. His eyes widen ever so slightly and he’s looking past Carol.

    Immediately, Carol knows and she braces herself.

    “Carol, so apparently,” Zosia’s voice, excited and mid-thought, cuts in as she sidles up beside her, holding a husk of corn the size of a small dog. “If you leave the husk on while you grill it, it actually - ” She stops because she finally notices Rick and there is the smallest stutter in her step.

    Carol notices it and she’s thinking this is her worst nightmare. And it’s not even just Rick catching them out together in public while they’re very clearly off duty. But just Rick in general, out in the wild in his cargo shorts, New Balances, and bright pink polo shirt - ready and armed to talk about concrete slabs at anytime. That is almost worse.

    “Oh, Rick. Hello,” Zosia says, voice too high, unable to mask the surprise.

    Rick’s eyes dart between them. “Lieutenant. Wow, uh, hi. Did you- did you guys come here together?”

    Carol swears the earth swings on its axis. She glances at Zosia. Zosia glances back. A whole conversation passes between them in that look.

    Please do not let me talk.

    “Well,” Zosia tries smoothly as she can, picking up on Carol’s turmoil and turning back to Rick. “It’s Sunday, so Zosia is fine. And yes, we did.”

    Oh no, why would she say that?  But then -

    Rick blinks and then his face lights up. “You know what, that’s great,” he says, genuinely delighted, and Carol halfway unclenches though she still can’t tell if Zosia is relieved or not that she doesn’t have to elaborate.

    “We need more friendships outside of work,” he goes on. “Helps keep morale up, ya know? Great to see you ladies getting along so well. What a lovely sisterhood you have created.”

    Sisterhood. Sisterhood. Carol very nearly chokes on the air and thinks she might actually have a stroke. She takes back her earlier sentiment.

    This is worse than concrete slabs.

    With horror, she turns slightly, just enough to catch Zosia’s face and Zosia is biting the inside of her cheek, eyes almost bulging out of her head like she’s trying to not laugh directly in Rick’s face. Because if Rick had any idea what they had done to each other last night or even this morning, it would absolutely send him to therapy.

    “Uh huh,” Carol manages. “You know me, all about that morale.”

    And then, because apparently the universe (and Zosia) decides that this humiliation is not enough, Zosia slides her arm around Carol’s shoulder and pulls her in, patting her arm with exaggerated affection.

    “Yes, Rick,” she says brightly. “Very grateful to have found a sister in Carol.”

    Carol stares straight ahead, willing lightning to strike her on the spot. She could kill Zosia right now. But just as quickly, she’s thankful that this is Rick, oblivious as the day is long and not, you know, Chief Schrader.

    Rick nods, completely satisfied. “Right, well you ladies have a wonderful day. I gotta find my wife now.”

    “Mhm,” Carol hums. “You too.”

    Zosia just nods and he walks off.

    They stand there until Rick’s hideous pink shirt disappears into the crowd, until the thought of being sisters trails away with him. Then they both exhale, long and hard, and start laughing.

    “Christ, that was too close,” Carol says, hands on her face. She turns and presses her forehead into Zosia’s shoulder, who still has her arm around her. “And fucking sisters? Really?”

    Zosia snorts, arm tightening around her and pulling her closer. “Well, we are fucking, but we are definitely not sisters,” she says against Carol’s ear.

    Carol groans. “Please, I don’t want to hear the word sister out of your mouth ever again.”

    “Not even scissor sister?”

    “Oh my god.”

    Lately, Carol’s house always feels a little too quiet on Sunday nights after having spent a weekend with Zosia. It’s not lonely, per se, just sort of very noticeably without her.

    Carol drops her overnight bag in her bedroom and heads to the bathroom, where she immediately turns the shower on and waits for it to warm up, and once under the steaming hot water, she allows herself to come down from the softness of the last two days.

    The way Zosia is always humming when there’s silence, the way her socked feet had tangled with Carol’s under the coffee table while they built lego, and the way neither of them had been able to keep their hands to themselves while they watched that awful rom-com on Netflix that neither of them had, obviously, been paying attention to.

    Now, out of the shower, wet-haired and dressed in an old t-shirt she had pulled out of her closet, Carol unzips her overnight bag to unpack.

    That’s when she sees them. Two tiny lego flowers - a Cow Parsley and a Lavender. They are tucked into the corner of the bag on top of her sweater and nestled next to her toiletry case, secured so delicately that she wonders when Zosia had the time to do this without her noticing.

    Carol stares at them for a moment, allowing a grin to spread across her face.

    “Wow,” she mumbles to herself. Of course Zosia would.

    Carol carries them downstairs to the kitchen where her basil plant sits on the windowsill above her sink, stubbornly alive despite her inconsistent watering schedule, and next to the real sunflower bouquet Zosia had gotten her, which is still thriving. She presses the little lego stems into the soil surrounding the basil.

    It looks both absolutely ridiculous and perfect at the same time and Carol can’t help but take a photo. The plastic flowers stand proudly beside the basil, bright and eternal, among the real thing.

    She sends the photo to Zosia.

    Carol: You know, I’m not the one who needs the immortal flowers

    The three dots appear immediately, as if Zosia had been waiting.

    Zosia: Maybe not, but I like knowing a small part of me gets to stay with you after you leave

    Carol actually scoffs at the message because it’s so disgustingly sweet that she might get a toothache and for a long moment, she just stares at it, reads it over and over again, while her chest tightens with affection and all the things she’s not ready yet to say or admit.

    Finally, after a very casual amount of time, Carol sends back two red heart emojis.

    Zosia sends back three.
