    Zosia is not sure how it happens, but somehow on Tuesday afternoon, she ends up in the evidence locker with Manousos.

    They ride the elevator downstairs with other people, but she figures he is getting out at the lobby with everyone else. But when the elevator opens and shuts at the ground floor and Manousos is still there, Zosia thinks, well this is going to be interesting.

    She already has her keycard in her hand before they even step out because she’d really, really like to say as little to Janice as possible. And without fail, as soon as Zosia turns the corner, with Manousos several steps behind her, there is Janice - arms crossed with that same infuriating lifeless expression on her face that she wears like a uniform.

    “Look alive, Janice,” Zosia says, holding up her keycard as she approaches.

    Janice’s expression flickers only a hair and it’s simply to narrow her eyes at Zosia, but before Zosia can even react to it, something very strange happens. Janice actually smiles. Not just a polite curve of her mouth, but a whole grin that shows off all thirty-two of her slightly off-white teeth, followed by her cheeks tinting a light rosy colour.

    For a second, Zosia actually thinks she might be getting pranked because Janice blushing at her carries about the same, or worse, odds as Zosia winning the lottery. But quickly, when she hears the scuffle of shoes behind her, she realizes it’s because Janice has laid her eyes on Manousos, who has caught up to her. She laughs internally to herself. Zosia doesn’t even need to scan her keycard because Janice is already eagerly buzzing the gate open without a second thought and with what she can only describe as doe eyes.

    “Hi, Manny,” Janice practically sings, like she’s already forgotten about Zosia’s existence.

    “Hola, Janice,” Manousos smiles at her in a very un-Manousos-like way that almost makes Zosia uneasy. “Thank you,” he sings back, holding the gate open for Zosia.

    Zosia watches the entire exchange in disbelief because she does not know either of the two people who just manifested in front of her eyes, least of all Manousos.

    “She knows you’re gay and married right?” Zosia says quietly to him as they enter the room.

    Manousos just shrugs, back to his regular self and Zosia breathes a sigh of relief at that. “She can think whatever she wants to think as long as she leaves me alone and I don’t actually have to speak to her. You see the way she treats Carol?”

    Zosia actually laughs at that, because yes of course she remembers, and then the door clicks shut behind them.

    It’s silent at first, just the jingling of Manousos’ keys as he opens up filing cabinet after filing cabinet and rifles through them. Zosia stands back for a moment, pretending to sign off on transfer documents, but she’s watching Manousos. She likes him, truly, even if they don’t talk much, or at all, beyond the scope of detective work. They did one time, many weeks ago, have a very dry conversation about Poland’s educational system, but that had been short lived because Carol had entered the room and Zosia had completely redirected her focus.

    Manousos, however, is an extremely intelligent hard and a loyal worker, and more so, he is extremely loyal to Carol. He knows her in a way that perhaps Zosia might not ever, and she is okay with that. He knew Carol at her lowest, ugliest point and still he never wavers in his support for her. He is her biggest cheerleader even if he does not look like it. For that reason alone, Zosia has the utmost respect for him.

    A few minutes pass and it feels like there is an entire dictionary of unspoken words between them and Zosia can no longer hold her tongue.

    “I know Carol told you about us,” she says coolly, not looking up from the papers in front of her.

    “Yes,” Manousos says, very simply, with his back still towards Zosia.

    “And?”

    “And it confirmed what I already knew.”

    Zosia has to laugh at that because even she knew early on that Manousos had been suspicious of them, but then she’s considering, with mild apprehension, how many other people might be suspicious of them as well.

    “I would like to think we are not that blatant,” Zosia says, peaking over a clipboard at Manousos who is still very deep into a filing cabinet.

    “Maybe not to anyone else, but you are to me,” he mutters but his answer is calm yet confident. He pauses and his back straightens. Then he shuts the cabinet and turns around. “I have known Carol a very long time. She is different around you.”

    Zosia sets the clipboard back on the table and looks at Manousos now because this deserves her attention. “Different good, or different bad?”

    “She is happy,” he says plainly, and then takes a deep breath like he isn’t sure how he ended up in such an intimate and honest conversation with his Lieutenant, who also happens to clearly be in love with his best friend. “I have not seen that since before…” his voice trails away, but he is still holding Zosia’s attention. “She has been through a lot,” he tries again. Then a pause. “And she told me you have, too. I’m - “

    Zosia puts a hand up, shaking her head. This is the last place she wants to talk about her dead son. “Don’t.” Then softer, “We don’t have to.”

    Manousos nods his head with understanding and then turns back to a different filing cabinet, unlocking it.

    “I just don’t want to see her hurt again,” he says, pulling a stack of papers and folders out and setting them on the table behind him.

    Zosia feels that in her soul. The whole conversation. The way it settles deep in her bones and softens her from the inside out. She would have never thought that Manousos of all people would leave her a little bit at a loss for words inside the evidence locker. But she understands then why Carol trusts him the way she does. Manousos has the kind of steadiness that does not ask to be admired or thanked. He simply carries things for people without making them feel indebted for it.

    “Carol is very lucky to have someone like you,” Zosia says, absently pulling a box down from the shelf, forgetting what she even came here for in the first place.

    “I know,” Manousos says, in his typical stoic fashion, sorting and gathering the documents. “But I also think she’s lucky to have someone like you.”

    Too casually, Zosia turns around and sets the box back on the shelf. God forbid Manousos see that he’s actually made her smile. Behind her, Zosia listens to him lock up all the cabinets and tuck everything back into place. She hears the door creak as he opens it.

    “Lieutenant,” he says finally, as he’s already halfway out the door. She turns around he looks back at her almost exasperatedly. “Please just - do not fuck with her oat milk.”

    Zosia huffs out a laugh at that and then rolls her eyes. “Goodbye, Detective.”

    With that, the door clicks shut and Zosia is left alone with nothing but the sound of the buzzing electrical panels and the gravity of what it means to be in Carol Sturka’s life.

    What it means to love her.

    It does not scare her.

    The clock on the wall in Carol’s dining room ticks 9:48PM. Case files have been open for nearly an hour on the table, but nothing has moved. Paper sits where they first spread it, untouched for the slow curl of edges under the dim chandelier.

    Zosia hasn’t looked at any of it in minutes, maybe longer. She’s been staring somewhere just past the table, past the walls, as if she’s listening to something only she can hear.

    Carol really, really tries to focus. She reads the same paragraph three times. A witness statement- names that should matter and details that usually fall into place for her like it’s second nature.

    But tonight nothing sticks. Because Zosia has been a complete shell of herself today and Carol has lost track of how many times she’s asked her if she’s okay just to be supplied with a flimsy “I’m fine” and it’s finally getting under Carol’s skin. She’s even surprised that Zosia had agreed to come over tonight, and not just because of this mood or trance she’s been in, but because they had decided together that sleepovers would only be for weekends in order to sustain a healthy work-life balance.

    They had already broken the rule once. Surprise.

    “Are you sure this timeline makes sense?” Carol asks, just to break the silence. She nudges Zosia’s knee under the table with her own.

    “Mm.” Zosia doesn’t look at her.

No fucking stop sign at an intersection and I - “ she shakes her head, voice fully trembling and a single tear rolls down her cheek and falls onto Carol’s hand. “I hit a pick-up truck. Or the truck hit us. I don’t-“ Her voice breaks completely now. “I don’t even know anymore.”

    *****

     

     

    A week later, they are back in Zosia’s apartment. Their “weekends only” rule has absolutely not held up. Now it’s a Wednesday morning and way too many minutes before the alarm is supposed to go off that the sun has barely made an appearance.

    Today, Carol is awake before Zosia, which almost never happens. Usually Zosia is already halfway through making coffee by the time Carol even manages to drag herself into consciousness. But this morning she is wide awake and the apartment is still. Pale light filters through the blinds that Zosia had finally put up, casting thin grey stripes. And from the bedroom, Carol can only hear the refrigerator humming and the quiet rumbles of traffic just outside the complex.

    Next to her, Zosia is dead asleep, bare shoulders peaking out from under the covers.

    Carefully, Carol slinks out of the bed and into the cold kitchen in the shirt that Zosia had been wearing last night, and opens the fridge with a vague intention of getting water, but then she stops.

    There, on the top shelf, between the eggs and Zosia’s ridiculously expensive butter that she insists tastes different, sits a carton of oat milk. Carol stares at it because it’s, well, oat milk. Not cow, not cream, not even almond. Oat. And not just any oat milk. The exact organic brand that Carol drinks; the one that’s in her fridge at home, and in the fridge at work.

    For a moment, her brain refuses to process it properly. Zosia hates oat milk and not just casually, but vehemently. Every conversation they’ve ever had arguing about the existence of oat milk- and there have been many- flickers through Carol’s mind.

    And now here it is, sitting in Zosia’s fridge like evidence of some sort of psychological break. Carol reaches in and picks up the carton and it’s already open. About a quarter of it is gone, which means that Zosia had bought it days ago, which means this hadn’t been an accidental grocery substitution. This had been intentional. And she’s been drinking it.

    Carol looks towards the bedroom - still asleep. She imagines Zosia standing in Sprouts, alone in the organic aisle, glaring at all the different nut milks with profound resentment before grabbing the oat milk anyways because Carol likes it in her coffee.

    Something gives way in Carol’s chest. It’s nothing dramatic, but instead a pang, a subtle little shift, like a lock clicking open, because this isn’t really about the oat milk.

    It’s about the fact that Zosia has made room for Carol in a place that Carol previously didn’t believe she would ever have the privilege to hold space in. But already there’s a second toothbrush in the holder, Carol’s pants peaking out of Zosia’s laundry hamper, and the throw pillows and blanket that she had purposely gone out to buy that now accent the couch because of that one time Carol had given Zosia grief that she didn’t have any.

    And now the oat milk.

    Carol leans against the counter, still holding the carton, and she can no longer fight the thought that arrives or even wants to.

     

    Yes, I am in love with her.

     

    Her eyes drift back towards the space between the kitchen and the bedroom and after a moment, Zosia appears there, oversized shirt and boxers, hair flattened on one side from sleep, face still soft with it. She squints against the kitchen light and immediately notices the carton in Carol’s hands.

    A look crosses her face, like she’s just been caught red-handed and doesn’t want to hear about it.

    “Don’t start,” she mutters, voice waking from the sleep.

    Carol looks between her and the carton. “You bought oat milk?”

    Zosia yawns and steps forward kissing her on the temple before edging around her and opening a cabinet reaching for mugs.

    “I did.”

    “You hate oat milk.”

    “I do.”

    “Then why is it here?”

    Zosia shrugs with elaborate nonchalance that fools absolutely no one. “You like it.”

    Carol just looks at her.

    Zosia glances back, catching the expression on Carol’s face, and frowns slightly. “Why are you looking at me like that?”

    Carol’s throat tightens unexpectedly. Because I love you, dumbass.

    Zosia walks closer, suspicion growing immediately. “Carol.”

    “You bought oat milk,” Carol says again weakly.

    “Yes, and this appears to be emotionally devastating for you.”

    Carol manages a stifled laugh through the sudden flutter in her stomach and Zosia reaches for the carton, her fingers stilling around Carol’s.

    “Give me that. It’s too early for you to be this weird.”

    But Carol holds onto it, unwilling to let go and she’s looking at Zosia who still has all this sleep in her eyes and yet still has the unwavering patience to entertain some bratty, too early in the morning game of tug-of-war because Carol can’t get over the concept that she bought fucking oat milk for her.

    “I love you, Zosia.”

    The words come out so naturally that it doesn’t even startle her, and for once she doesn’t have to second guess them and it doesn’t make her feel like crying.

    And Zosia’s reaction is actually kind of perfect. It’s not big or cinematic, just a soft exhale, a drop of her shoulders, and a quiet smile that only belongs to Carol, as if she had been waiting for Carol to get there first.

    “Kocham cię, kochanie,” Zosia says, watching the way Carol lights up in recognition of those words from a week ago. Finally, she pulls the carton out of Carol’s hand and sets it on the counter behind her, placing both hands on Carol’s face, and looking straight into her eyes. “I love you too, Carol.”

    In the bedroom, the alarm finally goes off, but there is no urgency to turn it off. Zosia simply leans in and kisses her. Who knew a chilly Wednesday morning would bring this.

    Love - arriving in the form of oat milk.

    “It was my Shrek impression, wasn’t it?”

    “Please shut up.”

    Chapter 13: what happens in vegas...

    Chapter Summary

    Cowboys, pinballs, and the red suit.

    Chapter Notes

    “Come to Vegas with me next weekend.”

    Carol looks up abruptly from the avocado she’s slicing for the turkey sandwiches she’s making herself and Zosia for lunch, knife stilling halfway through the green. Did she hear that right?

    “What?” She says immediately, looking at Zosia who is sitting across from her at the kitchen island, hair fluttering in the afternoon breeze that slips through the open window, wrapped cozy in green cardigan she had affectionately stolen from Carol. From the living room, quiet jazz music plays.

    “Do you want to come to Las Vegas with me next weekend?” Zosia tries again, watching as the confusion takes over Carol’s face.

    “Vegas? Next weekend?”

    Zosia’s expression stays neutral, but there’s a slight tension to her shoulders that Carol is quickly aware of - and the way her fingers have tightened around her mug of tea.

    “I have some loose ends to tie up there,” Zosia says. “And I don’t particularly want to go alone so I thought you might like to come with me.”

    Carol sets the knife onto the cutting board now.

    A trip with Zosia. Their first trip as- as- a couple? Is that what they are now? Is that what happens after you say I love you after sneaking around and sleeping together for months? Couples exist without the I love you’s, she knows that, but they have crossed that threshold. So what are they? Girlfriends? Domestic partners? Lovers? Gross, no. Everything just sort of happened on its own without any conversation, that now Carol is standing in her own kitchen, in an old Snoopy t-shirt, staring at the woman she is madly in love with, who is madly in love with her and wondering what the fuck they are.

    “A trip?” Carol repeats stupidly. “Together?”

    Zosia’s mouth twitches. “Yes, kochanie. That is heavily implied.” Then, a little quieter, “But if you don’t want to - “

    “Yes,” Carol says too fast. She sees the flash of relief cross Zosia’s face before her brain actually catches up with what Zosia had said.

    “Wait,” she says again, skeptical this time. “What kind of loose ends?”

    Zosia looks away then, gaze dropping to her mug. “I still have some things in storage I need to ship here, and…” she pauses for a moment, biting the inside of her cheek, then takes a breath, “there’s a shared bank with Amina that I need to close and we both have to co-sign for it.”

    Carol underestimates how quickly the human stomach can knot. It pulls tightly just beneath her ribs and she all but doubles over.

    “Oh.” It comes out like a pathetic little squeak.

    Zosia exhales slowly through her nose. “Everything happened so fast after the cheating and the breakup,” she says quietly. “I wanted nothing to do with her as soon as it was possible.     “There’s nothing in it,” she continues. “But I can’t keep my name tied to hers anymore. Not after…” she looks at Carol who is looking just a little bit past her, “everything.”

    Carol swallows hard and looks back down at the avocado, though she really isn’t seeing anything now. Just hearing the name. Amina. Suddenly that part of Zosia’s life feels real in a way it hadn’t before. This isn’t just some abstract ex-fiancée Carol can pretend only exists in stories and old photos, but an actual person that Zosia had shared a life with, who she would have to stand next to again and talk to.

    “I’m not upset,” Carol lies and Zosia raises an eyebrow. “Fine. Maybe a little.”

    “Yes,” Zosia answers honestly, but then she stands and slowly rounds the island until she’s right in front of Carol, hands wrapping gently around her wrists. “This isn’t me holding onto something,” she says carefully. “It’s the opposite.
